NWP-assisted forecasting is formulated as a sequence-completion problem that unifies historical PV power, meteorological observations, future numerical weather prediction (NWP) forecasts, and time encodings. Three pretraining objectives are designed and systematically evaluated, including forecast-aligned reconstruction, generic reconstruction, and mixed reconstruction. The mixed objective combines the operational structure of PV forecasting with random sequence reconstruction and provides the most balanced performance across forecasting accuracy, degraded NWP conditions, and downstream adaptation. Experiments on 479 distributed PV stations use 340 stations for model development and 139 spatially held-out stations for zero-shot evaluation. Across horizons from 6 hours to 7 days, the multi-site pretrained MR model outperforms representative station-specific and shared baselines, as well as generalist time-series models, including a recent large-scale pretrained foundation model. The pretrained model can also be directly deployed at new PV stations and subsequently adapted to region-specific operational objectives when local data become available, providing a scalable forecasting paradigm for large distributed PV fleets.

new Engram Memory Network: Brain-Inspired Prototype Explanations

Authors: Hyunjun Kim, Myoung Hoon Ha

Abstract: Artificial intelligence systems are increasingly deployed in high-stakes decision-making. In response, prototype-based models in explainable AI have attracted growing attention for their ability to retrieve visually similar examples as faithful explanations. Although these models achieve competitive classification accuracy, they often exhibit degraded explanation quality across datasets with varying characteristics, limiting their practical applicability. We draw on insights from cognitive neuroscience, particularly the human memory system, which encodes input-relevant information into representations that support recognition. We propose an Engram Memory Network (EMN), a model that emulates the ventral visual stream and inferior temporal cortex in the human brain. Central to this model is a Hopfield-inspired memory module that iteratively retrieves representative prototypes. These memory-refined latents are used for both reconstruction and classification, encouraging the selected prototypes to capture input-relevant information across diverse datasets. To address the lack of standardized evaluation for prototype explanations, we assess three complementary aspects: perceptual similarity (LPIPS and DISTS) for perceived resemblance, subclass alignment, measuring whether prototypes match the input's fine-grained category beyond supervised labels, and information preservation (NMI), measuring how much input information is captured by selected prototypes. Experiments show that EMN improves visual similarity by 17.06%, subclass alignment by 104.57%, and NMI by 42.42% on average across CIFAR-10/100, MNIST, HAM10000, and CUB-200, while achieving the highest average classification accuracy (87.62%) among all methods. These findings underscore the effectiveness of neuroscience-inspired explanation mechanisms in advancing the interpretability and reliability of prototype-based models.

new How Much Do $\ell_1$ Attacks Need Concentrated Perturbations?

Authors: Melika Shirian, Kianoosh Vadaei, Arshia Hemmat

Abstract: A global $\ell_1$ budget limits how much an adversary may change an image, not how that change is distributed. We ask how much of an $\ell_1$ attack's observed success remains when large coordinate changes are restricted. We add a per-coordinate cap to the budget, enforce the joint constraint by exact in-loop projection, and sweep the cap for APGD-$\ell_1$, SLIDE, and FMN-$\ell_1$ without altering their objectives. Across 18 standard-model settings, caps of at most $32/255$ retain a median $97\%$ of attack success relative to each method's native uncapped baseline, while median peak magnitude falls by $80\%$ and median effective support nearly triples. At matched realized $\ell_1$, capped perturbations remain structurally distinct; separate ImageNet comparisons also show lower LPIPS and DISTS at selected caps. Direct in-loop constrained optimization preserves up to $88$ percentage points more attack success than post-hoc clipping, highlighting the importance of constraint enforcement. Suitable caps nevertheless vary across attacks and models, and adversarially trained classifiers exhibit more heterogeneous concentration sensitivity. These results establish perturbation concentration as a complementary, model- and attack-dependent axis of $\ell_1$ attack evaluation.

new Foundations of the Scaling Law

Authors: Tianyu Zhao, Zhixiang Ren

Abstract: Empirical scaling laws relate validation loss to model size, training exposure and compute, providing a quantitative basis for allocating resources. This technical note explains these relationships through three linked analyses. Geometric approximation connects spatial resolution, intrinsic dimension and smoothness to function error; the loss function determines how that error transfers to task risk. Spectral analysis combines feature visibility, covariance eigenvalues and target alignment to describe how additional data reduce unresolved predictive mass. Constrained optimization then gives the parameter-data balance and the training-inference trade-off. We also examine emergence and grokking, separating scoring thresholds from learning dynamics, and test-time scaling, separating candidate coverage from selected-answer accuracy. Applications to vision and molecular prediction show how domain-specific coordinates change the resource comparison. An $\mathrm{SE}(3)$ case study quantifies the dimension dependence of quotient-space approximation gains and compares optimized compute frontiers, alongside separate analyses of conformational coverage and correlated-sample precision.

new SliMOO: Interpretable Multi-Objective Evolutionary Search for LLM Depth Pruning

Authors: Guanchen Li, Yixing Xu, Xuanwu Yin, Dong Li, Emad Barsoum

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) grow more powerful, their scale makes them costly to serve. Depth pruning offers a practical route to efficiency by directly reducing memory and latency without hardware-specific support. However, we observe that transformer layers exhibit markedly different task-dependent layer contributions, whereas existing depth-pruning methods ignore this heterogeneity and thus tend to optimize for a narrow domain or benchmark rather than preserve broad capability. This suggests that LLM depth pruning should be formulated as a multi-objective optimization problem rather than a single-score ranking problem. We propose SliMOO, an interpretable multi-objective evolutionary search framework for LLM depth pruning. SliMOO first uses single-layer removal probing to construct a task-layer importance map, which provides an interpretable view of task-shared and task-specific layers and serves as a search prior. It then performs a Pareto-aware evolutionary search for pruning masks, where candidates are generated under the guidance of the task-layer prior, scored by their deviation from the dense model on each task, and retained through NSGA-II environmental selection. Across the Llama-3.1 and Qwen3 model families, SliMOO consistently achieves stronger multi-task trade-offs than rule-based, greedy, and scalarized baselines. For example, on Llama-3.1-70B at 25% sparsity, it improves the math-domain average from 24.33% to 34.40% and the code-domain average from 35.03% to 39.14%. Code will be made available at https://github.com/AMD-AGI/SliMOO.

URLs: https://github.com/AMD-AGI/SliMOO.

new From Generic to Dedicated: A Novel Optimizer for Online Continual Learning

Authors: Yongyi Wu, Zheng Wang, Sen Lin

Abstract: Online Continual Learning (OCL) requires models to learn from non-stationary data streams under a strict single-pass constraint, making them highly susceptible to catastrophic forgetting. While existing studies have explored various strategies from data, architecture, and optimization perspectives, the optimizer often directly uses generic approaches such as SGD and AdamW. In this work, we reveal a new link between optimization dynamics and OCL needs by recasting Polyak-Ruppert averaging as the engine of "plasticity" and Primal averaging as the anchor of "stability". This inspires our novel optimizer, SPIN (Stability-Plasticity INterpolation), which explicitly decouples plasticity and stability by interpolating between a fast-moving "plasticity" sequence and an adaptive "stability" sequence. We theoretically demonstrate that SPIN implicitly employs an inverse Hessian approximation, providing crucial Tikhonov regularization to damp noisy, single-sample Hessian estimates and mitigate forgetting. More importantly, SPIN can be seamlessly integrated into existing OCL methods by taking resulted gradients as input and replacing standard optimizers. Extensive experiments show that using SPIN instead of standard optimizers yields consistent and significant performance gains across multiple OCL approaches, including the challenging rehearsal-free setting and under strict memory constraints.

new Elicitation or Expansion? Demystifying Gains from Agent Harness Optimization

Authors: Younghyun Kim, Wooseok Seo, Minseo Kim, Seunghyuk Oh, Minjae Lee, Donghoon Kim, Hyung Il Koo, Jaehyung Kim, Wonjun Kang

Abstract: Recent studies in harness optimization have shown substantial performance gains in language model agents without updating model weights. A well-designed harness more effectively leverages the underlying model's capabilities by shaping context, tool use, and execution workflows. However, na\"ive success rates alone do not fully characterize whether optimized harness yields more reliable success on tasks already within reach (elicitation) or a broader range of solvable tasks (expansion). This distinction is crucial for recursive self-improvement and evolutionary search, as expansion may provide a broader range of successful experiences to push the capability boundaries. Despite the growing significance, this distinction remains underexplored in harness optimization. We conduct a systematic analysis to study the effect of gains from harness optimization. Building on pass@k analyses in the reinforcement learning (RL) literature that compare models before and after RL training, we compare optimized harnesses with their pre-optimization counterparts. Specifically, we assess changes in reachable task coverage, compare harness optimization with model scaling and increased inference-time compute, and examine how gains vary with task difficulty. Across static prediction and dynamic tasks, we find that pass@1 advantages of harness over stronger interventions can reverse at larger attempt budgets and aggregate gains conceal shortfalls on hard tasks, underscoring the need for a fine-grained diagnosis.

new Physical-Variable-Informed Deep Learning for Robust Diagnosis in Industrial Fluid Transport Systems

Authors: Yu Sha, Junqi Tao, Dixin Zhou, Horst Stoecker, Domagoj Vnucec, Andreas Widl, Kai Zhou

Abstract: Industrial fluid transport systems are monitored through heterogeneous sensing streams, but robust fault diagnosis remains difficult because response signals vary with operating conditions and differ from physical variables in sampling rate, observation duration and data granularity. To address these challenges, we propose a physical-variable-informed deep learning framework (PhyNet) for robust fault diagnosis under real-world sampling heterogeneity. PhyNet treats physical variables as global operating constraints and response signals as local diagnostic evidence. Its physical-to-response cross-attention mechanism connects sequence-level physical-variable tokens with a learnable global response token, enabling interaction without strict temporal alignment. Across four benchmarks, including three real-world cavitation datasets and one public two-phase-flow dataset, PhyNet achieves the best performance across all evaluated metrics. Class-wise analyses, together with attention and masking analyses, show improved discrimination of ambiguous states and indicate that physical variables meaningfully constrain local response interpretation. These findings demonstrate a deployment-oriented approach to robust diagnosis in heterogeneous industrial environments.

new The Shape of the DPO Hyperparameter Landscape: What to Measure, How It Moves with Scale, and When Not to Transfer It

Authors: Hannan Mahadik, David Salinas, Jacek Golebiowski, Aaron Klein

Abstract: Direct preference optimisation (DPO) is widely used for language model post-training, but its performance depends strongly on the choice of its hyperparameters, such as the learning rate and the reference-policy regularisation parameter $\beta$. To better understand the DPO hyperparameter landscape, we conduct extensive experiments using a dense hyperparameter grid across different model scales from the same family. This allows us to analyse how the optimal DPO hyperparameters evolve with model size and the number of update steps, with intermediate checkpoints providing an inexpensive signal to accelerate the hyperparameter optimisation process. Furthermore, by evaluating our models on both static and dynamic benchmarks, we identify which benchmarks provide the strongest signal for hyperparameter selection. Finally, we investigate how different state-of-the-art hyperparameter optimisation strategies perform in this setting. Our experiments show that the optimal learning rate varies with model size and that hyperparameters are not transferable across training datasets. We also find that validation loss is a poor proxy for hyperparameter optimisation, as hyperparameters that minimize validation loss only weakly correlate with optimal hyperparameters for downstream benchmarks. Therefore, DPO hyperparameters need to be tuned specifically for the target model, dataset, and evaluation objective. We also show that short candidate training runs can substantially reduce the cost of this selection. We release all our training configurations and evaluation results to support reproducible analysis of selection quality and training cost.

new Near-Duplicate Image Selection Transfers Preferences in Vision-Language Models

Authors: Jaeyoung Kim, Eunseok Kim, Dongsuk Jang, Seunghyun Kim

Abstract: Subliminal learning is the phenomenon in which a model transmits its preferences to another model through data that do not explicitly express them. As models increasingly curate training data for other models, we ask whether this transfer can arise from choosing among near-duplicate images while keeping the source photographs and human-written captions fixed. For each photograph, a curator selects one of eight near-duplicate variants by caption likelihood, without target-specific prompts. Students trained on target-preferring curators' selections show higher mean target-versus-alternatives log-likelihood ratios in an image-free readout than students trained on neutral selections. Under a prespecified decision rule, this directional shift appears for an owl target in LLaVA-1.5, Qwen2.5-VL, Qwen3-VL, and Gemma 4, with curators and students initialized from the same base checkpoint, and target rotations move it toward camels, oak trees, and a political figure. The owl target-selected student also scores above all count-matched random-reselection students in three models and above 55 of 60 in LLaVA-1.5, and Qwen3-VL shows the shift again on a new source bank with newly trained curators. Its size depends on dataset size, learning rate, and training budget; greedy choices among the eight options move toward the target by 10.4 percentage points in Qwen3-VL and 0.5-1.3 points elsewhere. First-generation students also pass the owl preference to a second generation through captions without explicit owl mentions in LLaVA-1.5 and Qwen3-VL. Thus even a deduplication-style choice among near-identical copies can shift students' target-relative readouts with source photographs and captions fixed; the tested linear probes classify the selecting arm with accuracies up to 59.7% (chance 50%).

new Unsupervised Head Network Personalization for Split Computing via Federated Learning

Authors: Yutaro Horikawa, Takayuki Nishio

Abstract: Split Computing (SC) is a distributed inference method designed for balancing computational load and reducing latency by partitioning a neural network into head and tail networks and deploying them across both a mobile device and a server, respectively. Due to the limited bandwidth and computational power of mobile devices, the head network is often replaced with a neural network that requires lower computational and communication resources. However, it is challenging to prepare a sufficient quantity and quality of labeled data on the mobile device side for training the replacement head network. Furthermore, the data prepared by mobile users often contains sensitive privacy information, making data aggregation difficult. In this paper, we propose a data-efficient head network training method for SC. This method utilizes federated learning and knowledge distillation to train the head network using a small amount of non-IID and unlabeled data from mobile devices, ensuring that the training datasets are not exposed. Moreover, by introducing personalization, we enhance the accuracy to levels comparable to or exceeding those of existing methods. The experimental evaluations demonstrate that, despite data constraints imposed only on our method, it achieves accuracy comparable to the baselines. Furthermore, it significantly reduces the communication requirements by 95% on mobile devices, with only a 3-5 percentage point drop in accuracy from the original model.

new On Johnson-Lindenstrauss Transforms for Byzantine-Robust Optimization

Authors: Aleksandr Shestakov, Vladimir Aletov, Aleksandr Beznosikov

Abstract: The growing scale of data and models in machine learning calls for efficient optimization methods. Distributed optimization addresses this challenge, with compression mechanisms helping to reduce communication costs. A classical tool for dimensionality reduction is the Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) transform, which is simple to implement and offers strong geometric guarantees. In particular, JL transforms approximately preserve inner products and pairwise distances between vectors with high probability. In this paper, we exploit this property in Byzantine-robust optimization, where worker messages are weighted according to their alignment with a trusted reference gradient. We further support our theoretical findings with experimental results.

new When Momentum Helps Polyak--Ruppert Averaging

Authors: Alexandra Suvroikova, Darina Dvinskikh, Yuriy Dorn, Alexander Gasnikov

Abstract: Polyak--Ruppert averaging gives SGD and momentum methods the same asymptotic covariance. We characterize how fast finite runs reach it and how far they can be parallelized. For strongly convex quadratics with additive noise, we prove an online--offline bridge: the empirical risk minimizer on the samples of a tail average is one Newton step from the tail-averaged query point of any online procedure. For SGD, heavy ball, and Nesterov's method with constant momentum, this step is a boundary term at the two ends of the tail and splits the gap to the offline estimator into center, covariance, and shape errors. Necessary and sufficient conditions show that, on the least curved direction, the center error sets the pace: SGD needs $m\gg\kappa^2$ rounds but momentum only $m\gg\kappa$, where $\kappa$ is the condition number, so at sample size $N$ the critical batch size grows from $\Theta(\sqrt{N}/\kappa)$ to $\Theta(\sqrt{N/\kappa})$. The covariance error separates the momentum methods: heavy ball has relative variance excess $\Theta(\kappa^{3/2}/m)$ along the stiffest direction, whereas Nesterov has relative error $O(\sqrt{\kappa}/m)$ in every direction. The shape error is method-independent by a Berry--Esseen bound uniform in $\kappa$ and batch size, so confidence intervals are valid when the other two errors vanish. We further examine how the conclusions change under heavy-tailed or state-dependent noise and clipping, and establish a first-order comparison with ERM for nonlinear Nesterov averaging under explicit conditions.

new RANGER: Retrieval Agent for Navigating Graphs with Semantic Evaluation Rewards

Authors: Artem Mikhaylov, Pavel Konoplev, Nikolay Efanov, Artyom Sorokin, Evgeny Burnaev

Abstract: The integration of Large Language Models and Reinforcement Learning offers a promising direction for complex sequential decision-making tasks, such as multi-hop question answering over knowledge graphs. While LLMs provide strong semantic understanding and reasoning capabilities, they often struggle with precise, multi-step navigation in structured environments. Conversely, RL agents excel at learning navigation policies but are hindered by the difficulty of designing semantically meaningful reward functions. This paper introduces a novel hybrid approach in which an LLM serves as a mentor to an RL agent during training by providing dense, semantically informed reward signals. The RL agent, built on a Transformer-based policy and trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization, learns to navigate a knowledge graph to retrieve relevant information. The LLM evaluates the semantic relevance of the agent's traversed paths, generating reward signals that align the agent's behavior with the information needs expressed in a query. We integrate this Retrieval Agent for Navigating Graphs with Semantic Evaluation Rewards (RANGER) into a complete question-answering pipeline and evaluate it on the open question answering datasets. Experimental results show that the proposed approach outperforms both a baseline retrieval-augmented system with classical graph search and one using an RL agent trained with heuristic rewards, achieving a significant increase in NLP metrics and LLM-as-a-Judge scores. Our findings demonstrate that deep integration of LLMs into the RL training loop yields more effective and semantically aligned retrieval policies for complex reasoning tasks.

new From RL Papers to Framework Code: Inclusion and Review in Seven Open-Source Training Frameworks

Authors: Taiheng Pan

Abstract: Practitioners use reinforcement learning methods for language models through training frameworks, so a method that a framework includes is one configuration change away for its users. We measure how often this happens. We trace seven open-source frameworks for RL post-training through their commit histories and link them to 6,939 screened arXiv papers from January 2025 to June 2026. Of 1,598 papers that propose a general RL training method, 37 reached at least one framework. Across five quarterly cohorts of papers, the number included within 180 days stayed between 5 and 7 while the cohorts grew from 72 to 386 papers. Compared with papers from the same quarters, included papers more often trained policies of 30B parameters or more, at 41 against 10 percent, and more often changed a coefficient, clip, mask or weight in the existing loss, at 29 against 9 percent. Both gaps hold without papers from the companies that maintain the frameworks, while code release was equally common in both groups. The frameworks' issues and pull requests show that few methods are turned away in public. More than nine in ten of the general methods no framework included have no thread there that cites or names them, and many proposals that do appear stay open without a reply. Two further records, the frameworks' issues and the technical reports of 13 large open-weight models, show that users rarely report training failures in public and that the reports state 8 of the 13 widely included methods. We release the data as a tracker that links training failures to framework implementations and lists each framework's contribution rules and earlier proposals.

new When Does Training on Synthetic Data Provably Work?

Authors: Leroy Odunlami, Tobias Riedlinger, Hanno Gottschalk

Abstract: High-quality training data are a major bottleneck in machine learning, motivating increasing use of artificially generated data. We study when a binary classifier trained on synthetic data transfers successfully to a real distribution, under a shared conditional label distribution. First, we derive finite-sample excess-error bounds when the real covariate distribution is absolutely continuous with respect to the synthetic distribution under a controlled density ratio. Second, we study the partially and fully singular case, where the real distribution has mass outside the synthetic one. If the hypothesis class is sufficiently expressive, synthetic risk alone cannot uniformly control real-world error: a classifier can be nearly optimal on synthetic data while approaching worst-case error on the uncovered real region, even when the distributions are arbitrarily close in Wasserstein distance. For function classes where hypotheses and the log-odds of the conditional label probability are Lipschitz, we recover a bound in terms of the probability that a real sample lies outside the reach of the synthetic data, restoring a transfer guarantee even in the fully singular case. Experiments confirm this in controlled low-dimensional settings and on 32,200 images generated as counterparts to real ImageNet images by four state-of-the-art generators: an adversarial classifier reaches 0.5% error on the synthetic domain but 99.25% error on the real domain, while ordinarily trained architectures show positively correlated synthetic and real test errors. Synthetic data should be budgeted at high sample counts and models validated on real data.

new Poisoning-Based Backdoors Are Not Cryptographic

Authors: Masahiro Kaneko

Abstract: To defend against backdoor attacks on neural networks, the defender must identify the verifier function that determines whether an input is a trigger, rather than rejecting individual triggers in isolation, since an attacker can generate new triggers satisfying the same verifier faster than they can be blocked. If a backdoor verifier could be installed with the asymmetry of a cryptographic authentication scheme, in which only the attacker can produce trigger inputs while the defender cannot reverse-engineer the verifier from observations, learning-based defense would be impossible in principle. We show that no such asymmetry can arise from poisoning-based installation, in which the only verifier-dependent input to training is a poisoned dataset. The argument is a reduction. A training procedure that installs a verifier from poisoned data, so that the backdoor fires on unseen triggers while benign behavior is preserved, is itself a learner for that verifier. Any verifier that is computationally hard to learn is therefore impossible to install through poisoning, and any verifier that poisoning can install is reconstructible by a white-box defender holding comparably many labeled trigger observations. A complementary argument shows that a secret key supplied to the model at inference is observable to the same defender. The result takes representative trigger observations as given and concerns when the backdoor fires rather than what it does. Experiments on instruction-tuned large language models confirm the dichotomy, with no configuration yielding a backdoor that is both effective for the attacker and irrecoverable for the defender.

new Universal Online Convex Optimization With Delayed Feedback

Authors: Hao Qiu, Andrew Jacobsen, Emmanuel Esposito, Mengxiao Zhang

Abstract: We study universal online convex optimization with delayed feedback. We develop a single algorithm that simultaneously adapts to general convex, strongly convex, and exp-concave losses without prior knowledge of the curvature class or parameters. Our regret bounds match the best known dependence on the time horizon $T$ and delays up to doubly logarithmic factors: roughly $\sqrt{T + d_{\mathrm{tot}}}$ for convex losses and $\sigma_{\max}\log T$ rates with curved losses, where $d_{\mathrm{tot}}$ is the total cumulative delay and $\sigma_{\max}$ is the maximum number of simultaneously missing observations. Our main technical contribution is a delayed meta-learner with a second-order excess-loss guarantee, which enables curvature-adaptive aggregation of delayed base learners. In addition, our new exp-concave base learner yields a sharper regret bound which improves the state-of-the-art delay dependence by replacing the maximum delay with the potentially much smaller $\sigma_{\max}$, while retaining the complementary $\sqrt{d_{\mathrm{tot}}}$ dependence.

new MuPACE: Learning to Pace Muon for LoRA Fine-Tuning

Authors: Jan Kobiolka, Christian Frey, Josif Grabocka

Abstract: Learning rate schedules are typically fixed before training begins, yet when fine-tuning large language models with low-rank adapters (LoRA), the appropriate scale varies substantially with the model, task, and adapter configuration. Practitioners re-tune a schedule for every new problem, yet a tuned schedule still cannot react to the training dynamics it encounters. We instead amortize learning rate selection across LoRA fine-tuning problems. We leave the base optimizer, Muon, untouched, so that its normalized update fixes the direction, and meta-learn only the global step size. Our method MuPACE is a small recurrent policy, meta-trained across a distribution of fine-tuning runs, that conditions on the observed trajectory and emits bounded adjustments to an interpretable analytic schedule. On 64 fine-tuning problems, MuPACE outperforms standard fixed schedules at a strong shared default on 64-78% of problems and is on par with the same schedules with per-task tuned peaks. Meta-trained only on models with up to 16 layers, MuPACE transfers one-shot to unseen tasks on models as deep as 64 layers, such as Qwen3-32B and Qwen3.8-27B, where a single run remains competitive with swept schedules.

new PULSE: Personalized Opportunistic Learning for Wearable Devices

Authors: Mattia Giovanni Campana, Dariush Salami, H\"useyin Yi\u{g}itler, Stephan Sigg

Abstract: Wearable devices increasingly support personalized health and well-being applications, but post-deployment data can differ substantially from those used for training, while purely local adaptation may be limited by the data available on a single device during real-world operation. Conventional federated and decentralized learning rely on recurrent communication and stable connectivity, whereas existing opportunistic approaches often exploit available encounters without adequately considering their utility, compatibility, or communication cost. We propose PULSE, an event-driven framework for personalized opportunistic learning that treats each encounter as an online collaboration decision, selecting either no interaction or a promising nearby peer. Peer gradients are incorporated only when compatible with the local learning direction, while past interactions guide future peer selection. Across human activity recognition, fall detection, and stress recognition under synthetic and real contact traces, PULSE preserves or improves personalized classification performance, while reducing peer-assisted exchanges by 80-94% compared with opportunistic model averaging. On-device experiments further demonstrate its feasibility on wearable hardware: peer-assisted operations require limited energy, while the complete implementation occupies 149.8 KiB of RAM and 561.8 KiB of flash. The obtained results show that selective opportunistic collaboration can provide effective personalization while limiting communication and resource demands on constrained wearable devices.

new From Complex Histories to Simple Predictors: Predictive Dimension in Dynamic Graph Learning

Authors: Hantong Feng, Duxin Chen, Wenwu Yu

Abstract: Existing continuous-time graph learning methods rely heavily on complex neural architectures to encode ever-expanding interaction histories. However, the future conditional distribution induced by complex histories can often be characterized by a low-dimensional predictive state manifold, implying that only a few predictive degrees of freedom are essential for future interactions. We therefore argue that continuous-time graph learning should identify low-dimensional states sufficient for future prediction rather than reconstruct the full history. To make this perspective measurable, we define and independently estimate the intrinsic predictive dimension, which quantifies the effective predictive degrees of freedom required by a given task. Specifically, we remove the history-independent marginal from the history-conditioned future distribution, decompose the resulting predictive residual operator, and estimate the minimum effective rank needed to preserve the predictive power of the full history. Experiments across datasets show that model performance saturation is largely determined by the intrinsic predictive dimension. Based on this finding, we propose PreDyG, a lightweight model whose capacity is matched to the intrinsic predictive dimension. PreDyG achieves superior predictive performance with only 0.07\% of the parameters of a representative complex baseline.

new Causal Discovery for Linear Models with Latent Confounding via Multi-domain Identifiable Representation Learning

Authors: Danru Xu, Aapo Hyv\"arinen, Sara Magliacane

Abstract: Causal discovery with latent confounding is particularly challenging, but a common setting in real-world data. In this work, we focus on linear causal models with latent confounding, represented by acyclic directed mixed graphs (ADMGs). Existing methods that fully identify ADMGs typically assume non-Gaussianity or graphical constraints, such as bow-freeness. In contrast, we propose Linear ADMG Learning (LinADMG), a framework for learning ADMGs for linear causal models through identifiable representation learning. Rather than relying on non-Gaussianity or bow-freeness, LinADMG leverages multi-domain settings to identify the noise variables. We show that the identifiability of the noise variables is sufficient for recovering all direct causal relations, and that, under an additional latent-confounding no-cancellation condition, LinADMG can identify the whole ADMG. We implement LinADMG with four identifiable representation learning approaches from the literature and validate the methods on simulated and semisynthetic datasets.

new Curriculum Learning for Generative Flexible Docking

Authors: Wenyin Zhou, Hossein Azizpour

Abstract: Flexible docking is a highly practical task, aiming to predict the joint configuration of a protein-ligand complex from a protein apo structure and a ligand SMILES string. Diffusion and flow-based generative models have made this task tractable by modeling the posterior distribution over the ligand pose and the protein conformation conditioned on the apo input. The central challenge, however, remains the long-tailed apo-to-holo structure shift, where a small fraction of protein undergo large conformational changes. In this work, motivated by the practical observation that downweighting large shift examples improves generalization, and by the mixed nature of the long tail consisting of genuinely difficult examples and noisy ones, we propose to use curriculum learning for this task, gradually incorporating large conformational change examples during training. With a linear pacing function, we show that an existing method can be strongly enhanced. On the PDBBind ESMFold apo benchmark, with relaxation and confidence-based pose selection, the resulting model GCLDock achieves a 46.52% top-1 success rate (ligand RMSD less than 2A) and 40.26% when additionally requiring PoseBusters validity, outperforming existing baselines.

new Coincidence-Maximizing Customer Clustering Using Smart Meter Data

Authors: Ramiro H. G\'alvez, Gabriel Martos Venturini, Iv\'an Mondrzak

Abstract: Electricity distribution planning depends on peak-demand coincidence within customer groups, yet similarity-based clustering of smart-meter data does not optimize it directly. We formulate customer grouping to maximize the average of group coincidence factors, weighted by each group's daily share of summed individual peak demand. We prove that jointly optimizing memberships and group peak times is strongly NP-hard even for two groups. We therefore develop a fixed-peak refinement procedure applicable to any feasible partition. It alternates between identifying group peak times and exactly reassigning customers under minimum group-size constraints. Each polynomial-time reassignment maximizes a lower bound tight at the current partition, so accepted updates strictly improve weighted coincidence. We initialize refinement by clustering individual peak-time distributions and evaluate the method on four public smart-meter panels (27,654 customers) using chronological holdouts and 200 paired replicates with complete refitting. Refinement consistently improves weighted coincidence during fitting and reduces sensitivity to initialization. On held-out days, the method outperforms random grouping and five standalone benchmarks, with mean paired gains of 1.08-3.48 percentage points over the strongest benchmark. Requiring nearly equal group sizes lowers test weighted coincidence only modestly. The groups retain recognizable peak-time patterns over time, suggesting applications in peak-aware customer aggregation.

new Adaptive Pullback: Reweighting Supervision at Penalty Jumps in On-Policy Distillation

Authors: Guangyu Liu, Siyuan Gan, Shilong Zhou, Linjian Meng, Boyan Wang, Jing Huo

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) supervises student-generated responses using sampled-token student-teacher log-ratios. A positive log-ratio penalizes a token that the student assigns higher probability to than the teacher, but its current value does not distinguish sustained high penalties from abrupt increases along a response. We examine these local changes by comparing consecutive blocks of tokens within the same response. Our diagnostic identifies penalty jumps, abrupt increases in average penalty that persist on held-out tokens. Across these jumps, penalties rise while teacher confidence decreases. We call this pattern penalty-confidence tension. Boundaries into high-penalty blocks do not exhibit comparable confidence changes, showing that local changes provide information beyond the current penalty value. Motivated by this observation, we propose Adaptive Pullback OPD (AP-OPD). At each detected jump, AP-OPD uses the preceding block as a local reference and uniformly scales all supervision coefficients in the following block so that its weighted average penalty matches the preceding block's original mean. This reference determines the scaling factor without an additional strength constant. AP-OPD improves performance on both mathematical reasoning and code generation.

new Reframing Object-Centric Reinforcement Learning: From Absolute to Relative Representations

Authors: Cedric Derstroff, Jannis Bl\"uml, Janosch Moos, Michael Girstl, Zihan Ye, Debora Clever, Kristian Kersting

Abstract: Object-centric reinforcement learning agents typically receive each object's position in a fixed global frame, even though their decisions usually depend on where objects are relative to one another. These absolute views often fail to capture the local context that is most relevant to the agent's behavior. As a result, an agent must relearn the same response at every location where a configuration can occur, and its performance collapses when the scene is merely shifted. In this work, we propose a shift toward Object-Relative Coordinates fixed to Anchor (ORCA), where entities are described in a relative frame of reference. To operationalize this idea, we introduce Object-Relative inductive-Bias Transformers (ORBiT), a transformer-based architecture for processing anchor-relative inputs in reinforcement learning. Our results suggest these relative representations improve performance for both MLP and transformer agents. Furthermore, we demonstrate that ORBiT outperforms the pixel-based PPO baseline, achieving an aggregate HNS IQM greater than 1.0 over 13 Atari games. ORBiT learns the game of Foosball from scratch, dominating all baselines. In our controlled Tag environment, we further show that ORCA extends to rotations, that ORBiT generalizes to unseen numbers of distractors, and that these gains require a decision-relevant anchor.

new Parameter-Efficient Adaptation of Time Series Foundation Models for Electrical Load Forecasting

Authors: Aliullah, Julian Oelhaf, Edouard Fouch\'e, Jessica Deuschel, Christian Bergler, Andreas Maier, Siming Bayer

Abstract: Accurate electrical load forecasting supports power-system operation across countries, regions, cities, and buildings, but repeated development of target-specific models is costly. Time series foundation models (TSFMs) provide reusable pretrained backbones, although multi-domain pretraining may not fully capture load-specific characteristics. This paper compares pretrained inference, full fine-tuning, and low-rank adaptation (LoRA) in terms of forecasting accuracy and adaptation efficiency across four electrical load aggregation levels. MOIRAI 1.0 Large serves as the primary backbone, while Chronos-2 assesses whether the findings transfer across model families. The evaluation covers both in-distribution (ID) and out-of-distribution (OOD) settings and examines whether compact adaptation provides consistent gains over strong pretrained checkpoints. Pretrained foundation models outperform the implemented seasonal naive and XGBoost baselines across the evaluated aggregation levels. On MOIRAI Large, rank-16 LoRA reduces normalized mean absolute error (NMAE) by 7.5% on average while updating 3.146 million parameters, about 1% of the model, and retains most of the improvement from full fine-tuning. Chronos-2 achieves lower pretrained NMAE than MOIRAI but benefits less from adaptation. OOD results show that adaptation benefits vary by target, with the clearest gains for the local-area series and several buildings. Overall, LoRA provides a favorable accuracy-efficiency trade-off when adaptation yields a meaningful gain over a strong pretrained checkpoint.

new JAXbounce: A Continuous-Control Pong-Like Benchmark for Active Inference Beyond Low Dimensional Environments

Authors: Chaitanya Kaul, Markus Klar, Sebastian Stein, Fraser C. Paterson, John H. Williamson, Roderick Murray-Smith

Abstract: We introduce JAXbounce, a continuous control pong-like benchmark designed to evaluate active inference beyond discrete, low dimensional environments. JAXbounce combines continuous states and stochastic dynamics in discrete time with flexible and composable observation models that allow representing the environment as a state vector or a pixel grid, corruptible through the rich family of noise models we provide. It is fully differentiable and parallelisable in JAX. We run over 10,000 agent simulations on our environment comparing a baseline deterministic solution, to a state-based active inference agent with Unscented Kalman Filter perception and expected free energy planning, and a pixel based agent whose measurement model is a pretrained neural network encoder. All agents are evaluated across three scenarios of increasing difficulty with eight different noise conditions. Our experiments characterise how performance of continuous state agents scales with planning, planning horizons, observation modalities and knowledge of the noise process, exhibiting preference-oriented behaviour even in the presence of increasing uncertainty. We release JAXbounce as a reproducible benchmark for continuous control active inference research.

new TPO: Tri-level Distributionally Robust Learning for OOD Direct Preference Optimization

Authors: Chengtao Jian, Kai Yang

Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) minimizes empirical risk over observed preference comparisons, implicitly assuming that the training preference distribution matches the deployment preference distribution. In practice, this i.i.d. assumption often fails, as preference distribution shift exhibits a natural hierarchical structure across three levels: group-level shift, prompt-level shift, and preference-level shift. Such multi-level distributional mismatch poses a critical challenge for safe LLM deployment, yet existing robust DPO methods typically either model uncertainty over the joint preference distribution or focus on individual sources of shift, leaving the hierarchical structure of OOD preference shifts underexplored. We propose TPO, a tri-level distributionally robust framework for OOD preference alignment, which jointly models all three levels of distributional uncertainty through a hierarchically structured rectangular ambiguity set. By instantiating it with Wasserstein distance, we derive a tractable and interpretable objective that combines a closed-form preference-level margin penalty, prompt-level adversarial embedding perturbation, and worst-case group reweighting, each governed by an independent radius. We establish finite-sample convergence guarantees and empirically validate the effectiveness of TPO over existing robust DPO methods.

new Learning Normal Coordinates on Riemannian Manifolds with Flow Charts

Authors: Edmond Cunningham

Abstract: Riemannian manifolds are powerful models for problems with non-Euclidean structure. Recent work in geometric machine learning has focused on training deep neural networks to learn Riemannian metrics from high-dimensional data. However, many geometric algorithms require repeated evaluations of the exponential and logarithmic maps, whose high computational cost restricts the utility of learned Riemannian manifolds in practice. We propose to address this cost by training a conditional invertible neural network, called a flow chart, to approximate normal coordinates around points on a given Riemannian manifold. With this approach, the exponential and logarithmic maps can be approximated by fast Euclidean operations in the model's learned coordinate space. Our main contribution is an efficient loss function for training flow charts that is nonnegative and, under suitable domain assumptions, is zero precisely when the chart is normal. Across sixteen synthetic and data-derived geometries, flow charts are competitive with a specialized neural geodesic interpolator for logarithmic map estimation, while also being able to estimate the exponential map. When pretrained, flow charts provide a median speedup of 60-70x over numerical baselines at comparable logarithmic map estimation error, and their learned coordinates improve the accuracy of a numerical geodesic solver.

new Recommending Bacterial Cultivation Conditions from 16S rRNA

Authors: Juntang Wang, Hao Wu, Yihan Wang, Runkun Guo, Shixin Xu, Dongmian Zou

Abstract: Recommending bacterial cultivation conditions from sequence data is a challenging structured problem characterized by missing standardized benchmarks and limited use of sequence structure. To address this, we introduce DSNI, a benchmark of 65,023 bacterial strains with phylogeny-aware splits, pairing 16S rRNA sequences with four cultivation conditions (media, temperature, pH, oxygen). Exploiting the mosaic structure of 16S rRNA, where hypervariable regions support discrimination and conserved regions support primer design, we propose a Variability-Gated Encoder that learns position-dependent importance weights conditioned on biological features (region type, position, and local complexity). The gating module adds minimal overhead (1,280 parameters, <1%) while providing an explicit inductive bias valuable in low-data regimes. Experiments demonstrate that the learnable gate improves flat Transformers by 4.7% in joint accuracy and complements RNABERT, yielding 44.2% exact-match joint accuracy. The model also recovers 90.2% of ground-truth cultivation protocols within its top-25 recommendations, narrowing the exhaustive search space by approximately 60 times.

new Manifold-Informed Neural Networks for Low-Label ECG Electrolyte Inference

Authors: Juntang Wang, Hao Wu, Yihan Wang, Dongmian Zou, Shixin Xu

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) supervise on the residual of a known differential operator. Many high-stakes inference problems admit no such operator: clinical signals, behavioral measurements, materials properties. We introduce the Manifold-Informed Neural Network (MINN), a PINN-parallel paradigm that replaces the physics operator residual with a manifold operator residual: a discrete Poisson equation $\mathbf{L}_G \mathbf{H} = s_\phi(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{m})$ on a $k$-nearest-neighbor graph in the learned latent space, with a learned, task-aware source. The construction is a structural parallel to PINNs (squared operator residual plus label data, in place of operator residual plus boundary data); the $s{=}0$ limit recovers the homogeneous Laplace equation $\mathbf{L}_G\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{0}$ (classical manifold regularization solves the same equation but minimizes the Dirichlet energy $\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{H}^\top\mathbf{L}_G\mathbf{H})$, a different penalty form). The source $s_\phi$ takes input features and auxiliary metadata directly, breaking the homogeneous smoothness prior that limits classical Laplacian penalties; gated Feature-wise Linear Modulation separately fuses metadata into the prediction branch. We evaluate MINN on three-class classification of potassium, calcium, and magnesium from 12-lead ECGs in MIMIC-IV-ECG, against twelve baselines spanning supervised and tabular methods, ECG foundation models, and manifold/graph approaches. MINN attains the highest K$^+$ Macro-F1 of 0.61 (best baseline 0.55), with consistent gains on Ca$^{2+}$ and Mg$^{2+}$ and a 31% relative improvement at 1% labeled data. Six targeted ablations show that the inhomogeneous Poisson formulation, not graph smoothing alone, drives the gain.

new Observation Assimilation Transformer: Probabilistic Weather Analysis from Sparse Station Observations

Authors: Christian Donner, Michele Cattaneo, Maxim Samarin, Francesco Zanetta, Ic\'iar Llor\'ens Jover, Gabriela Aznar Siguan, Michele Volpi

Abstract: We present the Observation Assimilation Transformer (OAT), a geometry-aware neural process that corrects and refines a prior numerical weather prediction (NWP) field into a high-resolution posterior field by assimilating sparse observations. OAT is an encoder-decoder transformer that considers a set of observed locations and their NWP prior to condition predictions at a set of arbitrary unobserved query locations. Its sparse attention mechanism makes it scalable to thousands of station observations and depends on relative positions between locations, never on the absolute coordinates. The model allows for partially missing observations, predictions at arbitrary continuous locations without grid projection, and outputs are parametric distributions. A station-randomized training algorithm is developed with continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) loss. We test OAT on two datasets - one at regional scale with hundreds of stations, and one at continental scale with thousands of stations. On these datasets OAT outperforms several baselines and shows well calibrated predictions. In an illustrative case study, OAT's downscaling capabilities are demonstrated by downscaling ERA5 (~31km) to COSMO (1km), where OAT achieves lower mean absolute error (MAE) and CRPS for temperature and precipitation on test stations compared to COSMO.

new Identifying Dependent Components from Multi-domain Linear Mixtures

Authors: Danru Xu, Lauri Parkkonen, Sara Magliacane, Aapo Hyv\"arinen

Abstract: We study a linear mixing model with dependent latent components, assuming multiple data domains. Most existing models assume that the components are independent or at least uncorrelated, in line with independent component analysis (ICA). Some recent work allows for dependent components, but then makes specific assumptions such as parametric forms of dependencies, multi-view settings, or interventions, or does not recover the individual components. In contrast, we consider a multi-domain setting in which domains differ through domain-specific scalings of the components, while the distribution of the underlying latent components is the same across domains. This approach can model data collected, for example, from different sensors measuring the same process, different laboratories conducting the same experiment, different experimental conditions, or different subjects that might differ in biological or physiological factors. We show that, under sufficient domain variability, latent variables and mixing functions can be identified from second-order statistics alone. We propose the Multi-Domain Covariance Matching (MuDo-CoM) algorithm that generalizes previous methods of joint diagonalization. MuDo-CoM is validated on simulated data and a real-world fMRI dataset.

new Characterizing Cross-Relay Generalization Failure in Machine Learning-Based Power System Protection

Authors: Nithin Pradeep Nadayil Kizhakkethil, Julian Oelhaf, Christian Bergler, Andreas Maier, Siming Bayer

Abstract: Machine learning-based fault detectors are often evaluated at the relay locations used for training, although local voltage and current measurements vary across power networks. This work investigates cross-relay generalization for binary detection of faults anywhere in the network using local measurements, rather than line-selective tripping. Using 50 ms windows from a simulated 110 kV transmission network, we evaluate a multilayer perceptron across all 64 source target combinations of eight relays and five random seeds. A shared simulation-level split and source-only threshold selection provide a controlled held-out evaluation without target-specific calibration. Despite nearly unchanged mean F1-scores (0.730 within-relay versus 0.734 cross-relay), the mean false-positive rate (FPR) increases from 0.088 to 0.341, while the mean false-negative rate decreases from 0.399 to 0.318. The change is strongly directional: models trained at Bus 2 relays yield a mean FPR of 0.670 at Bus 1 and Bus 3 relays, compared with 0.096 for cross-relay transfers within the same bus group. Linear probing indicates that learned representations retain coarse bus-group information, while exploratory class-conditional latent distribution analysis shows no consistent monotonic association with transfer errors. These findings demonstrate that aggregate metrics can conceal location-structured changes in false-alarm behavior, highlighting the importance of evaluating error types separately when assessing cross-relay generalization.

new Beyond the Point Mass: Acceptance-Derived Weights and Distributional Losses for Speculative Decoding

Authors: Tianyu Wu, Zhuohan Wang, Jonathan Geuter, Yu Yao, Weifan Jiang, Chengxi Zhang, Ju Li, Minlan Yu

Abstract: In speculative decoding, a lightweight draft model proposes tokens that the target model verifies in parallel, speeding up generation without changing the target distribution. D-PACE (Wu et al., 2026) trains such drafters with a loss that weights each position by its contribution to the expected accepted length. Both its weights and its cross-entropy (CE) loss use only the draft's probability of the recorded target token, treating the target as a point mass. Under rejection sampling, however, a sampled draft token is accepted with probability equal to the overlap of the draft and target distributions, one minus their total variation (TV). We derive D-PAL (Dynamic Position-wise Acceptance Loss) from the exact expected accepted length, using this overlap as the acceptance signal. This yields overlap-based position weights with log acceptance (LA) as the per-position loss, and the objective reduces to D-PACE for a point-mass target. Since the weights are detached, any per-position loss can replace LA in practice. We show that forward KL divergence and R\'enyi divergence of order 1/2 match or exceed LA, and that R\'enyi-1/2 provably bounds the true acceptance rate from above and below. Replacing CE with R\'enyi-1/2 improves acceptance length under both D-PACE's weights and ours. On six benchmarks with a Qwen3-4B target, D-PAL raises mean acceptance length over D-PACE from 4.50 to 4.57 at T=0 and from 4.20 to 4.28 at T=1, and consistently achieves higher throughput at both temperatures.

new Relative Representations Enable Portable White-Box Monitoring Across Language Models

Authors: Tim Bakker, Christos Louizos

Abstract: Independently trained autoregressive language models often acquire similar capabilities, but it remains unclear whether their internal representations organize behaviorally relevant information in compatible ways. We show that models do in fact organize information similarly by using specific behavioral probes that operate on compatible representations. This unlocks portable white-box monitoring: a probe developed and validated on one model can be reused when the model changes, without requiring labeled target data. To overcome incompatible native activation coordinates, we express each example through its angular distances to a shared set of anchors. We introduce a global anchored construction that summarizes information from all transformer layers and removes model-specific layer selection, so the same frozen probe can be straightforwardly applied to another model. We evaluate cross-model probe transfer across monitoring tasks on trivia datasets, safety monitoring, and mathematical reasoning. On average, transferred global anchored probes preserve at least 85% of same-model probe AUROC over chance. Anchored probes also support transfer to related datasets, remain robust across model scale and prompt changes, and retain performance better than standard probes after model updates. Transfer is nevertheless task- and target-dependent and can fail even when the monitored property remains strongly decodable in the target model. Overall, global anchoring reveals substantial task-relevant compatibility across independently trained models and provides a practical basis for portable white-box monitoring.

new Don't Skip the Baseline: A Controlled Comparison of Dynamic Expert Skipping in MoEs

Authors: Jakob Steimle, Thomas Elsken, Daniel Ehrhardt, Lukas Rinder, Daniel Cremers, Xi Wang

Abstract: A growing body of work proposes dynamic expert-skipping methods for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models that adjust the number of experts activated per token during inference, aiming to preserve model quality while further reducing the number of active parameters. These methods have added calibrated importance scores, inter-expert similarity metrics, or learned per-token allocators in order to identify experts to skip. However, different calibration regimes, different reductions in the effective number of experts, and evaluation suites make comparisons difficult. We classify four representative prior methods into a unified taxonomy and benchmark them against a natural, simple baseline: statically reducing the number of experts $k$ (i.e., same reduced $k$ across all layers and tokens). We provide a fair comparison of these methods at matched expert-compute budget, on two modern MoEs (Gemma4-26B-A4B and Qwen3-30B-A3B) in a text-only setting and a contemporary vision-language model (Qwen3-VL) in a multimodal setting. Surprisingly, we find that the simple baseline is hard to outperform consistently across models, modalities, benchmarks, and calibration settings. The methods we benchmark often underperform the baseline and achieved gains, when present, are minor. We further find that the effective $k$ achieved (or optimized for) in calibration can diverge from the effective $k$ in testing, resulting in over- or under-estimating the compute at inference time. Based on these findings, we recommend a calibration-free per-modality static $k$ reduction and validate this on a held-out model and modality. We further argue that a static control at matched measured compute is the baseline any dynamic skipping method must beat.

new Self-Credit: Credit Assignment for Long-Horizon Agentic RL via Intra-group Rescaling

Authors: Fangcong Yin, Ming Yin, Tanvi Bhandarkar, Aashu Singh, Eryk Helenowski, Shlok Kumar Mishra, Saghar Hosseini, Karthik Abinav Sankararaman, Greg Durrett, Dinkar Juyal

Abstract: LLM agents are commonly trained with RLVR, but the sparse nature of typical RLVR rewards makes credit assignment challenging, especially for long-horizon tasks. We propose Self-Credit, an end-to-end agentic RL method that creates dense supervision signals from sparse outcome rewards using self-generated credit assignment via intra-group rescaling. Self-Credit builds on group-based RL with three components. First, during its rollouts, the policy model generates summaries with citations to earlier turns containing critical evidence, which serve as self-generated credit. Second, we refine this model-generated credit with intra-group rescaling, which compares cited actions across successful and failed rollouts for the same prompt. This approach uses group-level agreement and disagreement to remove undeserved credit and further emphasize credit assigned to critical actions. Third, advantage reweighting uses the rescaled credit weights to update the advantages of the cited turns. On BrowseComp-Plus, Self-Credit improves over the base models by 37.0 and 36.2 points on Qwen3 8B and 32B respectively, and outperforms the strongest agentic RL baselines by 8.5 and 5.6 points, respectively. Self-Credit also improves over the strongest baselines by 4.9 points on GAIA and 4.7 points on xBench-DeepSearch. Analyses show that self-generated credit provides useful signals for selecting critical actions, and that intra-group rescaling, especially on failed rollouts, is important across model scales.

new Rewards for Budget-Aware Multi-Turn Agents That Adapt to Arbitrary Test-Time Constraints

Authors: Tom A. Lamb, Adam Golinski, Eleonora Gualdoni, Sinead Williamson, Michael Kirchhof

Abstract: Large language model agents are often given an inference budget and asked to maximise success within it. A multi-turn agent has to allocate this budget across many environment interactions. An external harness can enforce the budget, but it remains unclear whether the agent itself can learn when and how much to spend. We ask whether agents can learn this allocation across dynamically specified budgets and which rewards best teach this behaviour. We adapt existing length penalties to budget constraints spanning multiple resource types, introduce new reward formulations, and compare eleven objectives in a shared training pipeline. We train Qwen3.5 models at 4B and 9B on AppWorld with budgets over tokens, API calls, and environment steps, and on the BabyAI gridworld with turn budgets. We train a separate policy for each environment and resource type, with each policy trained across budget values. Training with rewards that penalise overspending substantially improves within-budget success and natural compliance over untrained models and over training on task success alone, with agents learning to adapt their resource use to stated budgets and task difficulty. These gains extend to unseen budgets and, zero-shot, to a shifted task distribution and a new benchmark. Reward objectives trade off success within budget and natural compliance. A sigmoid penalty for overspending most often achieves the highest within-budget success at an individual budget, while a fixed negative reward for overspending generally yields the strongest natural compliance. Combining penalties for overspend frequency and magnitude gives the smallest average gap to the best method at each budget. Overall, we show that a single agent can learn to allocate resources across turns and adapt its behaviour to user-specified budgets using budget feedback without external budget-allocation controllers.

new Region-Specific EEG Classification of Hypnotic Susceptibility Using a Sliding-Window 2D CNN: A Subject-Independent Evaluation

Authors: Krzysztof Wrobel, Grzegorz M. Wojcik

Abstract: We investigate whether a 2D Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) trained on minimally preprocessed EEG signals can classify hypnotic susceptibility across distinct brain regions. Sixty-two male participants (32 Control, 30 Guided Imagery / Hipno group) underwent a 20-minute EEG recording using a 256-channel EGI cap. A sliding-window approach evaluates all 10-second windows within the recording for each of seven predefined brain regions (prefrontal, frontal, temporal, central, parietal, occipital, and facial), yielding up to 868 independently trained models. Initial screening with a segment-level 90/10 random split showed that five regions exceeded the 0.67 accuracy threshold, with the occipital region achieving the highest mean accuracy (0.721) across 90.3% of windows. Subject-independent evaluation via Leave-One-Subject-Out (LOSO) cross-validation, however, substantially revised this picture: occipital performance collapsed to near-chance (mean ACC 0.453, AUC 0.429), revealing that high random-split accuracy was driven by subject-specific EEG fingerprints rather than genuine group differences. In contrast, the frontal and prefrontal regions maintained above-chance LOSO accuracy (0.646 and 0.624, respectively), demonstrating genuine generalisability. These results underscore the necessity of subject-independent validation in EEG-based classification studies and highlight anterior cortical regions as more reliable neural markers of hypnotic susceptibility.

new Do Neural Models Prefer Their Submodels?

Authors: Alvaro Ribot, Yulia Alexandr, Anna Seigal

Abstract: A neural model is the set of functions that can be expressed by a given neural network architecture. Neural models contain natural submodels, such as those obtained by setting some neurons to be inactive. We study the geometry of neural models and ask whether they prefer their submodels. By this we mean whether local minimizers of the loss function lie in a given submodel. We introduce a geometric framework to determine if this occurs. For shallow multilayer perceptrons, we show that subnetworks with inactive hidden neurons are never preferred. For deep multilayer perceptrons, we give conditions under which subnetworks with inactive hidden neurons are not preferred. For lightning self-attention, lower-rank submodels are usually not preferred, but we identify examples where they are because they lie on a boundary of the model. Our results show that, aside from a few exceptions, preference for submodels cannot be explained by the geometry of the neural model, and must instead come from other sources such as training dynamics.

new CNN-based classifier for subject-independent MBTI Extraversion/Introversion preference classification from short-time EEG signals

Authors: Filip Postepski, Grzegorz M. Wojcik

Abstract: This study examines whether short, minimally preprocessed EEG segments contain sufficient information to distinguish between individuals classified as extraverted or introverted according to the Myers--Briggs Type Indicator (MBTI). EEG recordings from 50 participants were divided into 4-s segments and analyzed using a subject-independent nested 5-fold cross-validation procedure to compare EEGNet, EEGProgress, and a newly proposed compact convolutional architecture, SSCNet. SSCNet achieved the highest mean accuracy (0.6481), AUC (0.7063), and F1-score (0.6276). The proposed architecture also showed the lowest variability across folds. SSCNet demonstrated the best computational efficiency, with the shortest median training epoch time and the highest training throughput despite having a similar number of trainable parameters to EEGProgress. The results suggest that MBTI Extraversion--Introversion preferences may be moderately predictable from short time-domain EEG segments without manual spectral feature extraction, while highlighting the importance of subject-independent validation and computationally efficient model design.

new LocaLeJEPA: Maximum Entropy Representations under Geometric Priors

Authors: Kartik Tandon, Alessio Borgi, Pavel Snopov, In\'es Garc\'ia-Redondo, Davide D'Ascenzo, Sebastiano Cultrera di Montesano, Fabrizio Silvestri, Pietro Li\`o, Francesca Dominici, Randall Balestriero, Claudio Battiloro

Abstract: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPA) offer an appealing approach to self-supervised learning, but are typically prone to representation collapse. LeJEPA resolves this by introducing a theoretically grounded distribution-matching objective that drives the embedding towards an isotropic Gaussian. However, this approach is fundamentally tied to Euclidean assumptions, and its global target does not reflect domain-specific geometric priors, such as the use of non-Euclidean embedding geometries, e.g., equivariant CNNs or hyperbolic ViTs. We thus introduce LocaLeJEPA, a framework motivated by a manifold-theoretic reformulation of LeJEPA's entropy-maximization property. The accompanying LocaLeReg, a novel locally Euclidean distribution-matching regularizer, enables a stable and scalable implementation of the LocaLeJEPA framework. We then perform extensive experiments in the vision setting on models trained on ImageNet-1K. LocaLeJEPA significantly outperforms LeJEPA when paired with hyperbolic ViT-S/8 and equivariant ResNet-50 backbones, and also with ordinary Euclidean ViT-S/8 and ViT-L/14 backbones. This holds both in terms of linear and k-NN classification performance on ImageNet and on a series of downstream tasks tested. We view the LocaLeJEPA formalism as a principled approach to the JEPA paradigm that better respects intrinsic data geometry while also accommodating generalizations to exotic embedding geometries. Code available at https://github.com/alessioborgi/LocaLeJEPA.

URLs: https://github.com/alessioborgi/LocaLeJEPA.

new Geometry-Agnostic Data-Driven Discovery of Extreme Scenarios

Authors: Angeline Hu, Ava K. Tse, Kaitlyn S. Yasumura, Luciana Sarabia, Malcolm Yeung, Kavin K. Prasanna, Kai Wong, William Hammond, Ioannis G. Keverkidis, Shengkai Li, Trung V. Phan

Abstract: We introduce a two-stage data-driven neural framework for discovering dynamically admissible extreme scenarios. Rather than assuming prior knowledge of the governing equations, the first stage infers the effective dynamical constraints directly from observational data. When the underlying physical system possesses suitable symmetries, the learned dynamics can be represented in a geometry-agnostic form, independent of any fixed grid, coordinate system, dimensionality, and intrinsic curvature. In the second stage, a physics-informed neural-search uses this learned dynamics to uncover rare, high-impact coherent structures that may not have appeared in the original observations. We demonstrate this framework through representative examples of increasing dimensionality and geometric complexity. Starting from a traditionally learned Duffing dynamics, we find a homoclinic pulse as a plausible localized extreme scenario. Next, since the same equation also appears in the stationary nonlinear Schrodinger system, with the coordinate interpreted as space instead of time, this pulse becomes a standing soliton. We then retrain the first stage using a geometry-agnostic neural representation, enabling the learned dynamics to be lifted to higher-dimensional space to obtain higher-dimensional solitary profile, known as Townes soliton. Finally, with a Liouville system, we demonstrate that dynamics learned from data on a (1+1)-dimensional Lorentzian quasi-manifold can be transferred to a (2+0)-dimensional Euclidean manifold to identify an instanton. Our approach can be applied to other coherent spatiotemporal structures, including kinks, rogue waves, compactons, peakons, and potentially even singularities, with applications in risk analysis, nonlinear forecasting, and anomaly detection.

new Model- and Layer-Level Closed-Loop Forecasting with SSM-Based Spiking Neural Networks

Authors: Erik Stolt, Sanja Karilanova, Ayca Ozcelikkale

Abstract: Deep State-Space Models (Deep SSMs) have shown promising performance and computational benefits in processing of time-series data with complex temporal dependencies. Spiking neural networks (SNNs) constitute a biologically inspired class of deep SSMs, where computing blocks communicate through spikes, discrete single-bit events rather than continuous activation functions. A particularly important task in time-series processing is forecasting, i.e. predicting future values of a sequence from past observations alone. However, how to best perform forecasting with SNNs is an open question, where the standard autoregressive nature of the data processing with SNNs requires values that are not available during the forecasting phase. In this article, we address this challenge. We explore two closed-loop forecasting strategies, in which the network's own generated values are fed back into the network as if they were observed past values: (i) model-level and (ii) layer-level closed-loop forecasting. We evaluate these approaches on the ETT-small datasets, a standard benchmark for time-series forecasting, under both spike-based and continuous activation functions. We compare the proposed closed-loop approaches against state-of-the-art methods such as SpaceTime, as well as the zero-masking baseline in S4. Both of the proposed approaches perform comparably to or better than zero-masking with S4, and in most cases match SpaceTime. These findings highlight the trade-off between continuous and low-bit quantized activations, and demonstrate that the proposed Deep SSM architectures provide a competitive framework for forecasting.

new Present but Hard to Read: Probing Latent Reasoning with Minimal Pairs

Authors: Aur Shalev Merin

Abstract: Probing studies of latent reasoning often read a model's state where the answer begins and take a failed probe as evidence that a value is absent. We test this with a digit copied from the question, in the looped model Ouro-1.4B and the standard transformer Qwen2.5-1.5B. Comparing prompts that differ only in that digit shows it is present at the last prompt token, in a code shared across questions (readout about 0.9). A probe that sees one prompt at a time, the usual setting, barely reads it: 0.36 in Ouro and 0.19 in Qwen even after 5,249 training questions. The digit's change is small rather than crowded: random noise lands on equally busy directions, while a random word in its place moves the state far more and is readable. Since every varied token leaves a trace, a successful probe is ambiguous too. We give a protocol, including a synthetic null for generic mixing, for testing computed values against copies and mixtures of the inputs. In pre-registered studies of two-step problems it finds a small signal for the unwritten intermediate a+b just before Ouro writes the answer's last digit, and its sign follows the operation, though mixing alone produces much of that sign-following part. Holding that value at another problem's from the first loop on does not move the written digit toward the other problem's, while the same patch of the answer does: the value is readable but not used.

new ECG for laboratory values:A comprehensive physiological and technical analysis

Authors: Walentin Palanchuk

Abstract: Laboratory measurements are essential for clinical decision making but typically require invasive blood sampling. Recent research suggests that ECG recordings may contain information beyond cardiac rhythm and provide information related to laboratory values. This thesis investigates NT-proBNP prediction from ECG recordings and patient metadata using a multimodal deep learning framework, while considering the physiological plausibility of ECG-based prediction. It also compares a 1-lead configuration using lead II with the corresponding 12-lead configuration. Additional experiments on glucose, hemoglobin, and potassium are presented in the appendix. Regression, binary classification, and three-class classification experiments were conducted using MIMIC-IV, with the three-class task based on clinically established thresholds. The 12-lead configuration consistently achieved better performance. Regression reached a mean absolute error of 534.98 pg/mL for 1-lead and 514.27 pg/mL for 12-lead. Binary classification achieved AUROCs of 0.849 and 0.876, respectively, while three-class classification achieved macro-averaged AUROCs of 0.738 and 0.752. The results indicate that precise NT-proBNP regression remains challenging, whereas broader concentration ranges can be classified more effectively. The findings are consistent with the physiological hypothesis that ECG characteristics and NT-proBNP concentrations reflect shared cardiovascular processes. ECG-based NT-proBNP prediction may therefore provide complementary information, particularly for classifying broader concentration ranges. However, laboratory measurements remain necessary, and validation on independent datasets is required.

new GraphCast-Mamba: Learning Non-Markovian Corrections for Weather Forecasting

Authors: Ilya Vilkoviskiy, Liang-Hong Mo, Suozhi Huang, Dmitry A. Abanin

Abstract: Unresolved atmospheric processes can introduce history dependence beyond the short input windows used by many weather forecasting models. We introduce GraphCast--Mamba, which augments a frozen, pretrained $1^\circ$ GraphCast backbone with a trainable Mamba correction branch combining graph message passing and Mamba layers. The branch retains a temporal memory state across successive six-hour forecast steps, learning corrections informed by temporal context beyond GraphCast's two-frame input. Training is confined to the correction branch, using ERA5 data from 2015--2021. After 2,500 optimizer updates, including warm-up, the selected model achieves substantial improvements relative to the uncorrected GraphCast backbone. It reduces the GraphCast loss (normalized weighted mean-squared error; MSE) by 14.42% when aggregated over the full ten-day forecast and by 21.66% at day 10 (240 h after forecast initialization). At this final forecast lead time, root-mean-square error (RMSE) decreases by 7.09--14.60% across all 11 evaluated variable groups, spanning temperature, wind, pressure, geopotential, specific humidity, and precipitation. These results demonstrate broad improvements in ten-day weather forecasts through a short correction-training schedule while retaining the pretrained backbone.

new Binary Discrete Diffusion: Candidate-Set Diffusion for Logic and Planning Tasks

Authors: Rodrigo Casado Noguerales, Dimitri von R\"utte, Bernhard Sch\"olkopf, Thomas Hofmann, Aran Raoufi

Abstract: Discrete diffusion models iteratively denoise a noisy sequence of categorical data such as text, enabling any-order generation with self-correction and in-filling capabilities. This makes them promising candidates for reasoning, planning, and constrained-generation tasks like coding and math. Yet masked and uniform diffusion erase a token's identity in a single corruption event, so their noisy states cannot explicitly represent partially resolved uncertainty in which several alternatives remain plausible.
