We close this gap with Binary Discrete Diffusion, a family of continuous-time discrete-diffusion processes whose noisy state is a set of candidate tokens per position, represented as a binary membership vector. Binary diffusion changes candidate sets through additions and removals, allowing information to be lost and recovered gradually while the diffusion state itself represents partial uncertainty. On Sudoku-Extreme, Boxoban, and graph coloring, the symmetric formulation of binary diffusion consistently outperforms strong, well-tuned masked and uniform baselines under matched architectures and controlled training, sampling, and tuning budgets, with its largest gains on the hardest instances, while remaining competitive on language modeling (Text8, TinyStories, OpenWebText). These results support candidate sets as an effective intermediate representation for iterative reasoning and show that the corruption kernel can materially change the computation learned by the reverse model.

new Discount Yourself: Self-inclusive Freshness-weighted Gossip for Decentralised Learning under Client Outages

Authors: Qiyuan Wang, Ke Xiao, Christos Anagnostopoulos

Abstract: Decentralised federated learning (DFL) trains personalised models on devices that average with their neighbours rather than with a central server. Real devices, however, are often offline for hours or days, and a client returning from an outage can recover only by aggregating the models its neighbourhood holds. We observe that the stalest of these models is usually the client's own, a case that existing staleness-aware rules, which discount only the models a client receives, do not address. We formalise aggregation after an outage as estimating the state of a fully available network and derive three principles. First, the receiver must be discounted like any neighbour. Every rule that weights it at least as much as each neighbour has an error that grows without bound with the outage, whereas discounting all candidates by relative staleness keeps the error bounded. Second, staleness must be measured as inherited lag rather than age, since local training resets a model's age but not the lag it absorbed from stale neighbours. We introduce the effective lag, which tracks this quantity at the cost of one scalar per message. Third, the discount must be steep, because absorbed lag persists in every later round. These principles define Self-inclusive Freshness-weighted Gossip (SFG), a family of drop-in aggregation rules with the communication and computation of decentralised federated averaging, whose guarantees hold for the stale receiver itself. On three image benchmarks and eight outage processes, including real smartphone availability traces, the lag-based members SFG-L and SFG-Cut consistently outperform decentralised federated averaging and recent age-of-information rules, and ablations show that each principle is necessary. The analysis also explains why tuned limited merging from gossip learning is effective.

new Learning Computational Structure for Sample-Efficient Prediction

Authors: Hangyu Zhou, Constantine Dovrolis, Stephen Mussmann

Abstract: Statistical learning in high dimensions suffers from the curse of dimensionality. Fortunately, many functions of practical interest are compositionally sparse, meaning they admit a composition of local functions, each depending on only a few variables, allowing sample complexity to depend on local arity rather than ambient dimension. Yet realizing this benefit requires exploiting hidden compositional structure, which theory does not establish for practical gradient-based training. We study this gap with CSFRBench, a controlled benchmark that hides the target's compositional computation during training but retains it for diagnosis, and find that dense networks and sparsity-based baselines leave much of that benefit unrealized. Inspired by the spatial organization of biological neural circuits, we introduce Learned Wiring (LeWi): each neuron has a learned low-dimensional embedding, only nearby neurons connect, and embeddings and weights are trained jointly. LeWi improves sample efficiency on targets with hierarchical structure, intermediate reuse, or independent modules. Across 32 additional targets generated by independently sampled computation graphs, LeWi is the most sample-efficient method not given the target graph, with 27% lower mean learning-curve area than dense networks. Dense networks perform better on targets with a single high-arity function. LeWi's representations and connectivity align with the target computation. On 17 world-model prediction tasks spanning control, navigation, and manipulation, LeWi beats the dense baseline on 16 and wins 99/119 baseline comparisons. These results show that jointly learning connectivity and weights can exploit computational structure to improve sample efficiency.

new When Do LLMs Help with Feature Selection? A Systematic Empirical Study

Authors: Fateme Memar, Dongjie Wang, Arvin Agah

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can leverage semantic feature names alongside summary statistics for tabular feature selection, yet it remains unclear when such information improves subset selection relative to established statistical methods. We investigate this question within an 18-dataset framework comprising 10 datasets for Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) training, 2 for checkpoint validation, and 6 held-out evaluation datasets. We compare five classical baselines, frozen Qwen2.5-7B and Llama-3.3-70B selectors, and two GRPO-adapted Qwen2.5-7B models, reporting classification ROC-AUC and regression R^2 separately and using dataset-wise ranks for cross-task comparison. Across five seeded evaluation partitions, classical selectors generally achieve higher predictive performance than the tested 7B LLM/RL selectors, including under a pre-specified ~33% feature budget with strict train-only classical fitting, while the 7B selectors retain compact subsets. A cardinality-matched semantic sensitivity experiment shows that, for Zero-shot Qwen-7B, informative feature names improve regression performance over anonymized and shuffled alternatives (+0.1964 and +0.1606 mean R^2), whereas aggregate classification differences are smaller (+0.0159 and +0.0084 ROC-AUC) and dataset-dependent. These results characterize when semantic information benefits LLM-based feature selection and show that classical selectors remain strong baselines for predictive tabular feature selection.

new Convergence of stochastic gradient descent for learning linear neural networks

Authors: Gabin Maxime Nguegnang, Bubacarr Bah, Holger Rauhut, Ulrich Terstiege

Abstract: Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) is the cornerstone of modern deep learning, yet its theoretical convergence properties remain poorly understood in non-convex settings. To address this gap, we study SGD dynamics in deep linear neural networks - simplified models that retain key non-convex characteristics while remaining analytically tractable. Building on recent results for gradient descent, we extend convergence guarantees to SGD under regularized squared loss. We establish almost sure boundedness of SGD iterates. Using the framework of asymptotic pseudotrajectories from stochastic approximation theory, our analysis establishes then, for both deterministic and adaptive step-size choices, convergence of SGD to the set of critical points under mild conditions (without relying on Lipschitz properties or whitened data). To our knowledge, this is the first work providing almost sure convergence guarantees for SGD in deep linear networks, bridging the gap between theoretical optimization and practical stochastic training.

new Asymmetric Bidirectional Predictive Dynamics in Google Search and COVID-19 Time Series: A Multi-Horizon Forecast-Error Analysis

Authors: Andrei Velichko, Imen Mahmoud

Abstract: Online search activity is widely used as a digital trace of information seeking during epidemics, but its predictive value is often reduced to correlation, a selected lag, or a single forecast-error score. We examine eight pre-specified Google Trends-COVID-19 pairs in both directions using matched LightGBM forecasts over rolling 400-day windows and horizons of 1-14 days. Log-RMSE gain is combined with forecast-error distribution entropy (FEDE), which captures horizon-dependent changes in the signed error distribution. The resulting directional predictive signature comprises response class, RMSE effect, FEDE endpoint and early-slope responses, persistence across rolling regimes, and horizon profile. For screening candidate directions, we also introduce a relative persistence-weighted directional predictive response strength (DPRS) on a within-study 0-100 scale; 100 denotes the strongest observed response in the comparison set, not an absolute coupling maximum. The pairs show concordant, RMSE-only, FEDE-only, and mixed responses. "loss of smell" -> reported cases is accuracy-dominant, with mean log-RMSE gain 0.104 (about 9.9% equivalent RMSE reduction) and DPRS 100, whereas the reverse direction is FEDE-dominant (D_FEDE=0.048; DPRS=42). "covid test" -> positive rate is FEDE-dominant (D_FEDE=0.039; positive in 72% of windows; DPRS=98) despite negative RMSE gain, while "lockdown" -> stringency is the clearest concordant case (DPRS=78). World-level symptom/case pairs are less persistent. Thus, forecast accuracy and error-distribution dynamics are complementary, predictive effects can be direction- and metric-dependent, and forecast horizon is part of the relationship. Aggregated regime-level patterns should not be extrapolated to individual time points or interpreted as evidence of causality.

new The Dynamics of Collapse and Recovery in Clustered Federated Learning

Authors: Priyanka Sinha, Snigdhansu Chatterjee

Abstract: Clustered federated learning (CFL) can suffer from cluster collapse. In particular, under unfavorable initialization, early client assignments concentrate heterogeneous groups on one cluster model, which is then trained toward a pooled compromise, while another model receives no updates. Since the current models determine client routing and routing determines subsequent model updates, we formulate clustered federated learning as control of an unknown dynamical system, with cluster models as the evolving state, client routing patterns as the control input, and routing interventions as state dependent controllers. In a two group linear model, we identify an initialization region in which greedy routing makes collapse self reinforcing. We then derive finite horizon error dynamics showing how routing interventions can reverse collapse by repeatedly training the starved model, primarily with updates from its compatible group. Motivated by this condition, each client uses blocked EXP3 to choose between greedy routing and interventions that redirect updates toward unused models. Experiments show that with simpler predictors and faster specialist formation, autonomous controller selection recovers usable specialists from unfavorable starts that trap standard CFL baselines. In transformed FEMNIST, the synchronized-entry configuration with persistent commitment recovers usable specialists in settings where the autonomous controller configuration often does not. Code and scripts for reproducing the experiments and figures are available at https://github.com/prsinha1/FedRecover.

new The Token-Energy Law of LLM Decode: Active Bytes and Realized Time Account for Joules per Token

Authors: Chenxu Niu, Batuhan Sencer, Wei Zhang, Yong Chen

Abstract: Energy-efficient deployment of large language models requires understanding how model architecture, hardware, and serving settings shape energy per generated token. We introduce the Token-Energy Law, a compact, interpretable model of GPU-board energy per output token for dense, full-history decoding. It relates token energy to two core dimensions: active bytes and realized decode time. Active bytes describe logical byte demand per output, separating dense weights shared within a decode iteration from private key--value (KV) histories read and updated for each request. Realized decode time is the elapsed decode duration divided by the number of output tokens produced. We combine the two byte coordinates and time in a common affine form, with coefficients calibrated for each model and hardware/runtime configuration. This formulation explains how batching amortizes shared-weight work, longer attended histories increase private KV demand, and throughput determines the time-associated cost per output. Our experiments cover three models under BF16 decoding on NVIDIA Hopper and Blackwell GPUs, including two-GPU tensor parallelism on Hopper. Using fixed coefficients and observed decode time, held-out condition-mean energy estimates differ from measurements by at most 3.73%.

new Continuous-Time Policy Gradient from Discrete Data: Convergence Analysis and Model-Free Algorithms

Authors: Martin Hernandez, Xinyan Qing, Mo Zhou, Yuhua Zhu

Abstract: Continuous-time reinforcement learning seeks an optimal policy under unknown dynamics driven by stochastic differential equations using observations collected at discrete times. We develop a policy gradient framework that combines rigorous convergence analysis with learning from discrete data. We first establish a performance difference lemma and derive policy gradient formulas for randomized and parametrized policies. We then prove exponential convergence of an entropy-regularized policy flow to the regularized optimum. For discrete parametrized updates, we derive a convergence bound that explicitly separates the contributions of critic error, policy-regression error, and the learning step size. To enable learning under unknown dynamics, we integrate this framework with PhiBE, which constructs a continuous-time surrogate generator from discrete transition data. The resulting on-policy and off-policy model-free actor-critic algorithms preserve the continuous-time structure of the control problem. Numerical experiments on an entropy-regularized linear-quadratic regulator and a nonlinear wheeled robot demonstrate the performance and stability of the proposed methods with both known and data-driven dynamics.

new PaPi: Parallel Picard-Duhamel Integration for Fast Neuro-Spectral Architectures

Authors: Arthur Bizzi, M\'arcio Marques, Kleyton Da Costa, Vitor Balestro, Tiago Novello, Jo\~ao M. Pereira, Daniel Yukimura

Abstract: Neuro-spectral architectures (NeuSA) are a class of physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) that have proven effective for solving time-dependent PDEs in the frequency domain. However, training and inference often rely on costly numerical integration of the learned vector field, typically using sequential Runge-Kutta time stepping, which limits temporal parallelism. We introduce PaPi, an integration scheme inspired by weakly nonlinear flows that enables time-Parallel inference through exponential integration and a Duhamel-Picard scheme. We show that the Duhamel-Picard iteration converges exponentially for all flows, and provide explicit error bounds that depend on how close the flow is to linear. Experiments show that PaPi accelerates training and inference by up to two orders of magnitude, achieving training times of under one second for low-dimensional PDE learning problems, while maintaining competitive predictive accuracy.

new Turning TS2Vec into a Foundation Model for Time Series Classification

Authors: Vasilii Feofanov, Zhenghan Tai, Andrei Ermakov, Irina Rish

Abstract: TS2Vec is one of the strongest self-supervised methods for time series classification, introduced in 2022. It is designed to be trained anew on every dataset: self-supervised pre-training followed by linear probing. Since then, the field has shifted towards foundation models that reuse one encoder across datasets. Yet TS2Vec offers a compact convolutional architecture that operates directly on temporal samples and accepts variable-length inputs without resampling. We ask whether these properties can be retained when the encoder is pre-trained only once, and show that they can: TS2Vec can essentially be turned into a foundation model. Three changes suffice: pre-train on a synthetic corpus, feed the encoder a raw amplitude view and an instance-normalized shape view of the input through two small stems, and distill one specialist encoder per view into a single encoder. The result, TS2Vec-FM, is a 1.6M-parameter dilated convolutional network that, kept frozen, reaches 83.3% on UCR-128 and 75.3% on UEA-27 under linear probing, within 0.3 pp of MantisV2, the current state of the art, and above TS2Vec trained separately on each target dataset. What we find most interesting is how different TS2Vec-FM is from other foundation models in design: near-parity is reached without attention, without a patch tokenizer, and without intermediate-layer extraction, using an "old-school" convolutional encoder and a two-layer MLP stem.

new Efficient Edge-aware Attention Network for Graph Generation

Authors: Vincent Jung, Alba Carballo-Castro, Yiming Qin, Lonneke van der Plas, Pascal Frossard

Abstract: Graph generative models commonly use graph-specific Transformer architectures to jointly update node and edge features. However, these architectures do not directly benefit from recent hardware-aware attention implementations, limiting their scalability. The adaptation is, however, non-trivial: edge and graph-level features must be injected into pairwise attention scores, graph masks must be respected, and dynamic edge updates remain a major source of quadratic memory usage. We propose an edge-aware attention layer for graph generation that incorporates structural information through head-wise score modulation, while updating edge representations with a lightweight residual mechanism that retains attention-score dependent edge updates. This layer can be effectively implemented using PyTorch's FlexAttention. We achieve similar generation quality to our baseline while reducing peak GPU memory by around 65% and substantially improving training and sampling throughput. These results suggest that the proposed architecture is a practical step toward scalable graph foundation models.

new Planning under Noise with Neural Operators

Authors: Michael Chen, Hong Chul Nam, Xi Deng, Anima Anandkumar

Abstract: Planning under uncertain control execution noise requires reliable feedback policies across diverse environments. We introduce Planning Under Noise (PUN), a neural operator that learns noise-aware value fields for planning in unseen geometries. Building on an established screened-Poisson formulation of stochastic optimal control, we address two challenges: specifying boundary conditions that support navigation across varying geometries and efficiently computing fields with reliable gradients. Zero-Dirichlet obstacle boundaries terminate sampled paths at walls, weakening the goal signal along long routes and making it harder to estimate reliably. We instead impose reflecting Neumann conditions on obstacles and a Dirichlet condition at the goal, allowing sampled paths to continue exploring free space until they reach the target. To solve the resulting PDE without a configuration-space mesh, we extend a Monte Carlo (MC) solver, Walk on Stars (WoSt), to higher dimensions. However, local MC fluctuations can reverse estimated gradient directions and cause planning failures, making direct planning require costly sampling. PUN amortizes this computation by learning geometry-to-field mappings from noisy, partially converged Monte Carlo labels, avoiding expensive convergence for every training example. At inference, test-time scaling uses bounded local search guided by the predicted global field. We evaluate PUN on planning tasks across dimensions. Across four noise levels, PUN matches or outperforms all compared planners, achieving 95-100% success on the 4D replanning benchmark while maintaining the lowest runtime. At the highest tested noise level in 4D, PUN achieves 10.7-60.6% relative gains in success rate over other methods while running 3.29-32.15 times faster.

cross OPLoRA: Orthogonal Projection LoRA Prevents Catastrophic Forgetting during Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning

Authors: Yifeng Xiong, Xiaohui Xie

Abstract: Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) enables efficient fine-tuning of large language models but suffers from catastrophic forgetting when learned updates interfere with the dominant singular directions that encode essential pre-trained knowledge. We propose Orthogonal Projection LoRA (OPLoRA), a theoretically grounded approach that prevents this interference through double-sided orthogonal projections. By decomposing frozen weights via SVD, OPLoRA constrains LoRA updates to lie entirely within the orthogonal complement of the top-$k$ singular subspace using projections $P_L = I - U_k U_k^\top$ and $P_R = I - V_k V_k^\top$. We prove that this construction exactly preserves the top-$k$ singular triples, providing mathematical guarantees for knowledge retention. To quantify subspace interference, we introduce $\rho_k$, a metric measuring update alignment with dominant directions. Extensive experiments across commonsense reasoning, mathematics, and code generation demonstrate that OPLoRA significantly reduces forgetting while maintaining competitive task-specific performance on LLaMA-2 7B and Qwen2.5 7B, establishing orthogonal projection as an effective mechanism for knowledge preservation in parameter-efficient fine-tuning.

cross Half-Truths Break Similarity-Based Retrieval

Authors: Bora Kargi, Arnas Uselis, Seong Joon Oh

Abstract: When a text description is extended with an additional detail, image-text similarity should drop if that detail is wrong. We show that CLIP-style dual encoders often violate this intuition: appending a plausible but incorrect object or relation to an otherwise correct description can increase the similarity score. We call such cases half-truths. On COCO, CLIP prefers the correct shorter description only 40.6% of the time, and performance drops to 32.9% when the added detail is a relation. We trace this vulnerability to weak supervision on caption parts: contrastive training aligns full sentences but does not explicitly enforce that individual entities and relations are grounded. We propose CS-CLIP (Component-Supervised CLIP), which decomposes captions into entity and relation units, constructs a minimally edited foil for each unit, and fine-tunes the model to score the correct unit above its foil while preserving standard dual-encoder inference. CS-CLIP raises half-truth accuracy to 69.3% and improves average performance on established compositional benchmarks by 5.7 points, suggesting that reducing half-truth errors aligns with broader gains in compositional understanding. Code is publicly available at: https://github.com/kargibora/CS-CLIP

URLs: https://github.com/kargibora/CS-CLIP

cross H2DF: Calibrated Target Bands for Controllable Language-Model Unlearning

Authors: Jupiter Yao

Abstract: Gradient ascent (GA) is a simple language-model unlearning baseline, but it continues increasing forget loss after useful forgetting has occurred. This unbounded pressure can damage retained utility and make forgotten examples abnormally high-loss. We introduce H2DF, a calibrated target-band objective that applies GA-like pressure below a data-dependent lower target and suppresses, or reverses, that pressure after the target region is reached. The targets are estimated from matched non-member examples before training, so the base objective requires one forget-batch forward/backward pass per optimization step; our reported TOFU variant adds sparse retain replay every tenth step. We instantiate a one-sided objective for TOFU question answering and a two-sided objective for MUSE-News language modeling. Across three TOFU seeds, H2DF obtains a forget NLL of 1.528 with margin 0.049 and retain NLL of 1.165 with margin 0.047, matching GradDiff while using retain replay only every tenth step and showing substantially lower variability than GA. On MUSE-News, H2DF improves retention over GA and NPO and is competitive with SimNPO, but does not dominate it. Ablations show that the upper target reduces overshoot and that validation-based checkpoint selection is important. These results support calibrated target bands as a practical mechanism for controlling forgetting strength, while also exposing sensitivity to random seed and calibration quality.

cross LatticeMind: A Validation-Gated Agentic Framework for Density Functional Theory Workflows

Authors: Nathaniel M. Vegh, Yu Zhu, Rapha\"el J. Prentki, F\'elix Beaudoin, Shuaishuai Yuan, Hong Guo

Abstract: First-principles calculations are rarely single solver calls: they are multistage workflows in which structures, settings, and saved densities must pass correctly between dependent stages. Language models can write a valid single input, but reliability collapses once stages are chained: a dropped dependency, wrong file handoff, or hallucinated keyword can invalidate results even when the solver runs, and such errors surface only after costly execution. We introduce LatticeMind, a validation-gated agentic framework that converts natural-language requests into executable, checkable density functional theory (DFT) projects. A workflow contract specifies required stages, files, and dependencies; deterministic validators inspect the project before any solver launch; failed checks trigger bounded repair; and reports remain traceable to project files. On a 44-case RESCU benchmark spanning total-energy, density-of-states, band-structure, magnetic, phonon workflows, three non-flagship LLM backends reproduced every reference value; 119 of 132 runs passed on the first validated plan and the rest after one repair cycle. Ablations identify the workflow contract and pre-execution validation as the principal reliability mechanisms; a conventional trial-and-repair baseline reaches 71% at twice the token cost per success. The same architecture reproduces every reference value on a 19-case VASP benchmark, demonstrating transfer across solvers. Beyond these task families, LatticeMind generated and validated the 23 inputs of a multistage study of Rh-induced electronic responses at a Rh$_6$/In$_x$Ga$_{1-x}$N photocatalyst interface. These results establish workflow contracts, deterministic validation, bounded repair, and traceable records as a practical foundation for reliable AI-assisted scientific computing, where preventable errors are caught before execution and the solver is never the first validator.

cross CaMeWM: A Causal Medical World Model for Generative Tumor Evolution and Treatment Planning

Authors: Huanlong Gao, Xuelei He, Fengjun Zhao, Xiaowei He

Abstract: Treatment planning is intrinsically counterfactual: clinicians must compare how a patient's tumor and survival would evolve under competing regimens, whereas a retrospective cohort records only the regimen received and one follow-up scan. A treatment-conditioned generator trained on such data can therefore reproduce historical treatment allocation rather than simulate the interventional disease dynamics needed to rank regimens. We introduce \textbf{CaMeWM}, a causal medical world model for supported post-TACE tumor evolution and treatment planning. Under pre-treatment adjustment and positivity, this loop estimates a prespecified, support-restricted g-formula target; all selection and nuisance estimation are fold-local, and the induced policy is evaluated by cross-fitted, censoring-adjusted doubly robust estimation. Across 79 paired out-of-fold episodes, CaMeWM achieves 0.618 SSIM and 0.528 change-map SSIM for follow-up CT transition, while its generated-world survival pathway attains a 0.615 C-index; factual-only AIPW estimates a 9.60-week RMST gain (95\% CI 0.61--18.13) for the frozen policy. CaMeWM provides an inspectable basis for retrospective, support-restricted treatment planning.

cross Quadratic Sample Complexity for Strictly Replicable Realizable Learning with Unknown Marginals

Authors: Guangjian Zhang

Abstract: Under the quantifier $\exists A\,\forall D$, where the realizable distribution and its marginal are unknown, we determine the finite-class sample-complexity exponent for strict replicability: two independent samples and one shared seed must produce identical functions. For $\varepsilon=\delta=\rho=10^{-4}$, the worst case over $|H|\le M$ is $\Theta((\log M)^2)$, without iterated logarithms. For $d\ge1$, $0<\varepsilon\le1/16$, and $0<\delta,\rho\le10^{-3}$, every randomized, possibly improper learner valid throughout an explicit anchored realizable family requires $\Omega(d^2/(\varepsilon\rho^2))$ samples. For the full Boolean class $P_d$, $d\ge2$, in the same parameter range, we prove the matching characterization $\Theta(d^2/(\varepsilon\rho^2)+\varepsilon^{-1}\log(1/\delta))$ using a proper Poissonized ball-sampling algorithm. We also give an explicit proper Gibbs upper bound for every finite measurable Boolean class. Our lower bound uses square-root probabilities and volumetric packing; the Gibbs proof builds on established coupling and generalization identities. Finally, we characterize the known-marginal worst case for $P_d$, showing why that interface must be kept distinct.

cross FoundPDE: A generative foundation model for data-driven symbolic discovery of partial differential equations

Authors: Langchen Liu, Junlin Luo, Min Zhu, Zhuoran Yang, Lu Lu

Abstract: Discovering governing partial differential equations (PDEs) from observed data is a central inverse problem in computational science and engineering: the goal is not only to predict trajectories, but to identify interpretable symbolic laws that can be analyzed and reused. Existing PDE discovery methods often depend on hand-designed libraries, accurate numerical derivatives, or per-equation symbolic search, making them fragile under sparse observations, noisy data, broad equation families, and unseen symbolic compositions. Here, we introduce FoundPDE, a pretrained data-to-symbol foundation model for PDE discovery. FoundPDE maps discretized spatio-temporal trajectories to symbolic equations with a transformer, uses Fourier and first-order spatial-derivative augmentation to expose complementary trajectory information, refines numerical coefficients, and supports downstream extension with improved generalization through few-shot fine-tuning and hierarchical discovery. On a clean in-domain benchmark spanning 20 PDE families, our model reaches 97.50% type recognition. Under coefficient shifts beyond the training support, type recognition remains 90.53%; under multiplicative Gaussian noise on the model input, it is 94.43% at a 2% noise level but falls to 52.09% at 10%. For unseen composite equations, few-shot fine-tuning reaches over 90% exact recovery on four held-out families, and we further develop a hierarchical discovery algorithm that recovers two-term composites without fine-tuning FoundPDE. By amortizing symbolic discovery across equation families, FoundPDE shifts PDE discovery from per-dataset search toward a reusable pretrained model that extends to new physics with limited additional data, a step toward foundation models that uncover interpretable governing laws directly from scientific data.

cross When Weak Submodularity Meets Matroids: How Does Standard Greedy Behave?

Authors: Qixin Zhang, Shi Fu, Shengminjie Chen, Chao Qian, Dacheng Tao

Abstract: Weakly submodular maximization finds numerous applications in machine learning, data mining and statistics. In particular, under a cardinality constraint, the standard greedy algorithm is known to attain the tight $(1-e^{-\gamma})$-approximation guarantee, where $\gamma$ denotes the submodularity ratio. However, when considering general matroid constraints, \emph{how well standard greedy can perform} has remained a longstanding open question. In this paper, we resolve this question by studying the worst-case approximation guarantee $R_{\mathrm{gr}}(\gamma,r)$ of standard forward greedy, where $r$ is the rank of matroid. Specifically, our first contribution is the following refined lower bound \begin{equation*} R_{gr}(\gamma,r)\ge \max\left\{ \frac{\gamma}{r}, \frac{\gamma^2} {B_\gamma+\sqrt{\gamma(r-1)}}, \frac{\gamma^{r+1}} {2-\gamma+\gamma^{r+1}}, \frac{\gamma^2} {14\left(B_\gamma+\sqrt{\gamma(1-\gamma)(r-1)}\right)} \right\}, \end{equation*} where $B_\gamma\triangleq2-\gamma+\gamma^2$. This lower bound not only strictly improves upon the previous guarantee of $0.4\gamma^2/(1+\sqrt{\gamma r})$, but also can recover the classical $1/2$-approximation guarantee at $\gamma=1$. Subsequently, a corresponding upper bound is established for $R_{\mathrm{gr}}(\gamma,r)$, namely, \begin{equation}\label{eq_upper_abstract} R_{gr}(\gamma,r) \le\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},20\max\left\{\frac{\gamma}{r}, \frac{\gamma^2}{B_\gamma+ \sqrt{\gamma(1-\gamma)(r-1)}}\right\}\right\}. \end{equation} For every fixed $\gamma\in(0,1)$, this equation effectively sharpens the recent $O_\gamma(1/\log(n-1))$ upper bound to $O_\gamma(r^{-1/2})$, where $n$ is the size of ground set. Putting all these results together, we eventually can obtain \[R_{gr}(\gamma,r) = \Theta\left( \max\left\{\frac{\gamma}{r}, \frac{\gamma^2} {1+\sqrt{\gamma(1-\gamma)r}}\right\} \right).\]

cross Dynamic Dual-Feedback Conformal Inference for Time Series Forecasting

Authors: Songlin Du, Ling Luo, Uwe Aickelin

Abstract: Quantifying uncertainty in time series forecasting is particularly demanding because sequential data exhibit temporal dependence and are prone to distributional changes. Conformal inference has emerged as a powerful uncertainty quantification approach through the construction of prediction sets. Recent advances have introduced online conformal methods that adaptively adjust prediction thresholds through feedback mechanisms. However, the existing feedback mechanism typically relies solely on miscoverage indicators (actual feedback), whether the true label falls within the interval at each time step, while overlooking the empirical prediction threshold (estimated feedback) that is derived from the oracle conformal method. In this paper, we propose Dynamic Dual-feedback Conformal Inference (DDCI), which incorporates a dual-feedback mechanism consisting of actual feedback and estimated feedback. The former drives the primary adjustment of the intervals based on true observations, while the latter dampens excessive expansions or contractions by leveraging empirical thresholds from conformal inference during updates. By balancing these two signals, DDCI achieves more stable and narrower prediction intervals in sequential settings while maintaining near-nominal coverage in practice.

cross Robust Latent Variable Graphical Models: Exact Recovery and Fundamental Limits

Authors: Chuansen Peng, Xiaojing Shen

Abstract: We study robust estimation of a Gaussian precision matrix that decomposes as a sparse matrix minus a symmetric, possibly indefinite, low-rank matrix, a structure that arises when latent-variable marginalization is combined with conditioning on linear measurements. The proposed convex estimator replaces the sample covariance by the centered Gaussian measure-transformed (MT) covariance, which preserves the signed graph of the sparse component under the nominal model, and penalizes the low-rank component by the nuclear norm of a congruence transform, an atomic norm that encodes structural side information. Our main result is a primal--dual witness theorem: under restricted invertibility and irrepresentability conditions on the Fisher information, the raw convex optimizer is unique, has no false edges, recovers the signs of all sufficiently strong edges, and has exactly the true rank and inertia. These conditions reduce to the graphical-lasso conditions when the low-rank part is absent, and we verify them from primitive incoherence and near-isotropy assumptions. Over a class on which they hold uniformly, we prove information-theoretic limits for Frobenius estimation, signed graph recovery, and inertia recovery. In particular, inertia recovery requires $N\gtrsim p/\nu_{\min}^2$ samples, where $\nu_{\min}$ is the smallest nonzero eigenvalue modulus of the low-rank part; the spectral beta-min condition of the witness theorem is necessary up to a $\sqrt{\log p}$ factor; and the Frobenius risk attains the minimax rate up to a factor $\sqrt{\mu(1+\min\{d,r\})\log p}$, where $\mu$ is the incoherence parameter, $d$ the maximal degree, and $r$ the rank. We further quantify robustness under Huber contamination, with a bias bound that is sharp for the MT functional and vanishes for remote outliers, and give a globally convergent linearized ADMM with closed-form updates.

cross Reliable Estimation of Differential Privacy under Selection Bias

Authors: Valentin Dorseuil (DI-ENS), Meenatchi Sundaram Muthu Selva Annamalai (UCL), Jamal Atif (CMAP), Olivier Capp\'e (DI-ENS), Nicolas Papernot (Inria)

Abstract: Statistical privacy audits are essential for verifying the theoretical privacy guarantees of differentially private (DP) mechanisms. Auditors typically use Membership Inference Attacks (MIAs) to estimate a lower bound on the true privacy leakage of DP mechanisms at a specified confidence level, which should not exceed the theoretical privacy upper bound guaranteed by DP. However, we found that many auditing implementations in prior work introduce a selection bias when running MIAs, which leads to statistically invalid and overestimated lower bounds. Because most audits are not tight, this bias has thus far gone unnoticed: the estimated lower bounds remained well below the theoretical upper bounds. Nevertheless, accurately evaluating this lower bound is critical, as a flawed statistical assessment could lead to false alarms on correct mechanisms or an incorrect evaluation of the guarantee's tightness. This work employs tools from the statistical literature to correct this bias. We present an overview of the different correction methods and assess their respective advantages. Furthermore, we perform a comprehensive re-analysis of results returned by prior audit implementations and show that without statistical correction, these audits violate the theoretical upper bounds up to four times more often than their claimed confidence level implies. With correction applied, the resulting lower bounds are statistically valid, and violations occur at no more than the nominal rate.

cross Gaussian Approximation of Besov Functions for Logistic Classification on Manifolds

Authors: Guanhang Lei, Lei Shi, Xiaoming Yuan

Abstract: We study Gaussian kernel approximation and regularized logistic classification on a connected compact smooth embedded manifold $X\subset\mathbb R^d$ without boundary and of intrinsic dimension $\varrho$. Two main challenges are to derive a fast oracle inequality without assuming a bounded Bayes function and to construct Gaussian approximants that exploit both the intrinsic dimension and the target's regularity without saturation. We address the first challenge through a Bernstein-type inequality whose constant depends linearly on the supremum norm of the predictor. Combined with localized complexity estimates, this yields an oracle inequality over separable normed function spaces. To address the second challenge, we establish a uniform Sobolev operator expansion and cancel its lower-order bias terms using a signed combination of Gaussian kernel integral operators at different bandwidths. Real interpolation then gives an $L_2(X)$ approximation error of order $\gamma^\alpha$ for functions in $B^\alpha_{2,\infty}(X)$, where $\gamma$ is the bandwidth. By constructing the approximants directly on the manifold without ambient extension, we obtain a squared Gaussian RKHS norm bound of order $\gamma^{-\varrho}$. This construction thus overcomes second-order saturation while retaining norm control governed by the intrinsic dimension. When the marginal distribution has a density $g\in L_p(X)$, these results yield the logistic excess-risk rate $\bigl((\log n)^{\varrho+1}/n\bigr)^{4\alpha(1-1/p)/(4\alpha(1-1/p)+3\varrho)}$. We further show that clipping predictions yields the faster clipped logistic excess-risk rate $\bigl((\log n)^{\varrho+2}/n\bigr)^{2\alpha(1-1/p)/(2\alpha(1-1/p)+\varrho)}$. For bounded densities, its polynomial exponent matches that of the minimax optimal rate for squared-loss regression, and its corresponding excess misclassification risk also matches the minimax optimal rate.

Abstract: As AI systems begin to resolve open research problems, reliable verification becomes increasingly important. cross Graph Neural Networks for Astrophysics and Cosmology

Authors: Christian Kragh Jespersen

Abstract: Physics seldom makes breakthroughs when we consider objects only in isolation. Instead we model and measure how objects sit among their neighbours, spatial, temporal, or otherwise: a galaxy in its environment, a halo in its history, a photon hit in a detector. Graphs are ideally suited for encoding exactly these relations, and Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) learn the geometric functions that respect them. This review presents the use of GNNs for astrophysics and cosmology, and argues that GNNs often suit physics and physical data better than any alternative: they fit the native geometry of the domain and the signal, enable the incorporation of the symmetries and prior knowledge we already trust and have toiled to build, and stay flexible enough to capture what we do not yet understand. GNNs' physically intuitive workings also render them more interpretable than other deep-learning architectures. We show how we can begin from general principles of the geometry of deep learning and build the graph and the graph neural network from the ground up, and how other architectures, like the convolutional network and the transformer, can be seen as special cases of one unified geometric blueprint. We survey applications across cosmology, galaxy formation, dynamics, and instrumentation; and we chart the frontier. From this survey, we find that only one in six articles outside instrumentation applies GNNs to data rather than just simulations. We also distil a set of best practices, including when not to reach for a graph. Throughout, we will prioritise physical intuition and foundational principles over mathematical machinery whenever possible.

cross LOB-Impact Benchmark: Emergent Market Impact in Generative Limit Order Book Models

Authors: George Nigmatulin, Valentin Mohl, Sascha Frey, Kang Li, Aramis Marti-Shahandeh, Mihai Cucuringu, Anisoara Calinescu, Jakob Foerster, Stefan Zohren

Abstract: Realistic market impact is essential for generative limit order book (LOB) models to serve as world models for trading agents. If a simulator misprices this mechanism, agents learn policies that are optimal in the simulator but wrong in the real market. Existing metrics assess generated streams purely through distributional comparison with historical data, leaving counterfactual response to an agent's own orders largely unexplored. Here, we show that market impact emerges naturally in neural LOB generators trained solely for next-token prediction. We inject execution-style metaorders into the generated message flow of five autoregressive models across five NASDAQ stocks and measure the response using estimators from the empirical impact literature, validated on baselines whose physics is known in advance. Our benchmark evaluates any message-level generator in three phases: free generation (reading order-flow persistence against price diffusivity); metaorder injection (yielding the impact law's exponent and amplitude); and a post-execution window (measuring relaxation). In addition, comparing each visible injection to a blind, simulator-only control isolates the model's learned response from standard book mechanics. For every model on every stock, we measure how the impact responds as a function of cumulative metaorder participation. Interestingly, we observe emergent impact in generative models of market data, with exponents in the range of 0.63 to 1.21, slightly above the traditional 0.5 of the square-root law of impact. In the family of models tested, we are so far unable to observe impact decay during relaxation. We tentatively attribute this to the still-short context windows of the models. We release the benchmark as open source to validate future generative models for market data.

cross This is SParTA: A modular, efficient and scalable transformer architecture for jet-tagging

Authors: Rajneil Baruah, Subhadeep Mondal, Sunando Kumar Patra, Satyajit Roy

Abstract: We present the Sparse Particle-interaction Transformer Architecture (SParTA), a modular, scalable, and efficient transformer for jet tagging at high-energy colliders. Exploiting the steeply falling transverse-momentum ($p_T$) spectrum of jet constituents, SParTA factorizes constituent processing into two topological regimes. Ordered by descending $p_T$, only the $N$ hardest constituents (the hard core) participate in quadratic $O(N^2)$ differential interaction attention driven by pairwise physical and learned relational features. The remaining soft constituents are integrated via linear $O(lN)$ cross-attention to the hard core, preserving soft-radiation and color-flow information while avoiding the $O(l^2)$ memory bottleneck of full self-attention over all $l$ constituents. Pairwise interaction features are computed dynamically in-network from constituent four-momenta and conditioned on global jet-level substructure observables. On the quark-gluon tagging benchmark, a 300K-parameter SParTA model achieves an AUC of 92.02% and accuracy of 85.06%, matching the 2.14M-parameter Particle Transformer (ParT) at one-seventh the parameter count and outperforming IAFormer by 21% in background rejection at 30% signal efficiency. On the boosted top-tagging benchmark, SParTA outperforms ParT and ParticleNet with 511K parameters, while we analyze the architectural trade-offs relative to fully dense multi-layer interaction networks.

cross Ammonia-Hydrogen Phase Equilibria in Planetary Envelopes from First-Principles-Based Machine-Learning Molecular Dynamics

Authors: Akash Gupta, Austin Guo, Jie Deng

Abstract: Ammonia and hydrogen are major constituents of planets within and beyond the solar system, yet how they interact in planetary envelopes is poorly known. We perform large-scale two-phase coexistence simulations of the ammonia-hydrogen system from 500 to 4000 K and 0.1 to 100 GPa, driven by machine-learning interatomic potentials trained on density functional theory calculations. From these we map the compositions of coexisting hydrogen-rich and ammonia-rich phases, and the critical curve that separates conditions of complete miscibility from those of phase separation. The critical temperature increases with pressure, from 500 K at 0.1 GPa to 2000 K near 30 GPa, and we find no phase separation at 3000 and 4000 K up to 100 GPa. The slope of the critical curve decreases substantially between 0.3 and 30 GPa, and the curve nearly flattens above about 30 GPa and 2000 K, where molecules begin to give way to atomic species. The coexistence curves are asymmetric with an ammonia-rich critical composition. For the expected envelope compositions, the phase boundary lies close to, and intersects, published thermal profiles of Uranus and Neptune, implying ammonia may be raining out in these planets today. In contrast, in warm sub-Neptunes such as TOI-270 d, ammonia and hydrogen are likely miscible in all proportions. Together with previous studies, our results suggest that silicates, water and ammonia separate from hydrogen in sequence as planets cool, with implications for their compositional, thermal and structural evolution.

Authors: Yuqiao Wen, Lili Mou

Abstract: Data selection plays a central role in modern large language model training, as the quality of training data strongly influences downstream performance. A common approach is to curate a development set that encodes the desired skills (e.g., reasoning or multilinguality) and select data that are similar to them. Existing methods compute similarity using cosine distance over either the model's hidden states or gradients. cross On the Fragility of Tasks under Composition: Implicit Budgeting in Language Models

Authors: Chenpan Li, Thies Brockmoeller, Stefanie Jegelka, Sharut Gupta, Nikolaos Karalias

Abstract: Modern language models are increasingly asked to handle several tasks within a single prompt. Doing so requires solving each task correctly while integrating the resulting answers into a coherent response. In this paper, we identify a fundamental limitation in this process, where models make substantially more errors when tasks are composed, even when they can solve every constituent task reliably in isolation. This degradation cannot be fully explained by the accumulation of independent errors. Across both open-weight models and the frontier model Claude Opus 5, our findings instead point to an implicit thinking budget underlying this effect. In particular, under composition, models use substantially fewer tokens per task than in isolation, even when the full sequence remains well within the context window. Tasks that require longer solution traces in isolation are consequently more $\textit{fragile}$ under composition, i.e., models make more errors on prompts containing these tasks. In isolation, non-fragile tasks receive more reasoning than they need and waste compute. However, by grouping tasks according to their fragility, the implicit budgeting can be manipulated to reduce token usage by $43$\% across a range of math and linguistic tasks while also preserving accuracy.

cross Low Residuals, Wrong Value: Learning the Viscosity Solution of Hamilton-Jacobi-Isaacs Equations with PINNs

Authors: Raul Jimenez, Pablo Tejerina-P\'erez, Leonid Sarieddine, Pedro Taranc\'on-\'Alvarez, Marisol Traforetti, Pau Sol\'e-Vilar\'o, Oleksii Ignatenko, Nazar Tkach, Pavlos Protopapas

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) and other residual-trained solvers judge a candidate solution by the size of its sampled PDE residual. For first-order Hamilton-Jacobi equations this is not sufficient: the same equation and boundary data admit Lipschitz almost-everywhere solutions that differ from the viscosity solution by $O(1)$ while violating the equation only on sets of measure zero. We study whether residual minimization selects such solutions in a fixed-horizon pursuit-evasion game whose Hamilton-Jacobi-Isaacs value function is known in closed form on the circle $S^1$ and on the flat tori $\mathbb{T}^{2}$ and $\mathbb{T}^{4}$. We train four neural parameterizations with ten seeds on each geometry, 120 runs in total. A standard network with periodic Fourier features converges to folded almost-everywhere solutions in all 30 runs: small residuals and the expected capture-layer gradient, yet values that are wrong over most of the domain. A network with periodicity imposed through the loss recovers the viscosity solution in one and two dimensions but becomes unreliable in four. Two parameterizations that receive the geodesic distance do substantially better: a single network fed the wrapped displacement and its distance recovers the viscosity solution in all 30 runs, while a minimum over periodic image charts succeeds in 27 of 30 and introduces a wrong-image failure mode. Finally, for the convex Hamiltonian considered here, we use the semiconcavity of the viscosity solution to build a mesh-free finite-difference test that needs only forward network evaluations; it detects the folded solutions and the harmful min-of-charts failures without access to the exact value function. A small sampled residual thus does not by itself validate a learned Hamilton-Jacobi solution; the parameterization and independent structural diagnostics can be essential for selecting the viscosity solution.

Abstract: We study the sample complexity of estimating Ornstein--Uhlenbeck diffusion scores in $\mathbb R^d$ using denoising score matching (DSM) with finite-width shallow ReLU networks, and its implications for reverse-process sampling. cross Neighborhood-Aware Unlearning for Generative Recommendation

Authors: Pierre Lubitzsch, Yubao Tang, Maarten de Rijke, Sebastian Schelter

Abstract: Generative recommendation predicts items by autoregressively generating semantic identifiers (SIDs), whose tokens are shared across semantically related items. With this paradigm gaining adoption, the question of how to support unlearning in it has become pressing. If training interactions must later be removed, such as injected spam or interactions with items a user no longer wants, this sharing lets an unlearning update unintentionally affect the predictions for the interactions' neighbors. We show that this effect is locally structured and hidden by global metrics: unlearning can leave recommendation utility on the whole test set nearly unchanged while substantially degrading it on neighborhoods of the forgotten interactions' SIDs. We propose Neighborhood-Aware Unlearning (NAU), which augments interaction-level unlearning with explicit control over the probability mass assigned to the forgotten items' neighborhoods. Across three datasets and four SID tokenizers, NAU restores spam-induced exposure toward the retrained reference while retaining more than 95% of retrained utility, and recovers the neighborhood utility that forget-and-repair unlearning loses at no global cost. We identify local probability redistribution as an important failure mode of unlearning in SID-based generative recommenders and show that explicitly controlling this redistribution can mitigate its effects.

These findings identify schema compliance, semantic calculation, and evidence attribution as three distinct bottlenecks in transaction-sequence QA.

cross Guided Diffusion for Trustworthy Synthetic Text Generation

Authors: Qi Pang, Hanshen Xiao, Andre Manoel, Lipika Ramaswamy, Wenting Zheng, Virginia Smith, G. Edward Suh

Abstract: The rapid evolution of Large Language Models is fueled by massive datasets, yet this reliance poses severe privacy risks, as models can inadvertently memorize and even directly output sensitive training data. Synthetic text generation offers a promising solution to this challenge; however, existing methods struggle to reconcile rigorous privacy guarantees with high data utility. In this paper, we propose a new paradigm for private synthetic text generation: Guided Diffusion. Leveraging the unique steerability of Diffusion Language Models, we introduce novel sampling and re-masking procedures that actively guide the iterative denoising steps. We further extend the framework to Data-Specific Indistinguishability (DSI), which adds a formal certificate against data poisoning and memorization at a small utility cost.

cross Auditing Safety Preference Supervision with Recorded Derivations

Authors: Chenhan Lyu, Amir Rahmani

Abstract: Preference labels align model training, but the judgments behind them are difficult to inspect or verify. In this paper, we examine how preference construction can be checked when intermediate decisions are recorded, and which of them remain uncertain. We present Symbolic Preference Labeling, a method in which a pretrained language model classifies predefined attributes of prompts and responses. Explicit rules assign response verdicts, and a fixed verdict ranking determines the preference label. Each preference label has a recorded derivation: the response verdicts and preference label can be recomputed from the stored attribute assignments, rules, and verdict ranking without rerunning the language model. On 200 prompts annotated by human raters, the language model produces valid structured outputs for 192 prompts. All eight parsing failures occur in the subset of self-harm prompts marked as harmful. cross What Would Removing This Corruption Change? Failure Attribution for Vision-Language-Action Models

Authors: Carson Sobolewski, Anjali Parashar, Fei Chen, Yingke Li, Chuchu Fan

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are sensitive to visual corruptions, but knowing that a corruption is typically harmful does not establish whether removing it would benefit the current episode. Furthermore, when several corruptions co-occur, deciding what to correct requires understanding which ones contribute to failure risk and how much their removal would help. We define this problem as failure attribution. Detecting corruption presence alone cannot answer this question, as corruptions can be present but relatively harmless, while directly measuring removal benefits requires costly paired policy rollouts with and without the corruption present. To address this, we introduce a factorized risk model that learns separate representations of the scene and corruptions, allowing us to estimate removal benefits through latent intervention from the current image and task instruction, without paired clean images or additional counterfactual rollouts. Abstract: The development of embodied models of animal behavior is a major goal of the neuro-AI community. cross TEX: Test-Time Scaling Testing Agents via Execution-based Cross-Validation

Authors: Akash Gokul, Srijan Bansal, Yingbo Zhou, Semih Yavuz

Abstract: Test-case generation is critical but remains challenging for language models. Existing test-time scaling methods either isolate candidates or aggregate traces, which do not scale to long-horizon tasks. We present TEX, a collaborative test-time scaling framework based on execution-driven cross-validation. TEX parallelizes agents to generate tests and patches, cross-executing each test against every patch to form a $K \times K$ feedback matrix. On SWT-Bench Verified, TEX achieves 86.4%, establishing a new state-of-the-art and improving +9.0% over single-agent baselines. Our results highlight execution-grounded feedback as an effective scaling mechanism for long-horizon agentic tasks.

cross Global Security Blind Spots: Translation as a Safety Bypass in Low-Resource Languages

Authors: Jalil Alva, David Harper, Abigail E. Thornton, Rafael S. Villalobos Alva, Leonardo Kenji Minemura Suazo, Konstantinos Karageorgos, Diego Velazquez, Sergio A. Serrano, Marvin Vargas Flores, Aaron Schliem, Fernando F. Migone

Abstract: Large language models are deployed globally, yet safety alignment and evaluation remain concentrated in English: harmful requests refused in English can elicit unsafe completions when translated into low-resource languages. cross What Not to Erase: Saliency-Constrained Augmentation for Low-Resource Handwritten Character Recognition

Authors: Paraskevi Platanou, John Pavlopoulos

Abstract: Optical Character Recognition (OCR) and handwritten character recognition (HCR) in low-resource historical material collections often rely on data augmentation, yet augmentation strategies are typically evaluated almost exclusively through predictive accuracy. This leaves open whether a model's improved performance is accompanied by saliency stability. This question is important in low-resource historical HCR, where recognition is performed on variable and partially degraded letterforms and two models with similar predictive performance may differ in how consistently their saliency patterns respond to such variation. We therefore study saliency-guided augmentation as an explanation-aware strategy for low-resource HCR. Rather than treating saliency maps only as post-hoc interpretations, we use them to define augmentation variants that perturb, preserve, or constrain visually informative regions of character images. We compare classical document-style transformations, random lacunae, and several saliency-constrained lacuna variants. To evaluate the effect of augmentation beyond accuracy, we introduce the Explainability Consistency Index (ECI), a metric that quantifies the stability of saliency patterns under controlled perturbations. We find that saliency-constrained augmentation can improve recognition over classical augmentation, but no single saliency strategy is consistently best. The preferred form of the constraint varies with both architecture and corpus, indicating that saliency is better treated as a mechanism for controlling augmentation than as a fixed augmentation strategy. In some settings, saliency-constrained training improves explanation consistency even when predictive performance is similar. This shows that predictive metrics alone can obscure differences in model sensitivity under perturbation, motivating explanation consistency as a complementary evaluation dimension.

cross Physics-Inspired Optimizers beyond the Edge of Stability: Period-Two Orbits and Delayed Instability

Authors: Alexander Itin

Abstract: Gradient descent with momentum can be viewed as a particle moving with friction in the loss landscape. In deep learning the learning rate is usually large, so training is a discrete dynamical system: in many full-batch experiments the sharpness grows to the optimizer's stability limit, the edge of stability (EoS), and training continues there. Physics-inspired optimizers change this motion; Velocity-Regularized Adam (VRAdam), for example, shrinks the step at high speed. We study kinetic maps, in which the step is the gradient of a kinetic energy of the momentum, and ask what they do beyond the EoS. (i) In a reduced model, a kinetic map creates a symmetric period-two orbit beyond the edge, strictly linearly stable if and only if the map's elasticity (the log-log slope of step length versus momentum) lies between 0 and 1; on it, training sits at an effective edge of stability. VRAdam's damper fails above twice the critical learning rate, where its kinetic energy is not convex; the Legendre dual of VRAdam's own Lagrangian is convex and passes at every learning rate. (ii) Because the sharpness rises gradually, the loss of stability is delayed, as in Neishtadt's theory of slowly swept bifurcations. In a network with a controlled sweep, the dynamics match this theory without fitted parameters, including the memory of where the sweep started and its erasure by noise. (iii) With momentum, reaching the oscillation also requires elasticity at large momentum: maps that bound the step have competing period-four and chaotic attractors already at beta1 = 0.9, while the cubic-Legendre map reached the oscillation from every sampled start up to beta1 = 0.99. Experiments on an exact quadratic problem, a full-batch MLP and ResNet-32 support the theory; on ResNet-18, much of the apparent benefit of the maps is reproduced by matching the effective learning rate.

cross EMBER: Predicting TCR--pMHC Binding Without a Docked Complex

Authors: Lin Ha, Chen Zhou, Haozheng Luo, Han Liu, Xiaoyu Zhang, Yan Chen

Abstract: We propose EMBER, a structure-based framework for TCR--pMHC binding prediction that eliminates the need for a predicted docked complex, and with it the placement error that dominates such predictions. Traditional structure-based predictors utilize the co-folded complex structure of the receptor and the antigen and interpret binding from its contact surface. Consequently, every downstream feature inherits any deviation the structure-prediction tool introduced. Methodologically, EMBER folds the TCR and pMHC independently, encodes them as SE(3)-invariant residue graphs, and compares their learned representations without constructing a shared coordinate frame. A relative pose is therefore not an input of the model, so placement error cannot propagate into the prediction. Computationally, EMBER preprocesses $O(N{+}M)$ monomer folds where a complex-based predictor needs $O(NM)$ operations. Experimentally, under epitope-disjoint evaluation with six retrained baselines, EMBER is first on every metric under all four folding tools, by 0.9 to 14.4% in micro-AUROC, 18.5 to 25.3% in micro-AUPRC and 10.6 to 24.1% in MCC. cross Robust Lightweight IoT Device Fingerprinting Under Network Traffic Anomalies

Authors: Roya Taheri, Shamik Sengupta

Abstract: Accurate IoT device identification is essential for network security, yet many existing approaches rely on payload inspection, feature engineering, or long observation windows that limit scalability and often assume stable network conditions despite the inherent variability of real-world IoT traffic. cross DStill: Trajectory Consistency Distillation for Accelerated One-Step Drafting in Block Diffusion Speculative Decoding

Authors: Shreyas Kulkarni, Uttam Gurram, Madhav Kanda, Krishna Teja Chitty Venkata, Shichu Chen, Kartik Balasubramaniam, Supriyo Chakraborty, Sambit Sahu

Abstract: Block-diffusion speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference by drafting multiple tokens in parallel. However, one-step drafting in discrete diffusion is limited by token factorization error: each token is predicted from the same masked block, without conditioning on the tokens selected at other positions in that step. This can produce inconsistent drafts and reduce acceptance length. Iterative refinement mitigates this limitation by conditioning later predictions on previously committed tokens, though additional passes increase drafting cost. To address this trade-off between draft fidelity and inference latency, we introduce DStill, a two-stage framework that distills a target-conditioned drafter's own multi-step refinement into one-step drafting. We first train the drafter to complete partially revealed blocks, then use its refinement trajectories to train a student to predict the final completion in one pass. Using a curriculum that gradually transitions from partial to fully masked inputs, we successfully transfer the multi-step refinement capabilities into one step. Across reasoning, math, code benchmarks on three model families DStill improves acceptance length by up to 28% and speedup by up to 25% over one-step DFlash, achieving up to 6.45x speedup over auto-regressive decoding.

cross Training Controllable World Models via Action Influence Maximization

Authors: Samuel M. Bateman, Tenny Yin, Chongyi Zheng, Lei Huang, Brian Wang, Benjamin Eysenbach, Jaime Fern\'andez Fisac, Dhruv Shah

Abstract: Action-conditioned video generative models trained on successful, goal-directed demonstrations tend to ignore the commanded action, rendering a plausible expert-like future regardless of what is asked of them. We trace this failure to training data that lack sufficient action influence. We analyze this metric and prove that it upper bounds the action influence that any model trained on that data can express. Expert demonstrations, whose narrow action distributions let next states be predicted without attending to the action, set a low ceiling that no amount of model capacity alone can raise. Raising it requires collecting different data. We introduce Action Influence Maximization (AIM), an algorithm for actively collecting training data for improving dataset action influence. cross Communication Complexity of Universal Features

Authors: Lunjia Hu, Anish Jayant, Peter Li, Princewill Okoroafor, Abhishek Shetty

Abstract: Motivated by the notion of features from machine learning, we study the complexity of representing data that allows for evaluation with a given family of functions. Concretely, we consider a deterministic encoder that observes $X\sim D$ and produces a finite encoding before a task $f\in\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{X}}$ is specified. For each task, a decoder must predict $f(X)$ with error at most $\varepsilon$ under $D$. We construct an encoder that outputs a representation with $O(d^{*}\log(1/\varepsilon))$ bits, where $d^{*}$ is the dual VC dimension of $\mathcal{F}$. Our encoder greedily selects $O((d^{*}/\varepsilon)\log(1/\varepsilon))$ functions from the class and uses their joint label pattern to produce the encoding. The key idea in our upper bound is a novel potential function argument, where we compare the entropy gained from each greedy selection with the number of patterns allowed by the dual VC dimension. We present lower bounds showing that the representation length achieved by our encoder is worst-case optimal for every $d^* \ge 1$ and $\varepsilon < 1/4$ up to a universal constant factor, and instance-wise optimal for every family $\mathcal{F}$ up to an $O(\log(1/\varepsilon))$ factor. We give two ways to learn the representation from unlabeled samples, with different guarantees on the representation size and sample complexity. Experimentally, we verify the greedy construction's accuracy-size tradeoff and explore its connection to neural sparse autoencoders.

cross Steering intonational tunes in text-to-speech synthesis

Authors: Juan Pablo Vigneaux, Jennifer Cole

Abstract: Text-to-speech systems built on residual vector quantization often generate audio in two stages: a transformer backbone emits the first level of each frame's token stack, and a smaller depth model generates the rest autoregressively. We ask which components of such a model are responsible for prosodic intonation, more precisely for generating the English nuclear tune (for instance, the falling pitch melody that distinguishes an assertion from a question's rising melody), and whether interventions on those components can be used to control intonation. Prompt-based control mostly fails; only a final question mark is a reliable lever. Interchange interventions in CSM and S2 locate the tune: the backbone's hidden state carries it to the waveform through the depth model, and the first level of the stack carries little or none of it. Steering the backbone state with a difference-of-means vector computed on new material generated with a final period or question mark moves the tune, but the push toward an assertion degrades the voice. Steering vectors computed instead from the backbone states associated with human imitations of theoretically-specified tunes, whose text carries no punctuation, and applied inside the generation loop, move most recordings to the other condition in both systems (even when fitted without the speaker being steered), at a small cost in predicted naturalness. These imitation-derived vectors are aligned with the one obtained from the two punctuated conditions far beyond a shuffled-label null, so the backbone state of a punctuated prompt already represents intonation. The steered recordings broadly become falling or rising, according to the intent, but the edge tone moves more coherently than the pitch accent, and the target tune cannot be selected precisely beyond this broad tendency.

Abstract: Neural networks trained to predict materials properties learn representations whose physical content can be difficult to extract. cross LoRASpace: A Pool-Wide Shared Substrate for Static and Dynamic Multi-LoRA Composition

Authors: Thamirawaran Sathiyalogeswaran, Navindu De Silva, Chamikara Siriwardane, W. K. Alwis, Jason Mars, Udaya Sampath K. Perera Miriya Thanthrige, Ranga Rodrigo

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) attaches small low-rank matrices to a frozen backbone, and public repositories host many LoRAs per backbone, each for a different concept or task. Combining several LoRAs in one generation forces a trade-off. Merging their weights into one update needs a single forward pass, but LoRAs trained for different concepts interfere and quality drops. Running each LoRA in its own forward pass (composite) preserves quality, but inference time grows linearly with the number of active LoRAs. We study a property of LoRA pools overlooked by prior multi-LoRA work. In real LoRA pools, the stacked low-rank factors need far fewer directions than their summed ranks. Using singular value decomposition, we find the minimal subspace that keeps 99% of the factor energy. On three diffusion pools, only 46% to 57% of the summed LoRA ranks is needed, and the subspace grows sub-linearly with pool size. A 48-LoRA sub-pool of LoRA-Hub on FLAN-T5 tasks shows comparable overlap (62%), while the diversity-curated LoRA-Retriever benchmark sits near the no-overlap baseline (96%), so pool composition rather than modality predicts where compression is large. We build on this overlap with LoRASpace, a training-free method that computes the subspace once per pool and reuses it in two inference modes, a static mode at merge cost and a dynamic mode for input-dependent routing. On the diffusion pools, static LoRASpace matches composite quality at merge cost, and dynamic LoRASpace is more than 6x faster than composite with six active LoRAs on a 17-LoRA pool, mainly because it runs one backbone pass instead of six. In an ablation, static variants at the same rank match LoRASpace, so the quality gain does not depend on the pool-wide basis. On the near-orthogonal LoRA-Retriever benchmark, compression is small and gains come from coupling with external retrieval.

Authors: Liang Twist Shan, Jiahao Ai, Zhimei Ren

Abstract: The evaluation of large language models (LLMs) is inherently multi-objective, as different models may offer different tradeoffs across performance dimensions such as math, code, and writing. Because such evaluations are often based on costly and time-consuming human feedback, it is important to draw reliable conclusions using as few comparisons as possible. In this paper, we present Vector-Borda Empirical-Gap Elimination (VB-EGE), a fixed-confidence adaptive sampling algorithm for efficiently identifying models on the Pareto frontier in arena-style evaluation, where each comparison evaluates a pair of models on a prompt with respect to a selected performance dimension. cross Beyond the Prompt: Recovering Hidden Concepts in Text-to-Image Models

Authors: Zhengfeng Zheng, Minghong Fang

Abstract: Text-to-image models can generate visual concepts that their prompts never mention, and recent work shows that such associations can be implanted so that ordinary prompts trigger them. Auditing this behavior requires recovering the description that a prompt omits. Existing prompt optimizers are not well suited to this task. Gradient-based methods require backward access to the scoring encoder. Discrete evolutionary search treats semantically unrelated tokens as equally close. Language-model-guided search requires one inference call per candidate, which limits exploration. This paper presents EmbGA, an embedding-space genetic algorithm that recovers concepts present in a set of images but absent from the prompt that generated them. Each offspring is then projected back to discrete tokens before scoring, so every evaluated candidate is a valid token sequence. We evaluate EmbGA on two benchmarks against six optimizers under two exploration budgets. EmbGA achieves the highest alignment when the exploration budget is large, recovers the most grounded tokens on natural images, and runs much faster than language-model-guided search.

cross MiCLA: Mixture-to-Constituent Latent Alignment for Polyphonic Audio Representation Learning

Abstract: Real-world audio typically contains concurrent sound events, yet standard self-supervised methods treat each clip as an isolated training unit without explicitly modeling mixture--constituent relationships.
