cross Soliton Profiles in 1D: Enhanced Neural-Network Solvers and Constrained Deep Ritz Methods

Authors: Chandler Haight, Alex D Rodriguez, Svetlana Roudenko

Abstract: We develop and compare enhanced neural-network solvers for one-dimensional ground-state profiles, which are solutions of $$ Q^{\prime\prime}=bQ-\gamma Q^{p},\quad Q>0,\quad p>1, \quad b, \gamma >0, $$ the stationary profile equation for the nonlinear Schr\"odinger, generalized Korteweg-de Vries, and nonlinear Klein-Gordon equations. Its explicit sech-type solutions serve as exact reference for error computation and benchmark hyperparameter selection. We improve on the standard Adam-trained physics-informed neural network (PINN) baseline by introducing higher-order optimization via Gauss-Newton/Levenberg-Marquardt and L-BFGS refinements, as well as Fourier-feature PINNs, and constrained Deep Ritz (DR) formulations. In DR approaches we introduce two new neural-network formulations, which implement two classical variational characterizations: a Nehari-constrained Deep Ritz method, in which the height is learned by projecting onto the Nehari/Pokhozhaev manifold, and a Weinstein Deep Ritz method, in which a normalized profile minimizes the Weinstein quotient. The Deep Ritz formulations perform best overall, in particular, the Weinstein variant gives the best error-runtime balance. The neural-network based solvers remain less accurate and efficient than classical Petviashvili iteration in 1D, however, the current study provides a proof of concept: the mesh-free formulation, non-reliance on exact profiles or the related features make them promising in higher dimensions and for more complicated nonlinearities and dispersive operators.

cross What Moved the Alarm Score? Causal Attribution with Exact Computation and Scarce-Incident Estimation

Authors: Masaaki Takada, Ryusei Shingaki

Abstract: Alarms in industrial systems and scientific instruments often demand immediate diagnosis despite scarce incident data. To guide intervention, an explanation must trace how changes in the data-generating process propagate through the causal structure to alter the alarm score, rather than merely flagging shifts that may be downstream effects. Yet exact attribution across interacting causal components appears combinatorial, while existing methods often either assume an unchanged causal model or require enough incident data to estimate the changed system reliably. We address this setting by formulating the alarm-score change as a Shapley game over hybrid structural causal model (SCM) states. Treating structural functions and exogenous laws as distinct players yields a finer-grained, function/law-resolved report of how each type of change propagates to the alarm score. The same framework defines realized-event and population reports, with an event--population coherence identity linking their attributions. For ordered linear SCMs and quadratic scores, a causal first- and second-moment recursion combined with quadrature computes the full Shapley vector exactly in polynomial time, eliminating combinatorial computation. To estimate this target from scarce incident data, we adapt a well-observed reference SCM to the incident regime using cross-fitted estimates of mechanism changes and residual moments, and derive attribution-error bounds and top-k recovery guarantees. Experiments on controlled and measured shifts illustrate the diagnoses and assess scaling to hundreds of variables and estimation from small incident samples.

cross HypoNet Nankai PS: rapid hypocenter determination for the Nankai Trough using physics-informed neural networks with three-dimensional P- and S-wave velocity models

Authors: Ryoichiro Agata, Ayako Nakanishi, Dan Bassett, Satoru Baba, Yasuyuki Nakamura, Gou Fujie

Abstract: Rapid and accurate hypocenter determination based on three-dimensional (3D) seismic velocity models is essential for seismic monitoring in subduction zones, yet it remains computationally demanding. HypoNet Nankai, a hypocenter determination package that uses physics-informed neural network (PINN) travel-time inference for a realistic 3D P-wave velocity model of the Nankai Trough subduction zone, was designed to address this problem, but supported only P-wave arrival times. Here, we present HypoNet Nankai PS, which extends the framework to both P- and S-wave travel times. We first constructed an S-wave velocity model from the existing P-wave model using empirical conversion relations for marine sediments and the crust. Because the resulting S-wave velocity structure has lower velocities and therefore considerably steeper travel-time gradients, the original PINN training procedure designed for the P-wave velocity structure was insufficient. We therefore revised the training procedure by reconsidering the distribution of collocation points, the NN architecture, and the optimizer. Benchmark comparisons with the fast marching method showed that the revised approach substantially reduced the root-mean-square deviations for both P- and S-waves compared with the original algorithm. Recovery tests using synthetic earthquakes were also performed. When applied to 223 earthquakes recorded by DONET stations off the Kii Peninsula, the system yielded hypocenters generally consistent with the JMA catalog beneath the network, suggesting that the 3D velocity model captures travel-time variations accommodated by JMA's 1D model and station corrections. Farther seaward, the estimated hypocenters were substantially shallower than those in the JMA catalog and were located in the oceanic crust and uppermost mantle, consistent with previous relocations that used DONET data and 3D velocity models.

cross Learned Readout Tokens for Extracting Large Language Model Confidence

Authors: Nima Seifi, Martin Ester

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used within everyday applications where their responses have significant downstream impacts. For responsible deployment of such systems, we desire insight into how much we can trust their individual outputs. A popular approach for estimating LLM confidence is to train probes on the hidden states of the LLM's tokens to predict the correctness of the model's output. These probes typically read hidden states from tokens that are already in the model's vocabulary. In this work, we introduce a new approach to confidence probes that learn a new readout token and utilize its hidden states to train the probe. We show that our learned readout approach ScratchProbe outperforms its existing-token counterpart on a variety of datasets and LLMs. We also extend our method to a multi-token approach which we show provides further performance improvements. Finally, since the learned readout changes only the position a probe reads from, any existing probe can adopt it. Applied to a state-of-the-art probe, it improves AUROC on every backbone, in domain and out of domain.

cross Bayesian Neural Networks via Dirichlet Process Priors on the Data-Generating Distribution for Randomized Value Functions in Deep Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Sumit Vashishtha

Abstract: Bayesian neural networks (BNNs) offer a principled framework for quantifying epistemic uncertainty. However, standard weight-space priors yield complex, high-dimensional neural network posteriors that are computationally prohibitive to sample from, and popular approximate inference schemes often fail to capture epistemic uncertainty effectively---a failure mode particularly acute in reinforcement learning (RL) \citep{osband2018randomized}. To resolve this challenge, we introduce a new class of BNNs that quantify uncertainty by placing Bayesian nonparametric Dirichlet Process (DP) priors directly on the underlying data-generating distribution rather than on network parameters. Building on this foundation, we develop DP-DQN, a reinforcement learning algorithm that leverages DP-BNNs to maintain posterior distributions over value functions and perform efficient Thompson sampling. By grounding randomized value functions in Bayesian nonparametrics, DP-DQN unifies data-driven posterior reweighting (via the Bayesian Bootstrap) with generative prior hypotheses injected directly through the DP base measure, enabling decisive ``deep exploration'' with a single neural network without the overhead of multi-network ensembles.

cross When LLMs Manipulate: Measuring Behavioral, Social, and Temporal Effects

Authors: Sadia Asif, Israel Antonio Rosales Laguan, Shumaila Asif, Haris Khan, Muneeb Asif, Muhammad Zeeshan Karamat

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can influence user trust and decision-making through subtle linguistic cues, yet the behavioral effects of manipulative outputs remain poorly quantified. We introduce a behavioral computing framework for measuring \textit{behavioral vulnerability}, defined as users' susceptibility to manipulative AI responses. Building on DarkPatterns-LLM benchmark, we generate controlled manipulative stimuli using Manipulation Gradient Descent (MGD), targeting biases associated with authority, urgency, and social proof. We analyze participant responses using the Multi-Stakeholder Impact Assessment Network (MSIAN) to model cross-user influence and the Temporal Harm Propagation Model (THPM) to characterize the persistence of behavioral effects over time. In a study involving 120 participants, manipulative framing produced significant changes in trust calibration and compliance ($p < 0.01$), with effects also propagating across social contexts. These findings highlight the need to extend LLM safety evaluation beyond model outputs to measurable behavioral consequences and provide a computational framework for quantifying individual, social, and temporal risks arising from manipulative AI interactions.

cross Factual Access States in Diffusion Language Models: From Mechanism to Knowledge Editing and Retrieval

Authors: Yuchen Guo, Zhuoyuan Mao, Yiwen Guo

Abstract: Factual recall is a lookup: some representation inside the network serves as the key, and what that key is made of decides which queries can retrieve the fact, what an edit must cover, and what a retriever can read. We call it the factual access state. In autoregressive (AR) models the subject position enriches attributes largely independently of the relation, and relation-specific selection happens later, at the readout position. In masked diffusion language models (DLMs) we find the state is assembled differently, and much earlier: in the first attention layer the wording of the relation is routed into the position of whichever entity is visible and the early feed-forward layers look the fact up on this fused representation. The state therefore varies with the relation's surface form and with the query direction, so the queries about one fact occupy a region of state space rather than a point, and an operation on the fact succeeds only for queries that enter it. We treat the state as an interface to a DLM's factual knowledge, and put it to two uses. Writing to the state gives a knowledge editor: from one declarative statement, Multi-View Edit fits one shared value per direction across that region and installs it in the early-MLP block, preserving source-prompt reliability while substantially improving paraphrase and reverse question answering, where AR editors gain near zero. Reading it suggests a complementary use: the state at each entity position can serve as a relation-specific retrieval vector with no training, returning both pieces of evidence for two-entity queries on which pooled, prompted and lexical retrievers almost never return both.

cross Sensitivity-Orthogonal Residual Learning for Adaptive Sideslip and Cornering Stiffness Estimation

Authors: Kyoungtae Ji, Dongyeol Won, Kyoungseok Han

Abstract: Online adaptation of the cornering stiffness keeps a model-based sideslip estimator valid as tires change, but competes with learned model correction for the same filter innovation. A corrector with enough parameters can absorb the innovation pattern that a stiffness change produces, and adaptation is suppressed while the fit improves. We propagate the stiffness sensitivities of the predicted measurements through the vehicle states and construct a corrector orthogonal to them. A null-space parameterization enforces the constraint without a penalty coefficient and preserves the static least-squares stiffness estimate on each identification segment. In full-vehicle simulation, the stiffness response of the unconstrained corrector declines as its parameter count grows, whereas the orthogonal corrector responds at least as fully as the uncorrected filter at all sizes and lowers sideslip error by 12.7% to 22.0%. In a simulated drive with a stiffness step, the orthogonal corrector adapts as quickly as the uncorrected filter and lowers sideslip error during and after the transient. On public real-vehicle data, the 430-parameter unconstrained corrector loses most of its response to deliberate offsets of the filter's nominal stiffness, whereas the orthogonal corrector of the same size retains most of it, with a sideslip error slightly above the uncorrected filter's.

cross Ask to Learn: Query-Based Collaborative Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Kaicheng Zhang, Hongqian Niu, Laixi Shi, Didong Li, Guannan Qu, Zhun Deng

Abstract: Offline reinforcement learning is fundamentally limited by the coverage of the available data, while collecting additional trajectories through online exploration may be costly, unsafe, or infeasible. We study whether this limitation can be overcome without directly interacting with the underlying environment. Motivated by practical settings such as collaborator-mediated simulation, where a collaborator can provide information complementary to the offline data, we introduce query-based collaborative reinforcement learning to study how to acquire this information efficiently. The learner starts with a fixed offline dataset and adaptively queries state-time pairs; the collaborator samples an action according to a fixed, possibly suboptimal policy, and the simulator returns the corresponding reward and next state. For finite-horizon tabular problems, we derive an instance-dependent information-theoretic lower bound on the additional queries required to identify an $\epsilon$-optimal policy and develop a local quadratic approximation. Guided by this characterization, we develop an adaptive query-allocation algorithm whose query complexity nearly matches the local benchmark under suitable conditions. Experiments on multiple synthetic and semi-synthetic environments demonstrate the empirical efficiency of the proposed method.

cross A Radiation-Hardened, Fault-Aware Quantized Neural Network That Self-Heals

Authors: Peter Xiangyuan Ma, Joshua S. Bloom

Abstract: Deep learning (DL) models are increasingly being deployed into radiation-challenging environments in spacecraft, particle accelerators, and, soon, space-based data centers. Instantiated as a learned orchestration of numbers stored in persistent memory, models are susceptible to continual degradation as high-energy particles corrupt data. At inference time, new data passed through the network fundamentally break the off-line assumptions and guarantees of a static model determined during training. To preserve the quality and efficacy of the models, fault tolerance against bit flips is a clear objective. In this work we developed a runtime method for repairing the quantized weights of neural networks (NNs) after bit flips on commodity, commercial off-the-shelf (COTS) hardware, without storing extra replicas. Our work extends the well-established Fault-Aware Training (FAT) paradigm by adding an alignment penalty to concentrate undamaged activations and weights into a low-rank subspace, and we then reconstruct flagged corrupted coordinates from the uncorrupted survivors. We call this method {\it Projection Healing} and apply it to six regression benchmarks on quantized NNs. Holding a FAT checkpoint fixed and toggling only the runtime heal gives a further ${\sim}23\%$ reduction in the native-unit excess integral. Across those datasets the method extends the operational scrub interval by a median of $2.04\times$ at a $+5\%$ RMSE degradation and $1.96\times$ at $+50\%$, compared with $1.62\times$ and $1.07\times$ for classical Single Error Correction, Double Error Detection (SECDED). This introduces a new path towards designing resilient data-driven models, complementing established hardware redundancy, ECC, and FAT-based techniques.

cross CUA-Recovery: Benchmarking Error Recovery in Long-Horizon Computer-Use Agents

Authors: Xu Hu, Haoming Li, Yuxiang Wang, Jinxin Yang, Kangshuo Li, Licheng Wang, Dingyi Kang, Jiayu Song, Tianhao Wang, Zhanhua Hu, Xu Yang, Qiannan Li, Bingzhe Li, Feng Chen

Abstract: As computer-use agents (CUAs) move to long-horizon workflows spanning multiple applications, undetected errors can silently corrupt persistent state and cause cascading failures in subsequent actions. Reliable error detection and recovery have become critical bottlenecks for building robust CUAs. However, existing benchmarks focus primarily on end-to-end task performance, providing limited insight into whether agents can detect and recover from errors during execution. The few benchmarks that evaluate recovery rely largely on manually injected errors, which may not reflect the types or horizons of failures induced by real agent policies. We introduce CUA-Recovery, a benchmark comprising test and training suites across multiple desktop applications. We construct it using ReRail, a framework for synthesizing long-horizon tasks and trajectories in a persistent desktop environment. ReRail composes MyPCBench tasks into new long-horizon workflows with executable ground truth, collects verifier-confirmed failures from multiple policies, and turns them into verified recovery trajectories. CUA-Recovery comprises a human-verified test suite of 2,250 erroneous states over 100 tasks and a training suite of 8.6K trajectories over 377 tasks. Evaluation on the test suite shows that agents struggle both to recognize inherited errors and to complete the task. Across six agents and six takeover depths, Error Awareness Rate (EAR) reaches at most 44.0% and Pass@3 at most 25.5%. After fine-tuning Qwen3.5-35B-A3B on the training suite, its average EAR rises from 19.9% to 35.2% on held-out recovery states, and its Rubric Score goes from 5.0% to 16.1%. It also gains 15.3 points on MyPCBench and 10.8 points on Odysseys for Pass@3, showing that the training trajectories help both with recovery and with completing tasks from a clean start. Our code is available at https://github.com/xu-hu-2002/CUA-Recovery.

URLs: https://github.com/xu-hu-2002/CUA-Recovery.

cross Inferring continuous cognitive traits from limited neuroimaging data using pairwise convolutional neural networks

Authors: Marjan Rashidi, Robert Bain, Craig Stark, Jeffrey Krichmar, Mary Hegarty, Elizabeth Chrastil

Abstract: Individual differences in cognitive abilities vary widely, yet the structural brain features supporting these differences remain poorly understood. Traditional brain-behavior analyses rely on predefined metrics such as volume or cortical thickness and typically assume linear relationships with behavior, limiting sensitivity in small datasets. Here, we introduce a pairwise convolutional ranking framework that learns directly from whole-brain T1-weighted MRI by making comparative rather than absolute predictions, which performs well even with small samples (N=108). We tested this method with four small-scale spatial abilities tasks. Regression analysis predicted performance with high accuracy across all tasks, achieving greater predictive power over standard regression and pointwise deep learning baselines. Iterative cortical-region removal (backward elimination) revealed brain regions supporting individual performance common to all four tasks, as well as task-specific regions. These results demonstrate that pairwise learning extracts meaningful behavioral information from structural MRI, providing prediction and interpretable insight into the neuroanatomy of individual differences.

cross CyberHull: Deterministic Malicious URL Isolation via Convex Hull

Authors: Avijit Gayen, Aniket Ghosal, Durba Chakraborty, Sayan Mondal, Md Abdul Aziz Al Aman, Angshuman Jana

Abstract: The emergence of increasingly sophisticated and evasion-resistant phishing attack strategies represents a significant threat to modern cybersecurity, particularly in cyber-physical systems and internet-based networks. Traditional probabilistic methods and certain machine learning approaches often struggle to detect novel attacks and may lack interpretability or portability. To address these challenges, we introduce CyberHull, a resource-efficient and deterministic framework for identifying malicious URLs through mathematically defined convex hull boundaries across multiple orthogonal feature domains, including structural content entropy, hyperlink graph topology, and semantic token distribution. CyberHull constructs compact convex hulls, referred to as vaults, using the QuickHull algorithm, with a training complexity of O(n log n) under suitable assumptions. URL classification is performed through point-in-polyhedron tests that impose relatively low computational overhead on standard CPUs. Experimental results demonstrate that CyberHull achieves 92 percent detection accuracy, substantially outperforming a baseline linear approach based on Principal Component Analysis (PCA), which achieves 32 percent accuracy. Furthermore, CyberHull offers a computationally efficient alternative to resource-intensive deep learning approaches for malicious URL detection.

cross When Does Experience Memory Help Closed-Loop LLM Driving Agents? A Controlled Evaluation with Resampled Warmup Pools, Token-Matched Fillers, and Audited Baselines

Authors: Yingxin Jia, Xuanyi Li

Abstract: When does experience memory help a closed-loop LLM driving agent? Published gains cannot say, because the two factors we find to matter most, which warmup experiences the pool contains and what any injected tokens do to the prompt, are controlled in none of them. We answer with a controlled evaluation on one audited HighwayEnv testbed (1Hz, gpt-4o-mini for the decisive contrasts). We treat the warmup pool as a resampled random factor (ten disjoint pools) and add token-matched irrelevant-filler controls, oracle-certified diagnostics, caution-pricing metrics, and quantified audits of the baseline ports. The probe suite spans flat-text retrieval (a DiLu port), a single-frame grid port (RESPOND-inspired), our episodic temporal-graph memory, a retrieval-free consolidated rulebook, and a flat K-step observation-history baseline. Four findings follow, each a property of this testbed. (1) Headline memory gains belong to the warmup draw. The reported pool is the best of ten for both retrieval memories, which beat No-Memory in only one pool of ten (sign test p=0.011), and even the rulebook's apparent pool-immunity collapsed on five fresh draws. (2) No retrieval design tested exceeds token-matched filler. The collision-only merge ceiling is reached by a single 15-token caution instruction, and metrics that price caution erase the rulebook's lead. (3) Representational sophistication is nowhere robustly operative. (4) Baseline port quality silently inverts conclusions. On this testbed, a single-pool comparison without filler controls would have reported memory gains that these controls reverse. We release the testbed, audited ports, seeds, and caches.

cross Anytime-Valid Runtime Monitoring of Learned Barrier Certificates

Authors: Zhipeng Cao, Taoran Wu, Weiru Cui, Luke Ong, Bai Xue

Abstract: Learned barrier certificates provide a principled mechanism for establishing safety of stochastic systems, but their validity may deteriorate during deployment when the underlying dynamics differ from those used for training. We propose an anytime-valid runtime monitor that audits a learned barrier certificate using observed closed-loop trajectories. The monitor is intended to detect loss of statistical support for the deployed barrier condition; it does not by itself guarantee detection before unsafe entry or safety of a subsequent intervention. Specifically, the conditional barrier-drift condition is formulated as an episode-wise sequential hypothesis test, and observed barrier increments are transformed into a nonnegative test supermartingale, yielding finite-sample false-alarm control under arbitrary stopping times. Inspired by finite-horizon safety verification, we further introduce a deterministic temporal shaping of the monitoring boundary, making the monitor more conservative early in a prescribed deployment horizon while preserving anytime-validity. The monitor is designed to detect statistical evidence that the safety certificate is no longer consistent with the observed dynamics, rather than to predict unsafe entry. To assess the monitor empirically, we evaluate empirical alarm behavior under valid and invalid certificates. Code and data are available at https://github.com/FormalCert-AI/runtime-barrier-monitor.

URLs: https://github.com/FormalCert-AI/runtime-barrier-monitor.

cross Give the Model a Finger: Developmentally Aligned Evaluation of Counting in Vision-Language Models

Authors: Yingxin Jia, Hui Geng, Wenxin Yang, Mohan Zhang

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are exact on small sets and drift into estimation on larger ones, a profile reminiscent of children before they master counting. Children cross the barrier with epistemic actions: pointing at each item while reciting number words, moving counted items aside, partitioning the array. We give VLMs the same actions as dumb image tools with no visual intelligence of their own in a training-free agent loop. Our central claim is about measurement, not intervention: a model that counts by marking leaves a normally hidden serial procedure on the image, and the developmental principles of counting turn those marks into an interpretable audit of serial visual reasoning: every tag is classified against point-level ground truth as on-target, duplicate, phantom, or omitted, the answer is checked against the number of tags, and order irrelevance is tested by forcing opposite traversal orders. Across 9 models, 682 images, and 7 harness conditions we find that (i) the exact-counting range is a model-specific signature spanning nearly two orders of magnitude (R80 from 3 to 150 dots); (ii) as an intervention the finger mostly fails: tags help 4 of 9 models on small-count photographs, hurt imprecise pointers, and rarely beat a verbal routine or a blind point list; what the loop buys is an auditable trace, not accuracy; (iii) as measurement it succeeds: a tag-perturbation probe shows that precise pointers read their answers off the tags, pointing precision tracks which models benefit, dense scenes fail by omission, and models rarely revise after seeing their own tags; and (iv) the capacities dissociate: models follow an instructed traversal order yet often change their answer when it reverses, and children's regular-over-scattered advantage appears only under the routine and survives lattices that defeat a rows-times-columns shortcut.

cross TerraNostra-Bench: Benchmarking Spatial Downscaling and Temporal Rollout for Global 1-km Land-Use Reconstruction

Authors: Amirpasha Mozaffari, Marina Casta\~no, Stefano Materia, Joan Vedr\'i, Jordi Varela-Agrelo, Dario Garcia-Gasulla, Etienne Tourigny, Mario Acosta, Amanda Duarte

Abstract: Uncertainty in the terrestrial carbon cycle remains a major constraint in climate projections, partly due to imperfect land surface representations in Earth system models. We propose a data-driven framework for high-resolution land-use reconstruction, treated as a semantic-segmentation task, that combines coarse-resolution scenario data and climate classification with static geophysical features to produce annual land-use maps at 1 km resolution. We introduce TerraNostra-Bench, a change-aware evaluation protocol that separates spatial downscaling from autoregressive temporal rollout and scores models on the rare land-use-change pixels rather than the dominant static background. Using it, we compare convolutional (U-Net) and transformer (Swin) backbones in autoregressive and non-autoregressive schemes against persistence, linear, and shallow convolutional baselines. The autoregressive prior is the most informative input yet accumulates error over long rollouts: autoregressive models lose 5.7-7.2 accuracy points over 13 years, against 2.2-2.8 for non-autoregressive models. This trade-off is governed by the autoregressive choice rather than the backbone. It can be tuned by an inference-time prior-trust threshold that requires no retraining and by the training-time weighting of reconstruction and change objectives; the two levers trace complementary frontiers, with calibration recovering up to 10 accuracy points while keeping change sensitivity above the persistence floor. Extrapolating beyond the training window, backward to 1955-1959 and forward to 2015-2019 under scenario forcing, the models retain most of their in-window skill and show the same trade-off, indicating it is intrinsic to the design. This work provides the methodological foundation and evaluation surface for a forthcoming temporally continuous land-use product.

cross Certified Adaptive List Decoding for Error-Correcting Codes

Authors: Jaeho Hwang, Byung-Sun Won, Uiwon Hwang

Abstract: Maximum-likelihood (ML) decoding of linear block codes is NP-hard, so neural decoders are evaluated by how closely they approach it. We observe that proving a candidate optimal can be far easier than finding it, and build a decoder that does both. We cast decoding as a search over an absorbing-state discrete diffusion model, whose sequential commitment of coordinates turns generation into a tree, and run a probability beam search over it. A dual bound from a linear-programming relaxation and a distance-based test, both derived from the code, then certify per frame that the current candidate is the global ML solution; because both are theorems, certified frames incur no accuracy-computation trade-off. Across $15$ codes and five operating points we certify up to $99.9\%$ of frames, the two tests dividing the space as their derivations predict, one strong for sparse parity-check matrices and the other for codes with a large minimum distance. Certification also behaves as a per-frame reliability signal, with $96.7$-$100\%$ of all frame errors falling on uncertified frames. Against an exact ML reference at $5$ dB the decoder is within one standard deviation on three of the four codes where the reference is uncensored, and against four neural baselines it attains the best bit-error rate in $71$ of $75$ cells.

cross ProVe: Proposer-Verifier Decoding

Authors: Ziqi Jin, Weiwen Xu, Jiaxi Li, Yujie Zhong, Qi She, Aixin Sun

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive (AR) language models by letting a small model draft tokens serially and a large model verify them in parallel. We reverse these roles. Starting from a standard AR checkpoint, we fine-tune the large model into a proposer that predicts a block of future tokens in a single forward pass, then use one additional forward pass to verify the proposed block. This gives ProVe, Proposer-Verifier decoding. The verifier can be a smaller AR model or the proposer itself; the latter gives ProVe-lossless, which reproduces the proposer's greedy AR decoding. Block proposals already provide substantial room for acceleration: with a block size of 16, the proposed block has an average AR-matching prefix of 6.6 tokens on GSM8K. Across six benchmarks with a 32B proposer, ProVe-lossless reaches 2.5x average AR throughput without training a separate draft head. With a small 3B verifier, ProVe reaches 2.7x average throughput at 2.6 accuracy points below AR on the six-task average, providing a controllable tradeoff between speed and accuracy.

cross SAPD: Learning from Agent Experience without Environment Interaction

Authors: Juncheng Diao, Haibin Wang, Wei Shi, Yifan Ding, Qingbin Li, Rongxiang Weng, Xunliang Cai

Abstract: Scaling the training of Large Language Model (LLM) agents typically requires a large collection of executable environments, whose construction, maintenance, and operation constrain the expansion of training experience. Existing offline trajectories offer an opportunity to reuse interaction experience, yet effectively refining the current policy without revisiting these environments remains challenging: supervised fine-tuning (SFT) can lead to catastrophic forgetting, while online reinforcement learning requires real-time interaction with the environment. To address this challenge, we propose Step-Advantage Policy Distillation (SAPD), a method for learning from offline trajectories using relative step advantages. SAPD decomposes multi-turn trajectories into individual decision contexts for on-policy distillation, allowing the student to generate responses conditioned on recorded histories, and introduces relative step advantage signals to stabilize training. The entire process requires neither new environment observations nor additional multi-turn interactions. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Search-based QA show that SAPD improves performance across different teacher configurations, mitigates catastrophic forgetting compared with SFT, and runs at least $4.7\times$ faster per training step than online OPD. We will release our code soon.

cross LaLa-World: Scaling Latent World Models with Natural Language Actions

Authors: Junha Song, Geonmo Gu, Jeongeun Park, Heejeong Nam, Lucas Maes, Byeongho Heo, Dongyoon Han, Sangdoo Yun

Abstract: This paper shows that JEPA-style latent world models (LWMs) can learn across diverse domains and data sources by adopting the language-conditioned interface of video generation models. We establish this approach as an effective and efficient basis for planning with imagination and downstream policy fine-tuning. To this end, we introduce LaLa-World, a Language-conditioned Latent World model that learns from simulation and robot trajectories together with broader image and video data. Two designs make this work. First, we describe both numerical actions and visual changes in language, enabling joint training of a single future predictor across heterogeneous datasets. Second, we build the predictor on a language model, allowing it to interpret language conditions while predicting future latent states. The resulting predictor achieves higher average MPC planning performance than existing LWMs trained separately for each domain or task. Adding image and video data further improves average planning performance and robustness to action phrasing. In a matched comparison with an RAEv2-based video generation model baseline, LaLa-World reaches comparable planning performance with $6\times$ less training cost and $13.9\times$ faster planning. Beyond planning, the pretrained LaLa-World serves as an initialization for LaLa-Policy, which jointly predicts actions and future visual representations. LaLa-Policy performs competitively with Cosmos Policy on LIBERO and RoboCasa, with up to $5.5\times$ faster inference.

cross From Tokens to Files: Multi-Granularity Detection and Localization of AI-Involved Code

Authors: Yue Xu, Xiyang Wang, Hui Liu, Bolei He

Abstract: The increasing use of large language models for code generation calls for methods that distinguish purely human-written code from AI-involved code: files in which any part, however small, was generated or substantially modified by a model. Beyond flagging such files, we aim to localize the lines or blocks attributable to AI. Existing approaches compress a file into a single score, from surface stylistic features or a global code representation, which averages away the evidence when only a few blocks are AI-involved. We propose a framework that models AI authorship at the token level and predicts at the line, block, and file levels. A frozen code language model is equipped with two low-rank adapters, trained separately on machine-written and human-written files; their per-token log-likelihood ratio provides local evidence of AI authorship. Lightweight heads aggregate this evidence within lines and blocks, using the surrounding code context, and their local predictions are aggregated into a file-level decision. We train on mixed-authorship files built from human originals and their AI-generated or AI-modified counterparts, including continuation and fill-in-the-middle scenarios. On an internal test set of 11,773 AI-involved files across six generation regimes against 2,541 human files, our method reaches 72.4 macro F1 at a 1% FPR operating point. With no adaptation, it reaches 62.5 line-level F1 on HybridCodeAuthorship, an external human-AI co-writing benchmark with no training split, above the 56 its own paper reports as the best line-level result. On 19,445 human-written files from repositories created before 2022, the false-positive rate stays between 0.55% and 0.81% under the transported 1% threshold, so the operating point holds on real human code.

cross Enumeration-Guided Training of Neural Hamilton-Jacobi Safety Certificates

Authors: Mihaela-Larisa Clement, Tosca Pierro, Ezio Bartocci, Luca Marzari

Abstract: Hamilton-Jacobi (HJ) reachability analysis offers formal safety guarantees for control systems, but training accurate neural certificates remains difficult, particularly when unsafe regions are hard to characterize. We propose a verification-guided training framework that addresses this by using probabilistic enumeration of safety constraints to detect forward-invariance violations, i.e., states where the learned certificate incorrectly classifies unsafe behavior. This produces a compact, targeted set of counterexamples that more precisely captures the failure boundary than inputs generated by standard adversarial attack methods. Crucially, using this representation to fine-tune a pretrained HJ value function yields larger certified regions and reduces certification time across all tested benchmarks. We further introduce a shielding mechanism based on the refined certificates to enforce safety constraints at deployment time. We evaluate the proposed safety filter in a realistic, physics-based Isaac Sim environment and through real-world autonomous racing experiments, achieving zero safety constraint violations compared to a nominal controller and a backup policy based on Linear Quadratic Regulation (LQR). These results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed shielding mechanism in ensuring safe operation under high-speed, safety-critical conditions.

cross Lost in Recovery: Demystifying Recovery Behavior of Computer-Use Agents via Goal-Oriented Action Replay

Authors: Byeonghun Bang, Sangwoo Park, Jinheon Baek, Kangsan Kim, Youngwan Lee, Sung Ju Hwang

Abstract: Recent advances in large (vision) language models have enabled computer-use agents that automate digital workflows via fine-grained keyboard and mouse operations. However, such agents remain fragile in long-horizon settings, where errors accumulate and executed actions leave unintended side effects, making the ability to recover from failures critical. Yet, existing remedies remain inadequate: environment resets are infeasible in proprietary settings, action replay is prohibitively expensive over long horizons, and API-based rollback is limited to web environments, leaving model-based recovery largely unexplored. To address this gap, we present ReTrace, a scalable evaluation framework for systematically studying recovery behavior in computer-use agents. We formalize Transition-Consistent Recovery: identical downstream actions from the recovered and target states must yield identical outcomes. We operationalize this criterion through goal-oriented action replay following controlled error injection. Using ReTrace, we present the first systematic study of autonomous, model-based recovery, revealing that (i) ideal recovery can unlock model's latent task completion capabilities, (ii) current computer-use agents fail to exploit their latent recovery capabilities, and (iii) accurate model-based recovery yields measurable end-to-end gains. Together, these findings establish recovery as a fundamental capability for building reliable computer-use agents. Our code and dataset is available at https://github.com/BangProx/ReTRACE.

URLs: https://github.com/BangProx/ReTRACE.

cross Neural Closed-Loop Identification of Nonlinear Systems with Stability and Generalization Guarantees

Authors: Laura Meroi, Mahrokh G. Boroujeni, Leonardo Massai, Giancarlo Ferrari-Trecate

Abstract: Neural networks are expressive models of nonlinear dynamics, but identifying such systems from closed-loop data raises two difficulties: the feedback loop correlates inputs with measurement noise, biasing standard open-loop estimators, and the identified model must be stabilized by the data-collection controller, so that its closed-loop behavior matches the true plant's. We derive a complete parameterization of all nonlinear systems stabilized by a given controller, generalizing the classical dual-Youla parameterization to the nonlinear setting. Building on this characterization, we propose Internal Controller Identification (ICI), a computationally tractable framework for identifying potentially unstable systems while ensuring closed-loop stability by construction; in the linear case, our indirect method reduces to an open-loop identification problem, for which standard consistency results apply. The framework accommodates contextual information, letting a single certified model identify a family of systems, and supports a PAC-Bayesian training objective that, when data are scarce and prior knowledge is available, bounds the identified operator's expected prediction loss. Experiments show that ICI identifies open-loop unstable plants where uncertified baselines diverge, outperforms direct methods under weak excitation and feedback-correlated noise, generalizes zero-shot to environments unseen during training, and, with limited data, avoids the overfitting of empirical risk minimization while providing a non-vacuous generalization bound.

cross Annealed correlation and quenched noise sensitivity in biased deep neural networks

Authors: Johan Jonasson

Abstract: In earlier work by Jonasson, Steif and Zetterqvist (2023), fully connected deep neural networks with layers of width $n$, i.i.d.\ standard Gaussian weights, sign activations and no bias terms were shown to be annealed and quenched noise sensitive with respect to any joint probability distribution $\mathbb{Q}^{(n)}$ on the input $\boldsymbol{\omega}$ and its perturbation $\boldsymbol{\eta}$ satisfying a mild positive-correlation condition and $\mathbb{Q}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\eta} \in \{\boldsymbol{\omega},-\boldsymbol{\omega}\}) \to 0$. Here we add independent Gaussian biases of the natural order and show that quenched noise sensitivity is retained over a broad range of depths, indeed under the slightly weaker assumption that $\mathbb{Q}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\omega} = \boldsymbol{\eta}) \to 0$. Thus, subject to the positive-correlation condition, quenched noise sensitivity holds under the minimal requirement that the perturbed input is asymptotically unlikely to coincide with the original input. At the same time, the annealed covariance remains bounded away from zero and is entirely accounted for by fluctuations in the network-dependent output means.

cross Building Telecom Language Models with RSOD: Domain Data, Specialist Post-Training, Memory-Conscious Distillation, and Networking Evaluation

Authors: Anil Srirangapatna Nagesh, Nitin Gupta, Anuj Taneja, Vivek Singh, Shane Eleniak, Theo Zhao, Moulidharan N, Shashank Keshetty

Abstract: Telecom language models must combine broad networking knowledge with specialist behavior across routing, broadband services, access technologies, and operational diagnostics while remaining practical to deploy as a single model. cross On-Policy Transition Distillation

Authors: Runyang You, Yongqi Li, Tiezheng Yu, Wenjie Li

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) consolidates expert capabilities into a single student by matching teacher and student final-layer distributions on student-generated rollouts. We investigate whether depthwise prediction transitions, the changes in predictions between layers, can provide additional task-relevant supervision. Our diagnostic study finds that stronger teacher--student agreement in intermediate transitions is associated with higher answer correctness within questions, with correct responses exhibiting lower mean transition KL across all measured intermediate layer pairs. These observations motivate On-Policy Transition Distillation (OPTD), which augments final-layer distribution matching by aligning teacher and student layer-to-layer log-probability increments on student-generated rollouts. Evaluations across three backbones and eight benchmarks spanning mathematics, coding, and instruction following show that OPTD achieves the best or tied-best overall performance in both dual- and three-domain distillation. In the three-domain setting, OPTD recovers $\approx 90\%$ of the overall teacher--student capability gap, compared with $\approx 75\%$ for OPD.

cross Auditing Where Concept Bottleneck Accuracy Comes From on CUB

Authors: Sascha Saralajew, Eleonora Poeta, Eliana Pastor, Ammar Shaker

Abstract: Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) promise predictions that humans can inspect and correct through concepts such as "curved beak". Yet on CUB-200-2011, their standard benchmark, progress is measured like that of ordinary classifiers: by task and concept accuracy. Neither reveals whether the label is predicted from the exposed concept scores, or whether a concept is supported by visual evidence of that concept rather than by co-occurring features such as cues to the species as a whole. We audit where the accuracy of CBMs and Concept Embedding Models (CEMs) on CUB comes from. The CUB annotations are too noisy to explain it: class-level training labels agree with per-image annotations on only ~82% of image--concept pairs, and classifiers trained on per-image annotations reach only ~37% species accuracy. Leakage probes show that CEM predictions cannot be reconstructed from their concept scores; the missing class information sits in internal embeddings. We identify four design axes through which class information can enter concept scores---receptive field, shared versus concept-specific representations, norm-dependent scoring, and task supervision. An ablation shows that most of it enters through non-local features: restricting a ResNet50 backbone to local BagNet patches lowers the accuracy recoverable from concept scores from 72% to 43%, close to what ground-truth annotations support, while joint training raises it again at the cost of concept accuracy. Localization and occlusion show that locality makes grounding measurable but does not guarantee it: under class-level labels, local concept scores use the head as a proxy for body, wing, and tail concepts, and CEM concept scores barely respond to occluding their supporting part. Task and concept accuracy should thus support, not drive, the evaluation of CBMs, and given the noisy annotations, CUB accuracy gaps should not be overinterpreted.

cross Quantifying the Threat: A Bayesian Probabilistic Framework for Heavy Metal Health Risks among Children in the Densu Basin, Ghana

Authors: T. Ansah-Narh, G. Y. Afrifa, J. B. Tandoh, E. N. N. Nortey, D. Adjei, R. Opoku-Sarkordie, R. Davis

Abstract: Groundwater in Ghana's Densu River Basin remains a key domestic water source, but multi-metal contamination threatens children's health. Previous deterministic hazard index assessments provide limited treatment of uncertainty, co-exposure, and spatial variability. This study applies a Bayesian probabilistic framework for non-carcinogenic risk assessment using 94 groundwater samples and measurements of Fe, Mn, Ni, Pb, Cd, and As. A Bayesian regression model propagates parameter uncertainty and accounts for contaminant co-occurrence. Adaptive Hamiltonian Monte Carlo is used for posterior inference, while inverse distance weighting generates spatially continuous risk surfaces. Pb, As, and Cd dominate cumulative risk. The framework provides uncertainty-aware, spatially explicit information to support groundwater monitoring, risk mitigation, and public health planning in data-limited regions.

cross Weak-$L^\alpha$ Noise, Strong Guarantees: Gradient Clipping under Affine Heavy Tails

Authors: Alyona Nekrasova, Makar Sidorov, Savelii Chezhegov, Alexander Gasnikov, Margarita Preobrazhenskaia, Igor Preobrazhensky, Eduard Gorbunov

Abstract: Gradient clipping is a key tool for obtaining high-probability convergence guarantees for stochastic first-order methods under heavy-tailed noise. Existing analyses typically assume a bounded central moment of order $\alpha\in(1,2]$, which excludes distributions with tails of order $u^{-\alpha}$ at the critical exponent. In this paper, we consider smooth convex optimization under affine heavy-tailed noise, whose scale may grow with the gradient norm. We derive high-probability convergence guarantees for clipped SGD (Clip-SGD) and its accelerated variant Clip-SSTM under a weak-$L^\alpha$ tail condition, which is strictly weaker than the corresponding moment bound. For $1<\alpha<2$, the clipping bounds have the same powers of the clipping level as in the moment case, and both methods attain the additive stochastic rate $K^{-(\alpha-1)/\alpha}$. The gradient-dependent noise contributes a faster-decaying term of order $K^{-2(\alpha-1)/\alpha}$, up to logarithmic factors. At $\alpha=2$, we characterize the additional logarithmic factor on the Lorentz scale and establish its necessity for the additive error at fixed confidence. We also analyze Clip-SGD with prescribed clipping levels and derive finite-horizon guarantees for reaching a neighborhood determined by the clipping bias. For additive noise, matching lower bounds show that this neighborhood is unavoidable, up to constants and the natural error cap. Numerical experiments with Pareto noise illustrate the theoretical results.

cross Near-Optimal Regret Bounds for UCB-Style Gaussian Process Bandits via Data Thinning

Authors: Shion Takeno, Shogo Iwazaki, Ichiro Takeuchi

Abstract: Gaussian process upper confidence bound (GP-UCB) has been widely studied, though its known regret upper bound is suboptimal. To obtain near-optimal regret upper bounds, existing studies mainly focus on non-adaptive methods, such as uncertainty sampling, whose practical effectiveness is often limited. This paper shows that, under a Gaussian noise assumption, UCB-style algorithms can achieve near-optimal regret upper bounds via data thinning, a statistical technique for creating independent datasets from a single dataset. First, we show that data thinning can yield tighter confidence intervals in the bandit setting, and that a direct extension of GP-UCB achieves a near-optimal simple regret upper bound. Furthermore, by leveraging the tighter confidence intervals and combining GP-UCB with a phased elimination algorithm, we show a near-optimal cumulative regret upper bound. Finally, we demonstrate the effectiveness of UCB-style algorithms incorporating data thinning via numerical experiments.

cross Fixed Sets and the Hardness of Computing Correlated Equilibria

Authors: Ioannis Anagnostides, Constantinos Daskalakis, Gabriele Farina, Noah Golowich, Tuomas Sandholm, Brian Hu Zhang

Abstract: We show that computing an approximate correlated equilibrium of a polytope game is PPAD-complete, and thus polynomial-time equivalent to finding a Nash equilibrium. This challenges the conventional view in game theory that correlated equilibria are more tractable objects than Nash equilibria. From a conceptual standpoint, we connect correlated equilibria to a natural generalization of fixed points, which we call fixed sets. A fixed set of a continuous function $f$ is a nonempty finite set $S$ such that $S \subseteq \operatorname{conv} f(S)$. Our main technical contribution is to prove that computing a fixed set is PPAD-hard even at inverse polynomial precision.

cross Predicting silent failures of learned inference under restricted access: what measurement physics and prior information explain

Authors: Mario Gianni

Abstract: Learned priors are used to reconstruct regions that measurements barely reach, and to report uncertainty there. They can fail silently: the reported interval excludes the truth while every ground-truth-free diagnostic stays within its noise. We call such errors the blind set and ask whether they can be predicted before the target data are seen. For a correctly specified linear-Gaussian model averaged over the prior, the risk of a silent failure factorises exactly into non-detection and non-coverage, which does not hold for learned systems in general; we build a score that approximates this reference by linearising a VAE prior. In a prospective study on real X-ray CT objects (2DeteCT) under simulated restricted access, with predictions committed by hash before any trial, the score ranked the realised blindness of two learned inference systems (latent Laplace and ensemble Kalman inversion) with Spearman $\rho = 0.79$ and 0.84 over 240 pre-specified errors. Simple structural predictors would also have passed these tests. Sequential post-hoc analyses, each specified before its own computation, show that under bounded reach the learned score gave no detectable improvement over a physics-based baseline, in rankings or trial-level log scores. At the weakest attenuation studied, prior information improved rankings over the physics-based baseline, most clearly for ensemble Kalman inversion, but a Gaussian prior did as well and the gain was not detectable at the trial level. The score is not a calibrated probability. Silent failures can be anticipated here, but their structure follows mainly from measurement access and the decision quantity rather than from the learned prior.

cross Foreword: Pre-Generation Prediction and Characterisation of LLM Test Failures using the Jacobian Lens

Authors: Somin Kim, Robert Feldt, Shin Yoo

Abstract: Testing LLM-based software is expensive: each input needs several sampled responses, each judged by a costly oracle. Further, every prompt or model change calls for re-testing. Pre-generation adequacy measures cut this cost by ranking inputs by failure risk based on the model's internal state. However, the state of the art, Clotho, models only passing reference inputs, and its hidden-state representation says nothing about why inputs fail. We present Foreword, a framework built on two ideas: learn from both passing and failing references, and represent inputs through the Jacobian lens (J-lens), an interpretability technique that reads intermediate activations out as scores over vocabulary tokens. Foreword pairs these representations with two estimators: a sparse logistic-regression probe or dual Gaussian Mixture Models (dual GMMs), which also yield a reference density (to flag inputs the references do not cover) and failing-class components (to group similar failures). On ten tasks and three open-weight LLMs, all four combinations of representations and estimators outperform Clotho on average, both for prediction of failure (ROC-AUC improves by 0.14 on average and by up to 0.31) and test prioritisation (15% more failures among the 100 top-ranked inputs on average, up to 33%). Prioritisation obtained using small models also transfers better to proprietary models (on average 22% and up to 41% more failures in the first quarter of prioritised inputs). Importantly, our qualitative case study shows that Foreword supports semantic characterisation of failures by pointing to ambiguities in labels or task specifications. Combined, Foreword can support testers for prioritising inputs, checking support for a prediction, and characterising specific failures, all without generating output.

cross Offline Makes Online Better: Calibrated Cache Compression for Video Generation

Authors: Zhen Wang, Ning Lai, Qingtao Yu

Abstract: Token merging is an effective approach to reducing KV-cache memory consumption in autoregressive video generation by clustering temporally redundant tokens and retaining only a small set of representatives. Existing methods, however, typically apply a uniform merging ratio across all attention layers and heads. We observe that different layers and heads exhibit substantially different yet jointly correlated sensitivities to token merging, and that these sensitivity patterns remain consistent across prompts. Based on this observation, we introduce CALM, a Calibrated Allocation for Layer-Head Merging that uses offline calibration to improve online cache compression. Exhaustively evaluating all possible layer-head allocations is prohibitively expensive. To enable efficient calibration, we show that attention-output error strongly correlates with end-to-end video quality and can therefore serve as a lightweight proxy for allocation risk assessment. We evaluate different allocation strategies using this proxy and construct a fixed layer-head risk map offline. Given a cache budget at inference time, CALM uses dynamic programming to determine the best allocation of merging ratios. Experiments across three different autoregressive video models demonstrate that CALM consistently outperforms uniform token merging under the same memory budget, by reducing the LPIPS to original videos by around 21% with higher video-quality scores.

cross Attractive Faces Skew Text-to-Image Reward Models and Leaderboards

Authors: Harvey Mannering, Zhiwu Huang, Adam Pr\"ugel-Bennett

Abstract: Human preference is now central to the evaluation, guidance, finetuning, and ranking of text-to-image models. However, the susceptibility of human preferences to bias in image generation remains underexplored. cross ExeBench-FHD: Addressing Fidelity Issues in Decompiler Evaluation

Authors: Akarsh Ashok, Dinil Mon Divakaran, Vivek Balachandran

Abstract: ExeBench is one of the most widely used benchmarks for evaluating LLM-based decompilers. We analyze ExeBench and identify nine issues that affect how faithfully a test measures a decompiler. Three of them prevent LLM decompilers from recovering C code from ExeBench's function. These are ambiguous parameter order when integer and floating-point types are mixed, unused parameters that optimized assembly may drop, and constants stored outside the function's assembly code. Six more affect test validity: non-void functions without a return, placeholder types, static or inline functions removed by the optimizer, functions named main, trivial bodies and truncated definitions. We present ExeBench-FHD, a backward-compatible revision of ExeBench that adds 16 new fields: call-site assembly, data sections, test wrappers, linked-binary disassembly and five warning flags. In ExeBench, 38.9% of functions have at least one of the five flags; dropping records with any of the four warning flags (all except unused_param, which marks functions that the new wrappers make evaluable) keeps 63.9% of the corpus. In an evaluation at -O2 on four architectures, the new fields raise GPT 6 Sol's re-execution rate by up to 26.5%, 19.1% and 21.0% on functions that read data sections, mix parameter types and have unused parameters, respectively.

Authors: Cecilia Diana-Albelda, Fiona Kekwick, Raghav Mehta, Aleksandra Kulbaka, Paul M. Matthews, Jesus Bescos, \'Alvaro Garc\'ia-Mart\'in, Wenjia Bai, Bernhard Kainz

Abstract: Adapting Vision Foundation Models (VFMs) to medical imaging via LoRA has become standard practice, yet model selection is guided almost entirely by downstream task metrics, treating backbones with similar performance as interchangeable. cross Geometry-Aware Block Selection for Sparse Variational Gaussian Processes

Authors: Matteo Hoxha

Abstract: Sparse variational Gaussian processes (SVGPs) are a widely used, principled approach to scalable Gaussian process modelling. Their success stems from their theoretically interpretable structure: they provide a lower bound on the marginal likelihood of the full GP while allowing scal- able stochastic optimization. Recent work has explored modifications of the posterior conditional distribution and has shown that such extensions can improve upon this well-established framework. In particular, introducing a block-diagonal structured scal- ing matrix leads to a tighter lower bound on the GP marginal likelihood. We investigate whether selecting blocks that tighten the variational objective improves test RMSE and NLL at a fixed inducing-point budget. Since the scaling matrix contributes to the variational lower bound through an upper bound on the log-determinant of the full residual covariance matrix, our work makes the following contributions. Second, we show how to train these models using a doubly stochastic optimization framework. cross Local Work, Global Progress: A Convergence Theory of AI Agent Collaboration

Authors: Ivan Ilin, Peter Richt\'arik

Abstract: Teams of large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly applied to complex tasks, but their collaboration protocols are mostly designed empirically, without convergence guarantees. We formalize collaborative problem solving as block optimization: each agent improves one part of a shared solution, guided by its local objective plus a correction for how its changes affect the other parts. A coordinator checks every proposal against the global objective and merges proposals that do not interfere with each other. We prove linear convergence up to an error floor that vanishes when the corrections are exact. Interfering proposals can also be merged once their combination has been checked; the rate then depends on how well the proposals actually combine, not on worst-case interactions. With GPT-OSS-120B agents on robot routing, a three-agent team finds collision-free plans in every run, better than those of a single agent that controls all robots. On a larger routing task, a six-agent team completes its search about twice as fast as the single agent (in median). Merging the best checked combination of proposals, rather than only proposals that do not interfere, cuts the team's search time by about 30%. With constants measured on the runs, our convergence bound holds in every round and follows the observed progress. We also test the theory on query planning in SQLite and on a synthetic task.

cross Adaptive Replay Refinement: Allocating Test-Time Compute Between Revision and Resampling

Authors: Ahsan Bilal, Muhammad Ahmed Mohsin, Muhammad Umer, Ayesha Mohsin, Sarmad Bilal, Dean F. Hougen

Abstract: Test-time scaling allocates additional inference compute to refining existing solutions or generating new ones. This raises a fundamental question: Should I keep improving this solution, or stop working on it and try a completely new solution? Existing methods make this decision using fixed refinement schedules, self-verification judgments, or learned controllers, which can waste compute or rely on signals that may be incorrect or require additional training. We introduce Adaptive Replay Refinement (ARR), a training-free method that detects when a stationary revision reaches a fixed point and reallocates the released compute to a new independent sample; replay decoding treats the current solution as a draft and verifies unchanged tokens in parallel, enabling efficient fixed-point detection. These results demonstrate that detecting refinement exhaustion enables adaptive reallocation from revision to independent sampling, improving inference efficiency while highlighting the limits of self-revision. Code is available at https://github.com/AhsanBilal7/adaptive_refine.

cross Debiased Prior-Data Fitted Networks for Amortized Bayesian Semi-Parametric Inference

Authors: Valentyn Melnychuk, Emil Javurek, Stefan Feuerriegel

Abstract: Prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) have recently gained popularity as an end-to-end approach to amortized Bayesian inference. Yet, their statistical behavior for semi-parametric targets, such as means under missingness or causal effects, remains poorly understood. In this work, we develop theoretical foundations for debiased PFNs to perform inference on finite-dimensional targets in general semi-parametric settings. First, we show that, despite being Bayes-optimal with respect to the target, an idealized PFN can exhibit a posterior concentration rate that implicitly depends on the concentration rates of (latent) nuisance functions and can become slower as the prior becomes more diverse. Then, to speed up the target posterior concentration rates, we introduce two generic debiasing strategies based on efficient influence functions: (i) a label correction and (ii) a prior correction. Finally, we show that the debiased PFNs (i) & (ii) possess concentration rates that asymptotically match a convergence rate given by traditional frequentist bias-corrected estimators. In this way, we establish a novel connection between amortized Bayesian inference with PFNs and frequentist semi-parametric efficiency theory. Under additional assumptions, this allows us to guarantee local and global optimality of PFN-based estimators (namely, the Bernstein-von Mises property and minimax optimality, respectively). Therefore, our work provides guidelines for designing debiased PFNs for semi-parametric inference with principled and provable asymptotic guarantees.

cross Disclosure Begets Disclosure: A Self-Reinforcing Privacy Loop in LLM Agents with Persistent Memory

Authors: Masahiro Kaneko, Hiromi Arai, Timothy Baldwin

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use persistent memory to personalize responses, and personal attributes in this memory pose a privacy risk if exposed. We ask how consulting memory during dialogue changes what users go on to say, and thus what the memory comes to reveal. In multi-session simulations, a simulated user with 24 personal attributes talks with a support agent that does or does not consult memory, and in both settings attributes are inferred from the full accumulated record. Across four support models, memory raises response quality but also empirical leakage, the share of attribute values correctly inferred from the record, by a margin that widens over sessions. The cause is a loop in ordinary, non-adversarial assistance. Memory-conditioned responses carry earlier values forward, often as presuppositions, and users affirm them or leave them uncorrected, so that cues and the agent's own guesses become recorded values. The loop reinforces itself, and removing the agent's mentions of an attribute lowers its later disclosure about twice as much as single-turn effects predict. Users cannot leave it on their own, since correcting a value discloses the attribute, declining to discuss it shifts later predictions toward sensitive values, and incorrect values left in place become entrenched. The loop is best weakened at the agent's response. Instructing the agent not to state or presuppose attributes brings leakage to about the no-memory level while keeping most of the quality gains, whereas filtering or anonymizing what users say or what is stored gives up much of them.

cross ABACUS: A Benchmark for Image Editing Models to Assess Object Category and Counting Under Scene Conservation

Authors: Aymen Lassoued, Angelique Loesch, Romaric Audigier

Abstract: Instruction-based image editing models can now add, remove a specified number of objects or change their category while preserving the scene. Yet these models often fail to produce the correct number, and these failures grow sharply as scenes become more complex and the requested count increases. Current benchmarks largely miss this problem: they score edits with holistic similarity metrics or with vision-language model (VLM) judges that are themselves poor at counting, so numerical errors go unnoticed or are graded incorrectly. As a result, the true numerical ability of today's editing models has never been accurately measured. We address this issue with ABACUS, a new benchmark, evaluation protocol and dedicated scoring metric for numerical image editing. Our protocol is judge-free: no VLM or MLLM scores an edit or counts objects (a VLM only confirms the category of rare ambiguous crops). Instead, it verifies each edit at the instance level against panoptic ground truth confirmed by detector consensus, checking both that the exact requested count was achieved and that the rest of the scene was preserved, across the add, remove and change operations. By evaluating leading image editors, our method reveals substantial numerical editing failures that worsen with increasing scene complexity and object count; failures that prior benchmarks often overlooked or misjudged.

Authors: Martin Schmidt, Gonzalo Mateos

Abstract: Generating graph signals is an important task in machine learning, and incorporating the underlying graph into a generative model enables structurally-informed distribution modeling. However, said graph is often noisy or imperfectly estimated, and structural errors can affect the generative process and the resulting signal distribution. While recent work has established stability guarantees for graph neural ordinary differential equations, the robustness of graph-aware reference dynamics, as used in topological Schr\"odinger bridge models, remains largely unexplored. Here, we investigate the structural stability of continuous-time generative models whose dynamics combine a graph filter with a learned graph neural network.
