cross CountVideoBench: A Benchmark to Evaluate Prediction Biases on the Object Counting task by Video-Language Models

Authors: Marc Chapus, Carlos Crispim-Junior, V\'eronique Eglin, Atilla Baskurt

Abstract: Object counting in videos remains a poorly characterized capability of multimodal large language models. Counting questions are included in many existing VideoQA benchmarks, but the ground-truth count distributions in these benchmarks are heavily skewed toward small values. Across the nine benchmarks we survey, the median ground-truth count is at most five, and the three most frequent values together account for between 45% and 93% of the videos. The resulting mean accuracy is then dominated by the most frequent counts; a value-dependent bias at a rare count contributes negligibly to the average and remains invisible. We introduce CountVideoBench, a benchmark of 2,600 synthetic videos covering 26 cardinalities in the interval [5, 50] (100 videos each), grouped into six clusters of consecutive counts that span the range while enabling near-neighbor comparisons. Each video contains a single class of static objects under a controlled camera motion, removing object motion and reducing recognition as confounds. We evaluate sixteen video-language models from nine architecture families in a zero-shot setting. No model exceeds 27.6% Exact Match. The dependence of accuracy on cardinality varies across models, ranging from monotonic decline to a non-monotonic peak-and-collapse pattern, which rules out increasing difficulty with cardinality as the dominant explanation of counting failure. Every model further concentrates its predictions on its own set of preferred integers, with accuracy peaks coinciding with these values and collapsing at adjacent counts. These findings highlight the need for balanced, diagnostic counting benchmarks to reliably assess this fundamental visual capability.

cross Confessions of a Masked MDP

Authors: Sebastian Reboul, Lorenzo Croissant, H\'el\`ene Halconruy

Abstract: In reinforcement learning, one presumes the action set to be known ahead of time, and, thus, one must know if any state-action pairs are not permissible ahead of computations. In some applications, determining this action mask is the main computational bottleneck. Isolating this problem to highlight its core properties, we study how to combine the search for an optimal policy with the learning of the mask itself, under a linear MDP model in which each attempted action reveals the exact value of a linear score determining its validity. In this framework, we first construct an algorithm based on deterministic optimistic optimization and show that it is minimax optimal, which we show is also computationally efficient. However, it is sub-optimal on non-degenerate instances, as we show. We propose an alternative, which is also computationally efficient, which plans its exploration strategy. This algorithm admits a formal interpretation as an analogue of information-directed-sampling for deterministic feedback, which may be of independent interest. Finally, we study an application of this framework to transfer learning, where the invalid action set changes over time.

cross Brain2Vec: A Self-Supervised 3D Foundation Model for Structural Brain MRI

Authors: Spiros Maggioros, Guray Erus, Gareth Harman, George Aidinis, Alexander Getka, Aristeidis Sotiras, Christos Davatzikos

Abstract: Deep learning models have become essential tools in medical imaging, yet training them end-to-end for diverse tasks remains challenging due to limited labeled data and computational constraints. To address this, we introduce Brain2Vec, a foundation model pre-trained on 74,425 T1-weighted Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) scans pooled from multiple studies. Brain2Vec can serve both as a weight initialization backbone for fine-tuning downstream tasks and as a standalone feature extractor. We evaluated Brain2Vec in three tasks: downstream predictive performance, convergence speed, and embedding quality. Using Brain2Vec's pretrained weights as initialization and fine-tuning on labeled data for a brain age prediction task, results demonstrate improved final accuracy and faster convergence compared to random initialization. Additionally, its frozen embeddings outperform anatomically defined volumetric features on four of five downstream classification tasks and perform competitively with a state-of-the-art foundation model pre-trained on substantially more data. Lastly, we show that Brain2Vec encodes information primarily from a spatially localized and neuroanatomically meaningful set of brain regions, and that manipulating a subset of latent features produces monotonic, region-specific effects on the reconstructed anatomy. Brain2Vec code and trained models are available as an open-source package on https://github.com/CBICA/Brain2Vec. Users can apply Brain2Vec locally by installing the package, or directly through the NiChart cloud platform https://cloud.neuroimagingchart.com, where Brain2Vec features can be derived without requiring local installation or specialized infrastructure.

URLs: https://github.com/CBICA/Brain2Vec., https://cloud.neuroimagingchart.com,

cross DISTRACT: Degradation-Indexed Study of Textual Reference Across Competing Targets

Authors: Dan Zlotnikov, Ohad Ben-Shahar

Abstract: Vision-language trackers (VLTs) accept descriptions intended to assist tracking, but accepting text does not establish that its content changes tracking decisions, particularly as visual evidence deteriorates. We introduce DISTRACT, a matched-intervention benchmark that holds video, target, and distractor fixed while crossing four text priors with four image degradations along a controlled severity axis. We evaluate five VLTs on four datasets using behavioral contrasts, layerwise representation probes, and activation patching. Molmo2 and JointNLT exhibit directed text use. Some effects contract under degradation, whereas others show no significant endpoint change after multiplicity correction. UVLTrack, MMTrack, and DUTrack respond only weakly to changes in text semantics, with no directed behavioral effect surviving FDR correction in the tested settings. Model-internal analyses reveal that at decision-near states, correct-versus-conflicting text is decodable in Molmo2 and JointNLT but not in the other three systems. In the responsive models, decodability peaks at late states where replacement produces little behavioral change, whereas replacing selected earlier states redirects conflict-associated trajectories toward the target. Because matched-correct donors do not significantly outperform unrelated donors, these interventions identify earlier intervention-sensitive states without establishing target-specific semantic rescue. DISTRACT thus exposes when and where text affects tracking under degraded visual evidence.

cross A Bochner Weitzenb\"ock Decomposition for Cellular Sheaf Laplacians, Curvature and Neural Sheaf Diffusion

Authors: Francesco Maria Guadagnuolo, Alessio Borgi, Dorina Thanou, Pietro Li\`o

Abstract: We extend the Bochner-Weitzenb\"ock decomposition to cellular sheaf Laplacians, splitting it into a positive semidefinite term, the Bochner sheaf Laplacian, and a block diagonal term, the Forman curvature. We show that the Bochner Laplacians form a class of cellular sheaves that extends both connection and matrix-weighted Laplacians, and generalizes signed graphs to $d$ dimensions. We give a holonomy characterization of their kernel, prove the related Cheeger inequalities, and attach to them a random walk that generalizes the Gremban expansion. For the Forman term, we prove a discrete Bochner theorem, which bounds the first cohomology of a cellular sheaf with nonnegative curvature, and we read the whole decomposition as an opinion-dynamics model. We then apply this machinery to Sheaf Neural Networks, whose learnt Laplacian is otherwise unobserved. The Bochner interpretation yields a node-level conviction score that acts as an operational importance measure under frozen-model interventions, while the Forman profile provides a local descriptor of the learnt operator.

cross Claims of emergence in AI should be grounded in information decomposition

Authors: Christoph Riedl, Fernando E. Rosas

Abstract: When a language model develops capabilities not traceable to its individual components, when a multi-agent AI system outperforms each agent on its own, or when a human-AI pair succeeds where either fails in isolation, AI researchers describe it as a case of ``synergy'' or ``emergence''. However, these are usually informal assessments not supported by a theoretical framework, making these claims impossible to compare or even meaningfully dispute. Here we argue that \emph{information decomposition can serve as a unifying formal framework to operationalize assessments about synergy in AI systems}. Information decomposition provides a way to distinguish qualitatively different \emph{types} of information, including unique information available from individual components, redundant information shared across multiple components, and synergistic information accessible only from parts taken together. This gives claims of emergence a precise, falsifiable criterion: a system's behavior is emergent only if it carries synergistic information about task-relevant outcomes. We apply this lens to attention heads, autonomous agents, and human-AI teams, showing how it organizes existing knowledge across scales and substrates, while also generating cross-level conjectures that siloed fields would miss.

cross BitMTP: When Multi-Token Prediction Meets Low-Bit Large Language Models

Authors: Ning Zhang, Shihao Wang, Jinrui Zhang, Chaodong Xiao, Lei Zhang

Abstract: For efficient deployment of large language models (LLMs), methods of ultra-low-bit backbones reduce weight storage, memory traffic, and linear-layer execution cost, while multi-token prediction (MTP) methods reduce sequential decoding steps by predicting multiple future tokens in parallel. Although complementary, the direct combination of these methods cannot accelerate decoding at comparable quality: masked drafting lacks intermediate token input supplied in next-token training, while wider computation and causal key-value (KV) updates can offset the savings from fewer steps. To address these challenges, we propose BitMTP, an algorithm-system co-design framework for efficient MTP on ultra-low-bit LLM backbones. A joint next-token prediction and MTP objective equips a single model with both autoregressive (AR) generation and block-causal drafting. Confidence-only decoding, speculative-only decoding, and hybrid decoding provide different quality-throughput operating points without using separate draft and verifier models. Accepted-prefix alignment keeps the committed prefix, materialized causal KV boundary, and CUDA Graph replay state consistent for continued generation. BitMTP uses this contract to select width-specialized low-bit execution and reuse captured graphs over shared KV storage. On NVIDIA H20, BitMTP with hybrid decoding achieves five-task mean speedups of 2.2x on BitNet b1.58-2B and 2.1-2.4x on 1.7B-4B Bonsai models at comparable task quality. On RTX 3090 and Jetson Orin, it further achieves up to 2.0x in five-task mean throughput and 3.3x on MBPP+, respectively. These results demonstrate that BitMTP accelerates ultra-low-bit LLM generation across backbones, model sizes, and GPU types. The source code is released at https://github.com/PolyU-VCLab/BitMTP

URLs: https://github.com/PolyU-VCLab/BitMTP

cross Variance Reduction with Delayed Feedback for Non-Convex Optimization

Authors: Wei Jiang, Dingzhi Yu, Zechao Li

Abstract: We study variance reduction for non-convex optimization when gradient evaluations return with a delay. The server can update the parameter while earlier evaluations remain unfinished. We first analyze an ordered recursive momentum estimator for stochastic optimization. Under average smoothness and bounded variance, its oracle complexity is $O(1+\sigma^2\epsilon^{-2}+L\Delta_f(1+\sigma/\epsilon+\tau)\epsilon^{-2})$, where the target is an expected squared gradient norm of at most $\epsilon^2$ and $\tau$ bounds the feedback delay. We next study finite sums with $n$ components and obtain $O(n+L\Delta_f(\sqrt n+\min\{\tau,n-1\})\epsilon^{-2})$ evaluations. An asynchronous snapshot variant continues updating during full-gradient computations under an explicit bound on their completion time. For the stochastic method, we further replace a maximum-delay bound by a bound on the weighted amount of missing feedback.

cross OptScientist: Automated Discovery of Optimization Laws

Authors: Ma ZhenYao, Yue Liang, Jian-Qiao Zhu

Abstract: Many scientific phenomena are best understood as outcomes of underlying optimization problems, yet automated scientific discovery has largely focused on recovering equations that describe observed input-output patterns. We introduce OptScientist, a novel automated framework for discovering compact symbolic optimization laws directly from data. OptScientist comprises (i) a model that infers symbolic optimization problems by first learning to represent data as an optimization problem and then extracting its symbolic form, and (ii) an LLM agentic system embedding the model in a discovery process that integrates theoretical priors from scientific literature, discovers candidate laws, abstracts their underlying mechanisms, and suggests new data to collect. We first evaluate OptScientist on OptFeynman, a novel benchmark of 99 physics-derived optimization problems, where OptScientist recovers ground-truth laws for over 80% of the problems and remains robust to noisy contextual information, while substantially outperforming machine-learning, symbolic, and LLM-based model-discovery baselines. On human risky choice and central-bank monetary policy, OptScientist discovers compact symbolic optimization laws that achieve held-out predictive performance competitive with leading baselines, while using few parameters and substantially fewer tokens than LLM baselines. Overall, OptScientist extends automated scientific discovery to uncovering optimization laws that generate observed phenomena, enabling automated theory building across the physical, biological, behavioral, and social sciences.

cross Continual Concept Personalization in Diffusion Models via Residual-Space Adaptation

Authors: Yuzhi Liu, Enis Simsar, Giulia Lanzillotta, Thomas Hofmann

Abstract: Text-to-image personalization aims to equip pretrained diffusion models with the ability to learn user-specific concepts from only a few reference images. Existing methods, however, typically train each concept independently or assume that all concepts are available simultaneously. In practical applications, concepts often arrive sequentially, requiring models to continually learn new subjects, objects, or styles while minimizing interference with previously acquired concepts. We address this continual concept personalization setting by extending residual-space adaptation to the stochastic adaptation process of pretrained diffusion models. Our work achieves the highest concept fidelity among competing continual personalization methods while maintaining near-zero forgetting. Beyond these empirical gains, the learned residual-space dynamics reveal where adaptation capacity is concentrated within the diffusion model. Restricting adaptation to the identified layers by our method preserves nearly the same performance while reducing the number of trainable parameters by up to 72\%, demonstrating that residual-space dynamics can identify the layers that contribute most to continual concept personalization and directly guide more parameter-efficient adaptation.

cross Evolving Semantic Indexes for Generative Retrieval

Authors: Jinming Nian, Jyun-Yu Jiang, Henry Tsai, Yi Fang, Zhirong Liang

Abstract: Generative retrieval (GR) casts search and recommendation as autoregressive generation of discrete semantic identifiers (SIDs). SID design largely determines GR quality, but its discrete, interdependent components (SID construction, collision handling, decoding, and training) cannot be optimized end-to-end with gradients and remain largely hand-designed. We introduce a framework that improves these components through LLM-driven evolutionary program search, to our knowledge the first application of this approach to GR. A locally served, open-weight 31B model mutates executable GR components, so no proprietary LLM API is used, and a 1M-parameter proxy decoder provides fast feedback in place of repeatedly training the 1B target generator. Across 21 SID programs on NQ320K, proxy and target Recall@100 show strong rank correlation (Spearman $\rho = 0.90$). Guided by this proxy, evolution improves the seed GR on NQ320K, MSMARCO300K, and a multilingual e-commerce dataset under a fixed training recipe. On NQ320K, jointly evolving SID construction and collision handling improves Recall@1 from 55.8% to 59.3% and Recall@100 from 85.8% to 90.6%. A component study shows that proxy reliability depends on what is evolved: improvements transfer reliably for the semantic index but not for the generator's training objective or the full pipeline. LLM-driven evolutionary search is thus a practical tool for GR design, provided proxy reliability is established for the chosen search space.

cross Two $\operatorname{ReLU}$ Layers is All You Need

Authors: Florestan Brunck, Christoph Hertrich, Kilian Ruess, Jack Stade, Amir Yehudayoff

Abstract: We prove that every $\operatorname{ReLU}$ network can be exactly simulated by a $\operatorname{ReLU}$ network with two hidden layers. We present two proofs: one that is based on fillings in homological algebra and a direct combinatorial proof.

cross WildMAE: Structure-Aware Masked Autoencoding for Wildlife Image Reconstruction

Authors: Yicheng Zhang, Yujie Jin, Ke Zhang, Jiaxin Wu

Abstract: Camera-trap imagery presents substantial variation in animal appearance, illumination, background clutter, and visual degradation. Masked autoencoders (MAEs) offer a label-free framework for modeling such data through reconstruction of randomly hidden image patches. We present WildMAE, a decoder-side extension of MAE that combines local structure-aware refinement with masked-to-visible cross-attention. A zero-gated convolutional residual path introduces a two-dimensional neighborhood prior, while gated cross-attention allows masked positions to retrieve contextual information from visible-position features without replacing the original Transformer decoder. The associated training recipe progressively introduces normalized-pixel, Sobel-edge, and perceptual objectives. In the original Snapshot Serengeti study, covering 4,744 test images at 75% masking, the reported reconstruction scores were 27.90 versus 24.33 dB PSNR, 0.840 versus 0.780 SSIM, and 0.160 versus 0.320 LPIPS for WildMAE and vanilla MAE, respectively. Additional historical records document favorable average differences under four synthetic degradation conditions and describe component variants. We present the method, experimental observations, and their scope together: the retained metrics are historical protocol-dependent results, rather than a newly reproduced, harmonized comparison or a validation of restoration from genuinely unavailable pixels. WildMAE provides a practical architectural case study for combining global token mixing with spatial and targeted contextual refinement in wildlife masked-image modeling.

cross Neural Implicit Energy Optimization for LiDAR Mapping

Authors: Hrishikesh Viswanath, Md Ashiqur Rahman, Chi Lin, Damon Conover, Aniket Bera

Abstract: Neural implicit representations provide memory-efficient signed distance field (SDF) parameterizations for large-scale LiDAR scenes, but the geometry recovered from sparse and often noisy inputs carries jitter and artifacts. Classical geometric optimization techniques resolve such artifacts with surface energy functionals, whose minimizers correspond to smooth surfaces. Motivated by this principle, we formulate neural LiDAR mapping as the optimization of energy functionals over the parameter space of neural implicit representations. Specifically, we regularize the signed distance field with an energy functional defined on the shape operator of its level set, which represents the curvature of the surface, thus encouraging smoothness along the surface. This adapts classical geometric priors to the scale of million-point LiDAR maps. Experiments on large-scale datasets show reconstructions with improved smoothness at preserved geometric fidelity, and no additional inference-time cost. The code is publicly available at \href{https://github.com/HrishikeshVish/nieo}{https://github.com/HrishikeshVish/nieo}

URLs: https://github.com/HrishikeshVish/nieo, https://github.com/HrishikeshVish/nieo

cross Adaptive Multi-Token Prediction: How far should we go?

Authors: Emile Anand, Mohammed Rehan Parwani, Xinyuan Cao

Abstract: Multi-token prediction (MTP) can accelerate language-model decoding by drafting several tokens and verifying them in a single target-model pass. However, the best speculation horizon changes with context: longer drafts offer greater amortization when reliable, but can waste compute on suffixes discarded after a rejection. Unlike autoregressive drafting, where a stopping rule can decide token by token, MTP must fix the horizon before drafting, as all heads fire in the same forward pass. We therefore formulate horizon selection as an online control problem, using verification feedback to learn how far the draft can be trusted in each context, and introduce Adaptive Multi-Token Prediction (AMTP): a lightweight controller that requires no retraining and leaves the exact verifier unchanged. To guide horizon selection, we characterize the expected number of tokens committed at each horizon via acceptance survival, the probability that a draft prefix is fully accepted. Before each round, AMTP predicts how far the draft is likely to survive and picks the horizon with the highest expected tokens per second, learning freely from every verification pass. Theoretically, we show that any online learner's prediction error translates into throughput regret against the best horizon for each context, and in turn into a bound on wall-clock completion time. With online gradient descent, this regret is sublinear. Across five draft-target pairings built on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct with Medusa heads, Meta's MTP models, and Qwen3-4B-Inst-2507-MTP, evaluated on AIME 2025 and GSM8K, AMTP decodes up to 4.1x faster than standard decoding and matches or outperforms the fixed window in 8 of 10 settings, with throughput gains of up to 10%.

cross Learning Adaptable Dexterous Skills via Hybrid Dexterous Mimicking

Authors: Yuxuan Kuang, Jiashun Wang, Yixuan Sun, Yufei Ye, Sungjae Park, Umar Iqbal, Katerina Fragkiadaki, Shubham Tulsiani

Abstract: Dexterous multi-finger hands hold promise to unlock diverse skills like functional grasps, in-hand reorientation, and dynamic handover that the human-designed world assumes, yet existing learning recipes force a trade-off. Goal-reaching reinforcement learning generalizes to arbitrary object poses but, lacking any human prior, converges to unnatural and unreliable finger strategies; motion tracking reproduces human-like behavior from a single clip but cannot deviate from it. We present AdaDex, a goal-conditioned RL framework that turns a single hand-object trajectory of a few seconds---from mocap data or HOI reconstruction from in-the-wild RGB(D) video---into an adaptable dexterous skill. At the core of AdaDex is Hybrid Dexterous Mimicking: a shared policy is trained on two populations of massively parallel environments: motion tracking environments trained with dense motion tracking objective, which instill the human prior from human motion; and generalization environments with noised initial states and goals and a sparse object-success reward, which force the skill to work far off the reference. From seconds of human motion per skill, the resulting policies can learn diverse adaptable dexterous skills, such as functional grasping, object reposing, in-hand reorientation with finger gaiting, and dynamic behaviors like throw-and-catch. Our method outperforms baselines in terms of generalization and functionality, and we demonstrate transfer across simulators and to the real world. Project page: https://yuxuank.com/AdaDex/.

URLs: https://yuxuank.com/AdaDex/.

cross Joints, Camera, Action! Co-Generating 3D Motion and Video for Arbitrary Rigs

Authors: Noam Rotstein, Yoad Tewel, Chen Tessler, Haggai Maron, Gal Chechik, Or Litany, Yoni Kasten, Yuval Atzmon

Abstract: Video generation models can now depict remarkably realistic motion. Yet it is unclear how to use this motion beyond pixels: to animate a specific 3D asset and replay the animation in its 3D scene. For instance, a video model can show a monkey jumping over a crossbar, but it cannot return that jump as replayable 3D motion placed in the 3D scene. Here we present a model that directly co-generates the video and 3D motion for any supplied rig, along with a camera that registers this motion to the scene. Prior video-motion models either handle only human skeletons, often adding billions of parameters, or triangulate 3D motion from generated 2D pose maps. We instead keep 3D motion in a separate model that supports arbitrary skeletons, and connect it to a pretrained video model through a small set of cross-attention layers. This separation lets the motion model first learn how skeletons move from motion-only data, which is easier to obtain and augment than paired video. Additionally, our model learns to locate each joint in the video frames, which lets us recover the camera and place the 3D motion in the scene. To train across many skeletons, we use a generative video editor to place animated 3D assets in scenes suited to their actions, curating the edits so their known motion remains valid. Together with human and robot video, this yields nearly 200K training pairs. Our evaluations demonstrate that our model animates diverse rigs from text more faithfully than video-first pipelines, and its motion responds to the surrounding scene. It also turns video into rig motion, rig motion into video, and enables keyframe-based control of the 3D motion. Videos and additional results are available on our project page: https://research.nvidia.com/labs/par/projects/jca/.

URLs: https://research.nvidia.com/labs/par/projects/jca/.

replace Simulating, Fast and Slow: Learning Policies for Black-Box Optimization

Authors: Tim Bakker, Fabio Valerio Massoli, Thomas Hehn, Tribhuvanesh Orekondy, Arash Behboodi

Abstract: Simulators are vital in science and engineering, as they faithfully model the influence of design parameters on real-world observations. Optimizing design parameters to minimize a desired objective function is notoriously challenging since simulators are often non-differentiable black boxes. Moreover, each simulation incurs significant compute time, thus doing such optimizations naively may be prohibitively expensive. Furthermore, in experiment design settings, practitioners are typically required to solve the same optimization problem for various input distributions before proceeding to real-world experiments. Starting these related optimizations from scratch each time can be inefficient if the forward simulation model is expensive to evaluate. To address these challenges, this paper introduces a novel method for solving black-box optimization problems on various input distributions by learning a policy that guides the training of a differentiable surrogate. The surrogate's gradients are then used to optimize the simulation parameters with SGD. After training the policy, the cost for downstream optimization of problems involving black-box simulators is amortized, and we require up to $10\times$ fewer expensive simulator calls compared to baselines such as previous local surrogate-based approaches, numerical optimization, and Bayesian optimization.

replace Jacobian Descent for Multi-Objective Optimization

Authors: Pierre Quinton, Val\'erian Rey

Abstract: Many optimization problems require balancing multiple conflicting objectives. To generalize gradient descent, which is limited to single-objective optimization, we introduce Jacobian descent (JD) and its stochastic variants. This algorithm iteratively updates parameters using the Jacobian matrix of a vector-valued objective function, in which each row is the gradient of an individual objective. While several methods to combine gradients already exist in the literature, they generally struggle when the objectives conflict. In contrast, we propose projecting gradients to fully resolve conflict. We prove convergence to the Pareto front in the smooth convex case with this approach, a stronger guarantee than the usual convergence to a weakly Pareto stationary point. Our method also enables instance-wise risk minimization (IWRM), a novel learning paradigm in which the loss of each training example is considered a separate objective. Early experimentation on image classification shows promising results for IWRM. Lastly, we provide an efficient implementation of JD using the Gramian of the Jacobian matrix to drastically reduce memory requirements.

replace Physics Inspired Neural Networks Enable Efficient Graph Coloring

Authors: Lorenzo Colantonio (Department of Physics, Sapienza University of Rome), Andrea Cacioppo (Department of Physics, Sapienza University of Rome), Federico Scarpati (Department of Physics, Sapienza University of Rome), Maria Chiara Angelini (Department of Physics, Sapienza University of Rome), Federico Ricci-Tersenghi (Department of Physics, Sapienza University of Rome), Stefano Giagu (Department of Physics, Sapienza University of Rome)

Abstract: Combinatorial optimization problems near algorithmic phase transitions represent a fundamental challenge for both classical algorithms and machine learning approaches. Among them, graph coloring is a prototypical constraint satisfaction problem characterized by phase transitions that fragment the solution space into distinct clusters, making the search for valid solutions challenging. Here we introduce a physics-inspired neural framework that learns to solve large-scale graph coloring instances by combining graph neural networks with statistical-mechanics principles. Our approach integrates a planting-based supervised signal, symmetry-breaking regularization, and iterative noise-annealed neural dynamics to navigate complex solution landscapes. When the number of iterations scales quadratically with graph size, the learned solver successfully tackles increasingly challenging instances, even in regimes where finding valid solutions becomes particularly difficult, demonstrating its ability to extract meaningful information from the graph structure. The model generalizes from small training graphs to instances up to 20 times larger, demonstrating that neural architectures can learn scalable algorithmic strategies that remain effective in hard connectivity regions. These results establish a general framework for learning neural solvers that operate near fundamental phase boundaries in combinatorial optimization and inference.

replace FiTv2: Scalable and Improved Flexible Vision Transformer for Diffusion Model

Authors: ZiDong Wang, Zeyu Lu, Di Huang, Cai Zhou, Xiaoyu Yue, Wanli Ouyang, and Lei Bai

Abstract: \textit{Nature is infinitely resolution-free}. In the context of this reality, existing diffusion models, such as Diffusion Transformers, often face challenges when processing image resolutions outside of their trained domain. To address this limitation, we conceptualize images as sequences of tokens with dynamic sizes, rather than traditional methods that perceive images as fixed-resolution grids. This perspective enables a flexible training strategy that seamlessly accommodates various aspect ratios during both training and inference, thus promoting resolution generalization and eliminating biases introduced by image cropping. On this basis, we present the \textbf{Flexible Vision Transformer} (FiT), a transformer architecture specifically designed for generating images with \textit{unrestricted resolutions and aspect ratios}. We further upgrade the FiT to FiTv2 with several innovative designs, includingthe Query-Key vector normalization, the AdaLN-LoRA module, a rectified flow scheduler, and a Logit-Normal sampler. Enhanced by a meticulously adjusted network structure, FiTv2 exhibits $2\times$ convergence speed of FiT. When incorporating advanced training-free extrapolation techniques, FiTv2 demonstrates remarkable adaptability in both resolution extrapolation and diverse resolution generation. Additionally, our exploration of the scalability of the FiTv2 model reveals that larger models exhibit better computational efficiency. Furthermore, we introduce an efficient post-training strategy to adapt a pre-trained model for the high-resolution generation. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate the exceptional performance of FiTv2 across a broad range of resolutions. We have released all the codes and models at https://github.com/whlzy/FiT to promote the exploration of diffusion transformer models for arbitrary-resolution image generation.

replace Feature Selection for Latent Factor Models

Authors: Rittwika Kansabanik, Adrian Barbu

Abstract: Feature selection is crucial for pinpointing relevant features in high-dimensional datasets, mitigating the 'curse of dimensionality,' and enhancing machine learning performance. Traditional feature selection methods for classification use data from all classes to select features for each class. This paper explores feature selection methods that select features for each class separately, using class models based on low-rank generative methods and introducing a signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) feature selection criterion. This novel approach offers a theoretically sound guarantee of feature recovery under specific assumptions and has been demonstrated to outperform some existing feature selection methods on standard classification datasets. Additionally, it can be easily applied in a class-incremental learning setup, yielding performance comparable to that of some current methods.

replace Weight Space Learning for Certifiable Few-shot Transfer Learning

Authors: Fady Rezk, Royson Lee, Henry Gouk, Timothy Hospedales, Minyoung Kim

Abstract: In contemporary deep learning, a prevalent and effective workflow for solving low-data problems is adapting powerful pre-trained foundation models (FMs) to new tasks via parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT). However, while empirically effective, the resulting solutions lack generalisation guarantees to certify their accuracy - which may be required for ethical or legal reasons prior to deployment in high-importance applications. In this paper we develop a novel transfer learning approach that is designed to facilitate non-vacuous learning theoretic generalisation guarantees for downstream tasks, even in the low-shot regime. Specifically, we first use upstream tasks to train a distribution over PEFT parameters. We then learn the downstream task by a sample-and-evaluate procedure -- sampling plausible PEFTs from the trained diffusion model and selecting the one with the highest likelihood on the downstream data. Crucially, this confines our model hypothesis to a finite set of PEFT samples. In contrast to the typical continuous hypothesis spaces of neural network weights, this facilitates tighter risk certificates. We instantiate our bound and show non-trivial generalization guarantees compared to existing learning approaches which lead to vacuous bounds in the low-shot regime.

replace Dissecting the Impact of Model Misspecification in Data-driven Optimization

Authors: Adam N. Elmachtoub, Henry Lam, Haixiang Lan, Haofeng Zhang

Abstract: Data-driven optimization aims to translate a machine learning model into decision-making by optimizing decisions on estimated costs. Such a pipeline can be conducted by fitting a distributional model which is then plugged into the target optimization problem. While this fitting can utilize traditional methods such as maximum likelihood, a more recent approach uses estimation-optimization integration that minimizes decision error instead of estimation error. Although intuitive, the statistical benefit of the latter approach is not well understood yet is important to guide the prescriptive usage of machine learning. In this paper, we dissect the performance comparisons between these approaches in terms of the amount of model misspecification. In particular, we show how the integrated approach offers a ``universal double benefit'' on the top two dominating terms of regret when the underlying model is misspecified, while the traditional approach can be advantageous when the model is nearly well-specified. Our comparison is powered by finite-sample tail regret bounds that are derived via new higher-order expansions of regrets and the leveraging of a recent Berry-Esseen theorem.

replace PALATE: Peculiar Application of the Law of Total Expectation to Enhance the Evaluation of Deep Generative Models

Authors: Tadeusz Dziarmaga, Marcin K\k{a}dzio{\l}ka, Laura Musio{\l}, Patryk Marsza{\l}ek, Artur Kasymov, Piotr Ko\'scielniak, Marcin Mazur

Abstract: Deep generative models (DGMs) have caused a paradigm shift in the field of machine learning, yielding noteworthy advancements in domains such as image synthesis, natural language processing, and other related areas. However, a comprehensive evaluation of these models that effectively captures the interplay among fidelity, diversity, and novelty in generated samples remains a persistent challenge. A recently proposed approach, the Feature Likelihood Divergence (FLD), offers a theoretically grounded and practical tool in this regard but faces notable computational limitations. In this paper, we propose PALATE, a novel framework designed to enhance the evaluation of DGMs by addressing the efficiency constraints of FLD. Our approach is based on a peculiar application of the law of total expectation to random variables representing accessible data. When integrated with the MMD baseline metric and the recently introduced DINOv3 feature extractor, PALATE provides a comprehensive evaluation framework that matches or exceeds state-of-the-art methods while offering superior computational efficiency and scalability to large-scale datasets. Through a series of experiments, we demonstrate that the PALATE enhancement constitutes a practical and computationally efficient framework for holistic DGM assessment, particularly in detecting sample memorization and evaluating generalization capabilities. The source code is provided as supplementary material.

replace Federated Learning: From Theory to Practice

Authors: A. Jung

Abstract: This book offers a hands-on introduction to building and understanding federated learning (FL) systems. FL enables multiple devices -- such as smartphones, sensors, or local computers -- to collaboratively train machine learning (ML) models, while keeping their data private and local. It is a powerful solution when data cannot or should not be centralized due to privacy, regulatory, or technical reasons. The book is designed for students, engineers, and researchers who want to learn how to design scalable, privacy preserving FL systems. Our main focus is on personalization: enabling each device to train its own model while still benefiting from collaboration with relevant devices. This is achieved by leveraging similarities between (the learning tasks associated with) devices that are encoded by the weighted edges (or links) of a federated learning network (FL network). The key idea is to represent real-world FL systems as networks of devices, where nodes correspond to device and edges represent communication links and data similarities between them. The training of personalized models for these devices can be naturally framed as a distributed optimization problem. This optimization problem is referred to as generalized total variation minimization (GTVMin) and ensures that devices with similar learning tasks learn similar model parameters. Our approach is both mathematically principled and practically motivated. While we introduce some advanced ideas from optimization theory and graph-based learning, we aim to keep the book accessible. Readers are guided through the core ideas step by step, with intuitive explanations.

replace Accelerating Optimization via Differentiable Stopping Time

Authors: Zhonglin Xie, Yiman Fong, Haoran Yuan, Zaiwen Wen

Abstract: Optimization is an important module of modern machine learning applications. Tremendous efforts have been made to accelerate optimization algorithms. A common formulation is achieving a lower loss at a given time. This enables a differentiable framework with respect to the algorithm hyperparameters. In contrast, its dual, minimizing the time to reach a target loss, is believed to be non-differentiable, as the time is not differentiable. As a result, it usually serves as a conceptual framework or is optimized using zeroth-order methods. To address this limitation, we propose a differentiable stopping time and theoretically justify it based on differential equations. An efficient algorithm is designed to backpropagate through it. As a result, the proposed differentiable stopping time enables a new differentiable formulation for accelerating algorithms. We further discuss its applications, such as online hyperparameter tuning and learning to optimize. Our proposed methods show superior performance in comprehensive experiments across various problems, which confirms their effectiveness.

replace Rethinking Generalization in Knowledge Graph Embedding: An Empirical Study and Sparse Peak Regularization

Authors: Linyu Li, Zhi Jin, Yuanpeng He, Dongming Jin, Haoran Duan, Zhengwei Tao, Xuan Zhang, Jiandong Li, Huanyao Zhang, Gadeng Luosang, Nyima Tashi

Abstract: Knowledge graphs (KGs) provide structured representations of entities and relations, and underpin data management and Web applications such as search, recommendation, and question answering. Since Web-scale KGs such as Freebase and Wikidata are inherently incomplete, knowledge graph embedding (KGE) maps entities and relations into continuous vector spaces and is widely used to infer missing facts through link prediction. Despite extensive progress in KGE model design, the role of regularization has been less systematically examined, especially beyond tensor-decomposition models. The primary contribution of this paper is a comprehensive empirical study of regularization for KGE from the perspective of generalization. Across translation-based, tensor-decomposition-based, GNN-based, temporal, and multimodal KGE models, we show that overfitting is common across model families and that appropriate regularization can substantially improve both generalization and predictive performance. Motivated by these findings, we further propose Sparse Peak Regularization (SPR), a simple and model-agnostic regularizer that introduces rank-based selective sparsity into the regularization target. SPR concentrates the penalty on dominant high-energy embedding components, which contribute most to the embedding norm and overfitting risk, while leaving low-energy components unpenalized so that weak but useful signals are not unnecessarily suppressed. Experiments on eight benchmark datasets and diverse KGE models show that SPR achieves the best results in nearly all settings compared with existing regularizers such as N3, DURA, and ER. These results show that regularization deserves more systematic attention in KGE, and that selective regularization is an effective and broadly applicable strategy for improving the generalization of KGE models.

replace Double-Diffusion: Balancing Speed, Accuracy, and Uncertainty in Probabilistic Forecasting for Urban Sensor Networks

Authors: Hanlin Dong, Arian Prabowo, Hao Xue, Shuang Ao, Tianyi Zhou, Yuxuan Liang, Flora D. Salim

Abstract: Urban sensor networks need forecasts that are accurate, carry useful uncertainty, and refresh fast enough to act on as new readings arrive. These goals conflict: deterministic models give no distribution, while diffusion forecasters model uncertainty but denoise from pure noise over many steps. We present Double-Diffusion, which integrates a closed-form graph-heat prior into a denoising diffusion model. The prior is a parameter-free low-pass forecast over the sensor graph, and it serves two roles: it is the residual target the model generates, and it conditions the denoiser. The reverse process therefore starts near the prior and denoises a short warm-started chain instead of synthesizing from pure noise; the name denotes this composition, a graph diffusion feeding a denoising diffusion. A compact denoiser, DD-Net, is trained as a Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Model (DDPM) in the Resfusion warm-start formulation, so generation refines the prior over a short truncated chain rather than synthesizing from pure noise; a graph-spectral read-out of the prior residual sets its switchable spatial filter per domain from training data alone. On four real-world air quality and traffic networks, Double-Diffusion attains the best CRPS of all probabilistic methods on every dataset and stays competitive in point accuracy with the strongest baselines, at a fraction of the sampling cost of from-noise diffusion. The code is available at: https://github.com/teddyicare/Double-Diffusion

URLs: https://github.com/teddyicare/Double-Diffusion

replace Gradient Descent on Logistic Regression: Do Large Step-Sizes Work with Data on the Sphere?

Authors: Si Yi Meng, Baptiste Goujaud, Antonio Orvieto, Christopher De Sa

Abstract: Gradient descent on logistic regression has many fascinating properties not fully captured by classical analyses. For linearly separable data, the iterates converge in direction to the maximum-margin separator regardless of how large the step size is. For non-separable data, however, cycles may occur even for step sizes below the local stability threshold of $2/\lambda^*$, where $\lambda^*$ is the largest eigenvalue of the Hessian at the solution. We ask whether requiring all examples to have equal norm is a sufficient condition for global convergence below this threshold. We prove that this is indeed true in one dimension. In higher dimensions, even equal-norm datasets can produce cycles, although global convergence holds for the population loss when the examples are uniformly distributed on the sphere. Finally, in one dimension, we relax the equal-norm assumption to an annulus condition that ensures global convergence below the stability threshold, suggesting a path towards analogous sufficient conditions in higher dimensions.

replace Blending data and physics for reduced-order modeling of systems with spatiotemporal chaotic dynamics

Authors: Alex Guo, Michael D. Graham

Abstract: While data-driven techniques are powerful tools for reduced-order modeling of systems with chaotic dynamics, great potential remains for leveraging known physics (i.e. a full-order model (FOM)) to improve predictive capability. We develop a hybrid reduced order model (ROM), informed by both data and FOM, for evolving spatiotemporal chaotic dynamics on an invariant manifold whose coordinates are found using an autoencoder. This approach projects the vector field of the FOM onto the invariant manifold; then, this physics-derived vector field is either corrected using dynamic data, or used as a Bayesian prior that is updated with data. In both cases, the neural ordinary differential equation approach is used. We consider simulated data from the Kuramoto-Sivashinsky and complex Ginzburg-Landau equations. Relative to the data-only approach, for scenarios of abundant data, scarce data, and even an incorrect FOM (i.e. erroneous parameter values), the hybrid approach yields substantially improved time-series predictions.

replace Optimized Weight Initialization on the Stiefel Manifold for Deep ReLU Neural Networks

Authors: Hyungu Lee, Taehyeong Kim, Hayoung Choi

Abstract: Stable and efficient training of ReLU networks with large depth is highly sensitive to weight initialization. Improper initialization can cause permanent neuron inactivation dying ReLU and exacerbate gradient instability as network depth increases. Methods such as He, Xavier, and orthogonal initialization preserve variance or promote approximate isometry. However, they do not necessarily regulate the pre-activation mean or control activation sparsity, and their effectiveness often diminishes in very deep architectures. This work introduces a semi-orthogonal initialization specifically optimized for ReLU by solving an optimization problem on the Stiefel manifold, thereby preserving scale and calibrating the pre-activation statistics from the outset. A family of closed-form solutions and an efficient sampling scheme are derived. Theoretical analysis at initialization shows that the proposed initialization mitigates dying ReLU, slows the decay of activation variance, and alleviates gradient vanishing, thereby stabilizing signal and gradient flow in deep architectures. Empirically, across MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, multiple tabular datasets, few-shot settings, and ReLU-family activations, the proposed initialization maintains stable performance as network depth increases, with particularly clear advantages in few-shot learning and large-depth settings.

replace Evaluating utility of clinical prediction models: A comparison of decision curve analysis and cost curves

Authors: Louise AC Millard, Matthew Sperrin, Richard M Martin, Peter A Flach

Abstract: Classification models typically predict a binary outcome and use one or more decision thresholds to produce a classification or inform a clinical decision. Decision curves and cost curves are model evaluation approaches that assess net benefit and loss across decision thresholds. In this paper we compare decision curves and cost curves, demonstrating that decision curves are closely related to a specific type of cost curve called a Brier curve. Users of both approaches set the classification threshold assuming calibrated model scores, using the relative clinical importance of correct positive and negative classifications. A given decision curve has a corresponding Brier curve such that reference lines common in each space can be included in either (e.g. the 'treat none' decision curve can be translated into an equivalent 'treat none' Brier curve). We suggest the upper envelope decision curve as a useful comparison to a model decision curve showing the maximum achievable net benefit through recalibration. We show that net benefit (used for decision curves) and Brier loss (used for Brier curves) will always choose the same model as optimal at any given threshold, but cannot be compared or aggregated across thresholds except under specific assumptions; doing so otherwise requires custom weightings.

replace Curriculum-Augmented GFlowNets For mRNA Sequence Generation

Authors: Aya Laajil, Abduragim Shtanchaev, Sajan Muhammad, Aya El Mir, Eric Moulines, Tiago Silva, Salem Lahlou

Abstract: We develop an approach for the de novo synthesis of messenger RNA (mRNA) sequences encoding target proteins. replace Wasserstein Robust Classification Error: Surrogate Loss Misalignment and Distributional Attacks

Authors: Bach C. Le, Tung V. Dao, Binh T. Nguyen, Hong T. M. Chu

Abstract: Wasserstein distributionally robust optimization (WDRO) provides a framework for evaluating model robustness through the worst-case expectation in the Wasserstein ball around a reference distribution. We prove that ordering hypotheses by increasing WDRO risk is equivalent to ordering them by decreasing robust accuracy if and only if the loss function is a positive multiple of the zero-one loss plus a label-dependent offset. For a class of loss functions including cross-entropy, we show that a hypothesis with perfect robust accuracy can conversely have a higher WDRO risk than another hypothesis with near zero robust accuracy. To evaluate robust accuracy directly, we develop Wasserstein distributional attacks (WDA), which add a weight generation stage after candidate support search to optimize the allocation of empirical mass under the Wasserstein constraint, rather than assigning each data point's full mass to one returned attack point as existing methods do. We further introduce a support-search algorithm for WDA that finds nearby misclassified inputs. On CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet, WDA using our search algorithm achieves the lowest adversarial accuracy in most tested settings. Our code is available at https://github.com/OLab-Repo/WDA.

replace Differential Privacy as a Perk: Federated Learning over Multiple-Access Fading Channels with a Multi-Antenna Base Station

Authors: Hao Liang, Haifeng Wen, Kaishun Wu, Khaled B. Letaief, Hong Xing

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) is a distributed learning paradigm that preserves privacy by eliminating the need to exchange raw data during training. In its prototypical edge instantiation with underlying wireless transmissions enabled by analog over-the-air computing (AirComp), referred to as \emph{over-the-air FL (AirFL)}, the inherent channel noise plays a unique role of \emph{frenemy} in the sense that it degrades training due to noisy global aggregation while providing a natural source of randomness for privacy-preserving mechanisms, formally quantified by \emph{differential privacy (DP)}. It remains, nevertheless, challenging to effectively harness such channel impairments, as prior arts, under assumptions of either simple channel models or restricted types of loss functions, mostly considering (local) DP enhancement with a single-round or non-convergent bound on privacy loss. In this paper, we study AirFL over multiple-access fading channels with a multi-antenna base station (BS) subject to user-level DP requirements. Despite a recent study, which claimed in similar settings that artificial noise (AN) must be injected to ensure DP in general, we demonstrate, on the contrary, that DP can be gained as a \emph{perk} even \emph{without} employing any AN. Specifically, we derive a novel bound on DP that converges under general bounded-domain assumptions on model parameters, along with a convergence bound with general smooth and non-convex loss functions. Next, we optimize over receive beamforming and power allocations to characterize the optimal convergence-privacy trade-offs, which also reveal explicit conditions in which DP is achievable without compromising training. Finally, our theoretical findings are validated by extensive numerical results.

replace A Likely Geometry of Generative Models

Authors: Frederik M\"obius Rygaard, Shen Zhu, Yinzhu Jin, S{\o}ren Hauberg, Tom Fletcher

Abstract: The geometry of generative models serves as the basis for interpolation, model inspection, and more. Unfortunately, most generative models lack a principal notion of geometry without restrictive assumptions on either the model or the data dimension. In this paper, we construct a general geometry compatible with different metrics and probability distributions to analyze generative models that do not require additional training. We consider curves analogous to geodesics constrained to a suitable data distribution aimed at targeting high-density regions learned by generative models. We formulate this as a (pseudo)-metric and prove that this corresponds to a Newtonian system on a Riemannian manifold. We show that shortest paths in our framework can be characterized by a system of ordinary differential equations, which locally corresponds to geodesics under a suitable Riemannian metric. Numerically, we derive a novel algorithm to efficiently compute shortest paths and generalized Fr\'echet means. Quantitatively, we show that curves using our metric traverse regions of higher density than baselines across a range of models and datasets.

Abstract: Edge AI must adapt to new classes and shifting data within a tight power budget, but online continual learning (learning from each sample before the next arrives) is where conventional processors are least efficient: each small update pays a full dispatch at full power. Abstract: Mixture-of-experts (MoE) models often exceed a single accelerator's memory capacity. Abstract: Predicting molecule-protein interactions (MPIs) is a fundamental task in computational biology, with crucial applications in drug discovery and molecular function annotation. replace When does Subgroup Balance Matter? Predicting Performance Sensitivity from Latent Separability

Authors: Anissa Alloula, Charles Jones, Zuzanna Wakefield-Skorniewska, Francesco Quinzan, Bart{\l}omiej Papie\.z

Abstract: Unequal representation of demographic groups in training data poses challenges to model generalisation across populations. Standard practice assumes that balancing subgroup representation always optimises performance. However, recent empirical results contradict this assumption: in some cases, imbalanced data distributions actually improve subgroup performance, and in others, subgroup performance is unaffected by the absence of an entire subgroup during training. We propose the latent separation hypothesis, which states that a fine-tuned model's dependence on subgroup representation is determined by the degree of separation between subgroups in the latent space of the pre-trained model. We formalise this hypothesis, provide theoretical analysis, validate it empirically across four vision and language datasets, and present preliminary evidence of a causal role through regularisation experiments. Finally, we show how quantitative analysis of latent subgroup separation can inform practical data collection and balancing decisions when fine-tuning foundation models.

replace OPTIMA: Optimal One-shot Pruning for LLMs via Quadratic Programming Reconstruction

Authors: Mohammad Mozaffari, Samuel Kushnir, Maryam Mehri Dehnavi, Amir Yazdanbakhsh

Abstract: Post-training pruning cuts the inference cost of large language models, yet it faces a trade-off: simple heuristics that zero weights are fast but degrade accuracy, while principled joint optimization methods recover accuracy but are computationally infeasible at modern scale. One-shot methods such as SparseGPT sit in between and apply efficient but approximate weight updates. To close this gap, we introduce OPTIMA, a practical one-shot post-training pruning method that balances accuracy and scalability. OPTIMA casts layer-wise weight reconstruction after mask selection as independent, column-wise quadratic programs (QPs) that share one Hessian per weight matrix. Solving these QPs yields, at solver convergence, the per-column minimizer of the layer-wise reconstruction objective under the shared Hessian, and the shared Hessian makes the QPs easy to batch on accelerators. We implement an accelerator-friendly QP solver that accumulates one Hessian per weight matrix and solves many small QPs in parallel with a convergence certificate per column, enabling one-shot post-training pruning at scale on a single accelerator without fine-tuning. OPTIMA integrates with existing mask selectors and improves average zero-shot accuracy in 40 of 49 (model, mask selector, sparsity) settings across the LLaMA and Gemma families, with mean gains of +0.61, +0.56, and +1.01 points at 50% unstructured, 2:4, and 60% unstructured sparsity, and up to +3.97 points at 60% sparsity, while matching ADMM-based exact reconstruction on its own masks. On an NVIDIA H100, OPTIMA prunes an 8B-parameter transformer end-to-end in 40 hours and 60 GB of peak GPU memory. This shows that principled column-wise QP reconstruction is a practical alternative to heuristic weight updates in one-shot post-training pruning.

replace Robust Residual Correction via Selective Deployment for Time Series Forecasting

Authors: Jianxiang Xie, Yuncheng Hua, Mingyue Cheng, Flora D. Salim, Hao Xue

Abstract: Post-hoc residual correction can reduce forecasting error, but always-on updates often fail to generalize: when residual signals are weak or noisy, expressive correctors can overfit and even increase the overall forecasting errors. We introduce CRC, a robust residual correction framework that couples a hybrid corrector with a validation-calibrated selective deployment rule. CRC proposes corrections via a conservative ridge "floor" plus conditional nonlinear refinement, but deploys them only when held-out evidence indicates net error reduction over a frozen baseline forecaster. CRC treats when to revise a forecast as a validation-calibrated deployment decision, not only how to fit residuals. Across standard long-sequence benchmarks and multiple backbones, CRC improves horizon-averaged MSE and MAE in most dataset-backbone pairs, with particularly large gains on complex datasets (e.g., over 20% relative MSE reduction on Traffic). We additionally report the non-degradation rate (NDR) as a stability diagnostic of harmful updates and ablate the validation-calibrated "firewall" that stabilizes MSE gains.

replace Activation Sensitivity as a Unifying Principle for Post-Training Quantization

Authors: Bruce Changlong Xu

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) methods for large language models compress pretrained weights to low bit-widths without retraining by optimizing layer-local proxy objectives. Activation-aware scaling methods such as AWQ protect input channels with large activation norms, whereas second-order error-compensation methods such as GPTQ redistribute rounding error using the inverse of the unweighted input covariance matrix. We show that both paradigms -- along with diagonal Fisher quantization, block reconstruction, and Hadamard rotation methods -- are special cases of minimizing a single loss-derived quadratic form governed by gradient-weighted activation sensitivity. From a Gauss-Newton expansion of the task loss at a linear layer $Y = X W^\top$, we prove that under isotropic output curvature the expected excess loss from weight perturbation $\Delta W$ reduces to $\frac{1}{2 d_{\mathrm{out}}}\operatorname{Tr}(\Delta W H_D \Delta W^\top)$, where $H_D = \frac{1}{n} X^\top D X$ weights each calibration token's outer product by its squared output-gradient norm $D_{tt} = \|\nabla_{y_t}\mathcal{L}\|_2^2$. Within this framework, AWQ corresponds to a diagonal approximation of $H_D$ with uniform token weights ($D = I_n$) optimized via channel scaling, while GPTQ corresponds to Cholesky error compensation on $H_D$ under $D = I_n$. We derive closed-form optimal scaling exponents for diagonal sensitivity protection, formulate sensitivity-weighted Cholesky quantization (SAQ-GPTQ) on $H_D$, and prove bounds on token-gradient heterogeneity bias, cross-layer error amplification across residual blocks, and finite-sample calibration concentration. Controlled numerical simulations confirm that under token-gradient heterogeneity, replacing $X^\top X$ with $X^\top D X$ preserves $>99\%$ top-channel saliency recall and reduces INT3/INT4 excess loss by roughly $3\times$ over unweighted GPTQ.

replace Optimal L2 Regularization in High-dimensional Continual Linear Regression

Authors: Gilad Karpel, Edward Moroshko, Ran Levinstein, Ron Meir, Daniel Soudry, Itay Evron

Abstract: We study generalization in an overparameterized continual linear regression setting, where a model is trained with L2 (isotropic) regularization across a sequence of tasks. We derive a closed-form expression for the expected generalization loss in the high-dimensional regime that holds for arbitrary linear teachers. We demonstrate that isotropic regularization mitigates label noise under both single-teacher and multiple i.i.d. teacher settings, whereas prior work accommodating multiple teachers either did not employ regularization or used memory-demanding methods. Furthermore, we prove that the optimal fixed regularization strength scales nearly linearly with the number of tasks $T$, specifically as $T/\ln T$. To our knowledge, this is the first such result in theoretical continual learning. Finally, we validate our theoretical findings through experiments on linear regression and neural networks, illustrating how this scaling law affects generalization and offering a practical recipe for the design of continual learning systems.

replace Expected Return Causes Outcome-Level Mode Collapse in Reinforcement Learning and How to Fix It with Inverse Probability Scaling

Authors: Abhijeet Sinha, Sundari Elango, Dianbo Liu

Abstract: Many reinforcement learning (RL) problems admit multiple terminal solutions of comparable quality, where the goal is not to identify a single optimum but to represent a diverse set of high-quality outcomes. Nevertheless, policies trained by standard expected return maximization routinely collapse onto a small subset of outcomes, a phenomenon commonly attributed to insufficient exploration or weak regularization. We show that this explanation is incomplete: outcome level mode collapse is a structural consequence of the expected-return objective itself. Under idealized learning dynamics, the log-probability ratio between any two outcomes evolves linearly in their reward difference, implying exponential ratio divergence and inevitable collapse independent of the exploration strategy, entropy regularization, or optimization algorithm. We identify the source of this pathology as the probability multiplier inside the expectation and propose a minimal correction: inverse probability scaling, which removes outcome-frequency amplification from the learning signal, fundamentally changes the learning dynamics, and provably yields reward-proportional terminal distributions, preventing collapse in multimodal settings. We instantiate this principle in Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) as a drop-in modification, IPS-GRPO, requiring no auxiliary models or architectural changes. Across different reasoning and molecular generation tasks, IPS-GRPO consistently reduces outcome-level mode collapse while matching or exceeding baseline performance, suggesting that correcting the objective rather than adding exploration heuristics is key to reliable multimodal policy optimization.

replace TTCS: Test-Time Curriculum Synthesis for Self-Evolving

Authors: Chengyi Yang, Zhishang Xiang, Yunbo Tang, Zongpei Teng, Chengsong Huang, Fei Long, Yuhan Liu, Jinsong Su

Abstract: Test-Time Training offers a promising way to improve the reasoning ability of large language models (LLMs) by adapting the model using only the test questions. However, existing methods struggle with difficult reasoning problems for two reasons: raw test questions are often too difficult to yield high-quality pseudo-labels, and the limited size of test sets makes continuous online updates prone to instability. To address these limitations, we propose TTCS, a co-evolving test-time training framework. Specifically, TTCS initializes two policies from the same pretrained model: a question synthesizer and a reasoning solver. These policies evolve through iterative optimization: the synthesizer generates progressively challenging question variants conditioned on the test questions, creating a structured curriculum tailored to the solver's current capability, while the solver updates itself using self-consistency rewards computed from multiple sampled responses on both original test and synthetic questions. Crucially, the solver's feedback guides the synthesizer to generate questions aligned with the model's current capability, and the generated question variants in turn stabilize the solver's test-time training. Experiments show that TTCS consistently strengthens the reasoning ability on challenging mathematical benchmarks and transfers to general-domain tasks across different LLM backbones, highlighting a scalable path towards dynamically constructing test-time curricula for self-evolving. Our code and implementation details are available at https://github.com/XMUDeepLIT/TTCS.
