replace Federated learning with heavy-tailed gradient noise and communication noise: a variance-reduction based algorithm

Authors: Shengchao Zhao, Yongchao Liu

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) is an emerging distributed machine learning paradigm that enables local devices to jointly train a global model while keeping data decentralized and private. We propose a variance-reduction based algorithm, VRA-FedSGD, for FL in the presence of heavy-tailed gradient noise and communication noise, where these noises are prevalent in large-scale machine learning over wireless networks and Internet of Things deployments. VRA-FedSGD employs a momentum variance reduction technique together with a nonlinear mapping to mitigate heavy-tailed gradient noise, and uses a variance-reduced aggregation mechanism to suppress heavy-tailed communication noise. In the mean sense, VRA-FedSGD achieves a convergence rate of {\small$\mathcal{O}\left(K^{-(p-1)/(2p-1)}\right)$} for nonconvex objective functions, where $p$ is the tail index of heavy-tailed noise. In the almost sure sense, VRA-FedSGD achieves a convergence rate of $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(K^{-(1-1/(p-\epsilon))}\right)$ for strongly convex objective functions, where $\epsilon$ is an arbitrarily small constant. Simulated experiments on a logistic regression problem with real-world data verify the effectiveness of VRA-FedSGD.

replace Data-Driven Soft Labeling Scales DNA Read Classification to Whole-Body Cell-Type Deconvolution

Authors: Dmytro Rizdvanetskyi, Nathan Roos, Pavlo Lutsik

Abstract: Cell-type deconvolution, the task of estimating the proportions of constituent cell types in a heterogeneous biological sample, is a core problem in computational biology. Methods that rely on epigenetic marks such as DNA methylation typically operate on aggregated methylation estimates, discarding the pattern-level information carried by individual DNA reads. Existing read-level approaches that exploit this information are scarce, and all remain restricted to few-class settings; scaling them further is an open problem because, at scale, non-discriminative reads dominate and hard labels conflict with the many-to-many mapping between methylation patterns and cell types, preventing classifier convergence. To overcome this, we propose data-driven soft labels that estimate the conditional cell-type distribution for each read, and integrate this scheme into $Syto$, a new modular framework for read-level classification-based deconvolution. On a whole-body atlas of 39 human cell types, $Syto$ reduces MSE by 3.7$\times$ over the best examined baseline, with gains transferring to an out-of-distribution dataset spanning 16 tissues. $Syto$ lays the foundation for modeling increasingly large cell-type panels, with improved applications in biology and healthcare. The proposed soft-labeling scheme is further translatable to any setting with a many-to-many signal-to-label mapping.

replace SafeExplorer: An Unbiased Mixed-Policy Gradient for Reinforcement Learning with Recovery Interventions

Authors: Elham Daneshmand, Majid Khadiv, Glen Berseth, Hsiu-Chin Lin

Abstract: Training agents directly on the task they will be deployed on often leads to the best policy performance, but training on physical robots can cause damage through falls, so minimizing training falls is critical. A standard mitigation hands control to a separate recovery policy whenever the agent leaves a designer-specified safe region. The resulting mixed-policy rollouts bias the on-policy update, and importance sampling cannot remove this bias when the recovery policy is deterministic. We propose SafeExplorer, a drop-in modification of proximal policy optimization (PPO) built on an unbiased policy-gradient estimator of the mixed-policy return. The estimator uses the score function only inside the safe region and never evaluates the recovery policy's density, so deterministic recovery policies pose no problem. On final reward, the estimator matches or outperforms truncated importance sampling, which is itself biased for that same gradient. SafeExplorer also speeds up learning near the safe-region boundary with a closed-form critic target at recovery-triggering states when dynamics and recovery are deterministic, and an imitation loss that copies recovery actions only from successful recoveries. On three simulated locomotion environments with ten seeds each, and given an already available recovery controller, SafeExplorer incurs $255\times$, $43\times$, and $21\times$ fewer training falls than standard PPO on HalfCheetah, Ant, and Unitree Go1. SafeExplorer also matches or exceeds the final reward of PPO. On Ant, where 8.7% of recovery interventions end in a fall, over $40\times$ the HalfCheetah rate, SafeExplorer reaches the success threshold on every seed and none of the five baselines reaches the threshold on any seed.

replace DiffEEG: A Self-Supervised Denoising Diffusion Model for Learning EEG seizure-related Representations

Authors: Abdulkader Helwan, Lina Abou-Abbas, Hussein El Amouri, Belkacem Chikhaoui, Khadidja Henni

Abstract: Deep learning for EEG-based seizure detection faces critical challenges: severe annotation scarcity and extreme class imbalance, where ictal events comprise less than 10\% of clinical recordings. We present DiffEEG, a 9.6M-parameter self-supervised foundation model that addresses both limitations through denoising diffusion pre-training and reinforcement learning (RL)-based fine-tuning. Pre-trained on 1.3M unlabeled segments from the Temple University Hospital Seizure Corpus (TUHSZ), DiffEEG learns generic neural representations via a 1D U-Net with multi-head self-attention. For downstream adaptation, a reinforced decision layer employs policy gradient optimization to directly maximize F1-score, prioritizing sensitivity to rare seizure events over overall accuracy. Under strict patient-wise evaluation (279 patients, Leave-One-Fold-Out), DiffEEG achieves 61\% accuracy and 59\% F1 for 4-class seizure subtyping, and 81\% accuracy with 85\% weighted F1 for binary detection, maintaining clinically viable seizure recall (59\%) despite extreme imbalance (6.7\% prevalence). Segment-level evaluation establishes an upper bound of 97.6\% accuracy, confirming strong architectural capacity. DiffEEG demonstrates that diffusion-based pre-training combined with metric-aware reinforcement learning enables clinically deployable seizure monitoring with minimal labeled data requirements.

replace Using Data-Derived Priors to Guide CNN Architecture Design for NIR Chemometrics

Authors: D\'ario Passos

Abstract: Convolutional neural networks (CNN) for near-infrared (NIR) chemometrics are often designed using generic architectural rules, although spectral datasets differ in sampling, smoothness, redundancy, and sample size. We tested whether these properties can provide empirical priors for CNN design. Across 25 NIR regression tasks, we computed descriptors of dataset size, spectral length and spacing, entropy, intrinsic rank, autocorrelation, and wavelet-scale structure. Two interpretable 1D-CNN scaffolds (a minimal single-convolution model and an extended shallow model with optional branching, dilation, etc) were optimized using five-fold cross-validated Bayesian hyperparameter optimization (HPO). Relationships extracted from near-optimal trials were converted into warm-start heuristics and evaluated directly and through leave-one-dataset-out (LODO) validation. The clearest relationships involved convolutional receptive fields. In the minimal CNN, the preferred kernel fraction decreased with spectral entropy and intrinsic rank, increased with the wavelet energy-support fraction, and the learning rate tended to decrease with training-set size. Direct and LODO heuristics were competitive with HPO, with median test-RMSE ratios of 0.953 and 1.017, respectively. The extended CNN showed similar but less transferable structure across branch usage, dilation, dropout, filter counts, and receptive-field choices. Ten stochastic refits showed seed sensitivity comparable to that of HPO-selected configurations. In a separate experiment, joint preprocessing and CNN HPO outperformed standardized-spectra HPO in 19 of 25 tasks, although gains were dataset-dependent. These results show that spectral descriptors can provide practical CNN design priors, guiding shallow NIR models toward plausible hyperparameter regions before target-specific tuning

replace FEAST: Federated Shared-Space Training for Resource-Heterogeneous Clients

Authors: Bostan Khan, Masoud Daneshtalab

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) must serve devices with varying computational capabilities. A fixed model cannot suit all devices, while training one model per deployment limit is costly. Federated supernet training instead learns one elastic model with differently sized subnetworks, then deploys a suitable one to each device. When client inference budgets differ, however, parameters exclusive to high-cost subnetworks are reachable by fewer clients. We propose FEAST, a federated shared-space training framework that counters this imbalance by jointly training multiple subnetworks within each client's limit. Budget-tailored sub-supernet routing sends only the relevant supernet portion, and sparse aggregation merges the returned parameter slices. The trained supernet directly serves the subnetworks used during federation and supports post-hoc extraction of additional subnetworks without federated retraining. We further show that independently assigning clients' training-data volumes and inference budgets can distort accuracy--inference-cost comparisons in heterogeneous FL simulations, and introduce a one-parameter $\gamma$-allocation protocol to control this coupling. In our experimental setup, the SuperFedNAS and DeepFedNAS supernet training procedures remain near chance at 25M and reach at most $17.09\%$ at $596$M inference MACs; FEAST reaches $71.06\%$ at $596$M, $2.4$ points above the strongest model-heterogeneous weight-sharing baseline at its largest tier. Across CIFAR-100, CINIC-10, and TinyImageNet-200, FEAST achieves the highest population-averaged accuracy among the evaluated weight-sharing methods when each client receives its largest affordable subnetwork. Sub-supernet routing reduces aggregate model-parameter traffic by $6.8\times$ relative to full-supernet transmission.

replace Generalized Convexity and Smoothness via Conjugate Duality, with Applications to Deep Network Training

Authors: Binchuan Qi

Abstract: Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) and its variants train deep neural networks (DNNs) effectively in practice, yet classical optimization theory does not fully explain this success: conventional analyses rest on differentiability, convexity, and smoothness, assumptions that DNN objectives routinely violate. We close this gap by generalizing convexity and smoothness, through Legendre functions and convex conjugation, into a unified optimization framework for DNN training. We introduce \(\mathcal{H}(\psi)\)-convexity and \(\mathcal{H}(\Psi)\)-smoothness, a single formalism that encompasses convex and non-convex as well as smooth and non-smooth objectives and reveals a natural duality between generalized convexity and smoothness. Building on these notions, we derive generalized gradient descent (GD) and generalized SGD via convex conjugation. We prove that generalized GD attains an optimal learning rate of exactly \(1\), and we establish rigorous gradient-energy-based convergence rates for both optimizers. We further recast DNN training as a composite optimization problem and show that its convergence hinges on jointly reducing the gradient energy and controlling the extreme eigenvalues of the structure matrix; a model capacity risk quantifies the residual error that no optimizer can remove. Experiments on six datasets (MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, TREC, and SST-2) and on convolutional, residual, and Transformer architectures, with five loss functions, are consistent with the predicted training dynamics.

replace The Complexity Kink: A Prompt-Side Structural Complexity Index for Code-Generation Reliability

Authors: Michael Hernandez, Tian Zhao

Abstract: Measuring complexity from a model's own code is biased by failure: a hard prompt can produce a short, broken program that looks simple. We instead score each prompt's structure on six dimensions from the prompt alone, never from generated code or test results. We draw 5,000 Python prompts across six bands of a preliminary single-rater score, and four out-of-panel LLM judges rescore every prompt (composite ICC(2,1) = 0.872). Twenty-one models answer every prompt (105,000 generations), and an independent LLM auditor, checked against known-answer cases, reviews each unit-test verdict. Averaged over models, pass rate has a nonmonotone breakpoint at composite 14.0: 83.4% at or below, 90.1% above. That jump is not a law of difficulty: with task-type fixed effects the breakpoint stays at 14.0 but the adjusted jump shrinks from 6.8 to 0.4 points, and neither of the benchmark's two prompt sources reproduces it on its own. Nineteen models pass more often above their own breakpoint and two less often; one of those declines comes from API calls that returned nothing. In post hoc follow-up, Kimi K2.5 shows the clearest decline, at 14.25, under a prompt-source control and within the later source alone. Among failed generations with measurable output complexity, 25.0% pair a prompt composite above 8 with output complexity of at most 10, twice the share among passing ones. The pooled breakpoint moves to 11.25 on raw test outcomes. Human agreement with the index is moderate, paraphrase and cross-language rescoring preserve its ordering, and overidentification tests reject the six dimensions as instruments, so we make no causal claim. The contribution is a prompt-side way to locate where each model's pass rate changes and to test how much that depends on the prompt mix and on how success is scored.

replace AURA: Angular Update Rate Adaptation for training complex-valued neural networks

Authors: Enrico Ballini, Allan Peter Engsig-Karup, Tito Andriollo

Abstract: Complex-valued neural networks (CVNNs) are increasingly adopted for complex-valued data; however, they are often trained with first-order optimizers inherited from the real-valued case. The efficiency of these methods depends largely on the step size, and their step-size rules ignore the angular information available in the complex plane. We address step-size adaptation in the complex domain by introducing AURA (Angular Update Rate Adaptation), a per-parameter step-size adaptation that can be added on top of any first-order optimizer, and removed from it, without altering its update direction. AURA measures the agreement between consecutive updates of each complex parameter, in length, alignment, and sense of rotation, and enlarges the step when they are consistent and reduces it when they are not. It requires no additional gradient evaluations and only inexpensive vector operations per step. We combine AURA with Adam and Muon and compare the resulting methods with well-known first-order optimizers on four test cases of increasing complexity, ranging from the approximation of scalar complex functions to physics-informed training. Fully connected neural networks are used throughout this work. All hyperparameters other than the step size are held fixed across test cases; for one case, we also tune the hyperparameters of each optimizer under the same budget. Our empirical tests show that AURA improves the convergence of its base optimizer in most cases with a small per-step overhead, and we identify the conditions under which it fails to do so.

replace Response-state Learning for Transferable Vibrational Spectroscopic Characterization with Electron Prior

Authors: Zetong Li, Zhuosong Xie, Hengyu Fan, Jiaao Yu, Qiyao Hua, Zheng Lu, Liming Xu, Juanni Wu, Honglin Li

Abstract: Vibrational spectral prediction can become inaccurate when localized stereoelectronic environments perturb intermediate response states and high-risk response units dominate characteristic spectral fingerprints, making prediction across external chemical space difficult. SO(3) Equivariant Neural Kalman Networks (SENK) form a response-state cascade that combines an equivariant transformer backbone for Hessian, dipole-derivative and polarizability-derivative learning, an Equivariant Neural Kalman bridge for state-dependent refinement and reliability sensing, and an NBO-informed electronic-prior pathway coupling consistency regularization with bounded, branch-specific guided spectral calibration. SENK outperforms DetaNet on QM9S and QMe14S while preserving full-spectrum IR and Raman fidelity from small molecules to drug-like systems. SENK remains stable and selectively improves spectrally sensitive features in biomolecular systems with complex stereoelectronic effects. It therefore integrates tensor prediction, reliability diagnosis and physics-informed calibration, supporting transferable vibrational spectroscopy from molecular systems to functional molecular materials.

replace Marginal Calibration Masks Generator Differences in Held-Out Evaluation of Multivariate Time Series, with an Application to ERA5 Weather Sequences

Authors: Mustafa \"Ozayta\c{c}, \"Ozge Karada\u{g} Ata\c{s}

Abstract: Generative models for multivariate time series are routinely evaluated with pooled distributional metrics computed after marginal calibration. We show that, under a held-out protocol, the calibrated Kolmogorov-Smirnov (KS) distance differs from a real-versus-real floor by at most the distance of the calibrated sample to the training block, so that it carries little information about the generator. We audit a three-discriminator GAN with evolutionary weight adaptation (StatD2GAN) against re-implemented RCGAN and TimeGAN baselines on ERA5 weather sequences under a pre-specified protocol: the final two calendar years are held out behind a 168 hour embargo, calibration is fitted on the training block only, and the decision rules were frozen in advance. Evidence comes from 25 matched (location, seed) pairs across five locations, tested with Holm-corrected Wilcoxon signed-rank tests. First, isotonic calibration drives KS to a median of 1.019 times a per-location noise-and-shift floor, and neither baseline is distinguishable from StatD2GAN on it. Second, the sorted-representation discriminator is the only component whose removal significantly degrades cross-variable dependence (Kendall tau MAE +0.116, Holm p = 0.002); a rank-transformed variant also degrades it, consistent with quantile supervision rather than copula matching. Third, calibration raises physical constraint violations; projection removes them, but the largest marginal change (0.0110, Lhasa) exceeds the pre-specified tolerance in three locations. Fourth, the re-implemented TimeGAN is significantly better than StatD2GAN on Kendall tau and ACF MAE, RCGAN is worse on Kendall tau MAE and constraint violations, and the pre-specified collapse criterion was not met. We recommend floor-referenced marginal evaluation, matched-pair testing, and dependence and sequence-level metrics.

replace QwenGyre: An Elastic Reinforcement Learning Framework for Training xLong-Horizon Agents

Authors: Weiqi Wang, Yuxin Zhou, Mouxiang Chen, Siyuan Zhang, Yi Zhang, Yuyan Luo, Zhiyu Yin, Chencan Wu, Jiemin Jiang, Wentao Yao, Chujie Zheng, JianWei Zhang

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly undertake extreme-long (xlong) horizon tasks, where a single execution can span hours, hundreds of model--environment interactions, and nearly 1M tokens per rollout. Applying online reinforcement learning (RL) to such executions poses two fundamental challenges: (1) severe execution variance and prolonged rollout delays cause massive GPU idling; and (2) complex non-linear branching generates massive trajectory redundancy, crippling training efficiency. To address these, we presents QwenGyre, an end-to-end framework for xlong-horizon online RL. QwenGyre elastically reallocates GPUs between rollout and training without interrupting live executions, while its trajectory processor reconstructs branching histories, scores partial progress, and deduplicates redundant paths to bound training costs. Scaled to our flagship model, Qwen~3.8 2.4T, with 700K tokens per rollout, QwenGyre yields a 6.0% absolute gain on NL2RepoBench (52.5% $\to$ 58.5%) in 48 steps. Across our evaluations on diverse domains of training datasets, QwenGyre delivers up to $1.85\times$ and $1.78\times$ speedups over Colocate and Async, respectively.

replace Binarization Flattens the Score Space

Authors: Jacob Cole

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) judges are often used as rewards to train policies on objectives that deterministic verifiers cannot capture. However, these rewards are often collapsed to pass/fail ({0, 1}), which reports the verdict but not how well a response met each criterion. We model each pass/fail verdict as a score on an unreported scale, compared with one cutoff. A stretch of that scale moves every score proportionally toward or away from the cutoff, but never across it, so no verdict changes. A policy is therefore free to apply any stretch without changing anything the panel reports. Under a joint-Gaussian model, a third grade adds a second threshold and removes this affine stretch ambiguity. On MATH and SciBench outputs from one seven-criterion judge, all 14 constructed criterionwise stretches were invisible after binarization but visible with three grades. At $n=1{,}024$, a test given both population laws had at least 96.5% power at a $1.5\times$ stress. Retaining grades closes one blind spot created by binarization, but verdicts alone remain insufficient as some changes are still indistinguishable from genuine improvement. These include arbitrary within-grade changes and fixed-covariance, loading-aligned mean shifts -- the signature of a sycophancy-shaped lift the panel reads as competence. The shared-factor reference approximation fit MATH and SciBench but not HealthBench, delineating its empirical scope. We recommend keeping at least three grades (for example, asking the judge whether each criterion is fully, partially, or not met and rewarding {0, 0.5, 1}), and externally validating gains along the remaining direction, which no finer scale removes.

replace ROSS: Relearning from Self-Generated Rollouts through Selective Supervision

Authors: Zhiwei Zhang, Huayu Deng, Fei Zhao, Jiayan Fu, Bin Liang, Kam-Fai Wong, Mu Chuan

Abstract: Large language model post-training generates self-generated rollouts through reinforcement learning and on-policy distillation, yet this experience is often treated as stale once the policy advances. Historical rollouts can remain compatible with a later policy while preserving behaviors that the policy no longer expresses reliably. However, they may also contain mistakes, abandoned attempts, and redundant actions that should not be imitated, motivating finer-grained selective supervision. We introduce ROSS (Relearning from Self-Generated Rollouts through Selective Supervision), which preserves the full historical trajectory as context while applying loss only to selected model-generated continuations. Across domain-specific reinforcement learning, multi-teacher on-policy distillation, and agentic reinforcement learning, ROSS consistently improves upstream checkpoints and outperforms baselines across mathematics, code generation, instruction following, and software engineering. On Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, ROSS improves the six-benchmark MOPD average from 58.40% to 62.20% and SWE-bench Verified from 64.20% to 68.40%. These results show that self-rollout training leaves behind reusable behavioral experience that can yield further gains through offline supervised fine-tuning (SFT), without additional policy rollouts.

replace $S^3$: Spectral Null-Space Swap Makes Reasoning Models Efficient

Authors: Hongbo Ma, Sansheng Cao, Jiajun Fan, Bangji Yang, Ge Liu

Abstract: LLMs trained with Chain-of-thought excel in reasoning capability, but often come with excessive token cost. We find that the core of reasoning capacity lies in the Thinking model's weight component within the null space of a projection defined by the corresponding Non-thinking model's dominant singular directions, and removing the subspace component can largely improve reasoning efficiency without hurting the accuracy gained during thinking-mode post-training. Unlike existing efforts that mostly operate within the dominant subspace, we are the first to unveil the critical role of the null space and harness it for model optimization. Motivated by this finding, we propose Spectral Null-Space Swap ($S^3$), a training-free composition of paired Non-thinking and Thinking checkpoints. Our method keeps the Non-thinking model inside its own dominant subspace and takes the Thinking checkpoint outside it, improving reasoning efficiency while maintaining accuracy. We extensively evaluate $S^3$ on 2B-30B dense and mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures spanning 28 evaluation environments across mathematical, multimodal, and audio reasoning domains. $S^3$ establishes new empirical Pareto Frontiers among training-free model composition strategies: across all settings, it reduces inference token overhead by an average of 27.4% compared to full Thinking models while simultaneously improving overall task accuracy by 1.0 percentage point (e.g., yielding +8.3% accuracy on HMMT25 alongside a 33.0% token speedup). We further use attention entropy for explanation and find that the retained component produces more concentrated attention, and we use a simplified analytical model about optimization to demonstrate why null-space can effectively reduce attention entropy, thereby improving the efficiency of reasoning.

replace Geometry-Dependent Online and Offline Approximation for Non-Monotone DR-Submodular Maximization

Authors: Vaneet Aggarwal, Yiyang Lu

Abstract: We study online and offline maximization of nonnegative, non-monotone DR-submodular functions over compact convex down-closed sets $K\subseteq[0,1]^d$. A supplied feasible diagonal point $\zeta {\bf 1}\in K$ gives an upper-linearizable comparison with coefficient $\underline\alpha(\zeta)=\tfrac12-(1-2\zeta)_+^2/[2(3-2\zeta)^2]$. This gives $4/9$ at $\zeta=0$ and $O(\sqrt T)$ expected online approximate regret with one gradient query and one projection per round, including stochastic gradients. The same coefficient admits $O(T^{3/4})$ regret with one noisy value query, or $O(\sqrt T)$ with two exact value queries. All objective queries lie in the cube. Our analytical offline upper bound at $\zeta=0$ improves the bound $2(e^{-1/2}-e^{-1})$ established in 2011, even with exact values and full gradients. Two constructions extend it to positive diagonal levels; one has a finite representation below $1/8$ and a piecewise rational formula above it. Online-to-batch conversion gives offline approximation and sample complexity, while a constant-objective reduction transfers the upper bound to online optimization. Both general-class coefficients equal $1/2$ for $\zeta\ge1/2$, and their deficits below this level have quadratic order. Analytic polynomial comparisons give coefficient $1/2$ for quadratics and $\max\{8/17,2\sqrt{3-4r}/(1+\sqrt{3-4r})^2\}$ for cubics, where $r=\min\{\zeta,1/2\}$, with online and offline query implementations. Finally, for discrete submodular maximization, multilinear extension and established rounding give $\underline\alpha(\zeta)-\varepsilon$ under one matroid constraint and $4/9-\varepsilon$ under a fixed number of knapsack constraints.

replace Exploring More, Reasoning Better: Stepwise Risk-Sensitive GRPO for Diffusion Language Models

Authors: Yue Yu, Bowen Zuo, David Crandall, Yinglun Zhu, Dongruo Zhou

Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) generate text by denoising a sequence or successive blocks, allowing several tokens to be revealed in parallel. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) reuses terminal feedback across these decisions, even as their conditioning context changes. We propose stepwise risk-sensitive GRPO (StepRS-GRPO), which varies the risk coefficient of the group-advantage transformation across denoising states while retaining the underlying trainer. For binary rewards, we show that this transformation is exactly a prompt- and state-dependent rescaling of centered outcome advantages. A capability-based calibration suggests a coefficient scale, while endpoint and interpolation ablations guide schedule selection. Across multiple dLLM backbones and mathematical reasoning benchmarks, StepRS-GRPO improves both pass@1 accuracy and pass@k coverage over centered GRPO, while increasing answer diversity. In our ablation studies, mass-matched controls support the contributions of state allocation and schedule direction, and the gains persist after matching the root mean square (RMS) of the advantages to that of centered GRPO. Reasoning-trace diagnostics further show that the diversity gains from StepRS-GRPO extend beyond final-answer strings.

replace FERPO: Forward Entropy-Regularized Policy Optimization

Authors: Sebastian Sanokowski, Alireza Sarmadi, Majid Khadiv

Abstract: Several state-of-the-art methods for online reinforcement learning in continuous control improve policies using action gradients of a learned critic. However, critics are typically trained to predict returns, and accurate value predictions do not necessarily yield accurate action derivatives, potentially leading to unreliable policy updates. We propose Forward Entropy-Regularized Policy Optimization (FERPO), an on-policy maximum entropy reinforcement learning algorithm that performs policy improvement using critic values without differentiating the critic with respect to actions. FERPO derives an optimal target action distribution from a policy-improvement objective regularized by entropy and Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence. We then fit the actor to this target by minimizing a forward-KL objective, estimated using self-normalized importance sampling (SNIS) with actions drawn from the rollout policy. By limiting the target distribution's deviation from the rollout policy, the KL regularization helps keep these importance weights well behaved. In contrast to reverse-KL objectives, which can favor a subset of the target distribution's modes, the forward-KL objective encourages coverage of multiple high-value modes and thereby promotes exploration. Experiments and ablations on MuJoCo Playground and ManiSkill show competitive performance and sample-efficiency gains. Computational benchmarks also demonstrate faster actor updates than Relative Entropy Pathwise Policy Optimization (REPPO).

replace TreeWalker: Partial Evaluation for Grouped Tree-Ensemble Inference

Authors: Durmus Karatay, Richard Newman

Abstract: Many prediction tasks evaluate a trained tree ensemble on groups of rows that share feature values: discrete-time survival models expand each patient into $G$ time steps, click-through-rate models score every item in a search session, and scenario analyses vary a few inputs while holding the rest fixed. Standard inference treats each row independently and ignores this input-side correlation. We present TreeWalker, which instantiates partial evaluation for grouped tree-ensemble inference: constant features are static, varying features dynamic. TreeWalker walks each tree once per group, partitions a row bitmask at varying splits, and skips empty subtrees via an unsplit shortcut. It reads standard LightGBM and XGBoost models; training is unchanged. We prove a structural work decomposition: per-tree work splits into the constant-projected subtree size $|T_c|$, $G$ leaf writes, and a predicate-mask provisioning cost $Q$. For the trace evaluator, per-row work approaches $(d_v+1)/(d+1)$ as $G \to \infty$, giving asymptotic speedup $1/(1-\bar\rho_G)$. On Intel, TreeWalker is ${\sim}3\times$ faster than a row-independent traversal at the reference model size ($T{=}500$, $L{=}8$; $G{=}16$ for SUPPORT/FLCHAIN and empirical groups for Expedia) and $7.7$--$9.1\times$ faster at $G{=}128$ on the survival datasets; on Arm, $2.5$--$2.9\times$ and $5.5$--$6.0\times$. On a scenario-analysis benchmark it is faster in all $16$ configurations on both machines. For f64 models, outputs match treelite GTIL up to tree-ordering roundoff; for f32 models, TreeWalker's f64 leaf accumulation is closer than native f32 to an exactly rounded reference on every audited row.

replace EDISCO: Equivariant DIScrete Diffusion for Euclidean Combinatorial Optimization

Authors: Ruogu Chen, Jie Han

Abstract: Euclidean combinatorial optimization problems (ECOPs), such as the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) and Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP), possess inherent symmetries under the two-dimensional Euclidean group E(2), including rotations, reflections, and translations. Existing learning-based methods, including recent diffusion-based methods, rely on data augmentation or regularization to approximate E(2)-equivariance. This paper presents EDISCO, the first discrete diffusion model for ECOPs with exact E(2)-invariant generative distributions over node-index solutions. EDISCO introduces an E(2)-equivariant edge-score network coupled with a categorical continuous-time Markov chain over discrete edge variables, and exact posterior sampling provides efficient multi-step inference. This design gives EDISCO a local geometric inductive bias: edge neighborhoods with the same relative geometry and combinatorial context are represented consistently regardless of absolute position or orientation, making learning more efficient and inference more robust than non-equivariant methods. EDISCO outperforms previous learning-based state-of-the-art solvers on synthetic TSP from 100 to 10000 nodes and CVRP from 50 to 2000 customers, while using only 33-50% of the training instances. Trained only on uniform synthetic data, EDISCO also outperforms competing learning-based baselines under spatial distribution shift and CVRP constraint-tightness shift. Code is available at https://github.com/ValleyC/EDISCO.

URLs: https://github.com/ValleyC/EDISCO.

replace Ranking Bandits for Carousel Interfaces with Observable Browsing Depth

Authors: Takuma Yasuda, Atsuyoshi Nakamura, Dai Hai Nguyen

Abstract: Carousel interfaces allow a recommender system to directly observe how far a user has browsed. This signal distinguishes displayed but unclicked items from items that were never displayed, whereas conventional ranking-bandit models, including cascade and position-based models, generally treat examination as latent. We formulate a ranking-bandit problem in which a learner presents a list of $L$ items, observes the user's maximum browsing depth, and receives click feedback only for positions up to that depth. The objective is to maximize the expected number of clicks under an unknown item-attractiveness vector and a browsing-depth distribution. We propose three algorithms based on UCB, Thompson Sampling, and DMED, all of which update item statistics only from observed exposures. We derive an instance-dependent logarithmic upper bound for our UCB-based algorithm and an asymptotic upper bound for our DMED-based algorithm that coincides with the lower bound as its parameter $\alpha\downarrow 0$, establishing asymptotic optimality in this limit. Simulations in synthetic shallow- and deep-browsing environments, together with experiments parameterized from RecGaze interaction logs, show that OD-TS attains final mean regret similar to PBM-TS, while the proposed methods achieve lower final mean regret than PBM-UCB.

replace Loopy: Low-Bit Quantization Framework for Looped Language Models

Authors: Zeyu Li, Yipu Zhang, Jintao Cheng, Xin Li, Wei Zhnag

Abstract: Looped language models provide a parameter-efficient way to scale iterative test-time computation by repeatedly executing a shared recurrent core. Post-training quantization (PTQ) can reduce the memory footprint and inference cost of looped language models, but errors introduced by a quantized shared core affect subsequent cores. Among PTQ methods, channel scaling and orthogonal rotations preserve the floating-point computation while producing representations with different quantization quality. We find that quantization configuration candidate rankings can change with recurrent depth, motivating configuration selection at the target deployment depth. However, evaluating every candidate over the full calibration set at this depth is costly. We therefore propose Loopy, a PTQ framework that formulates shared-core quantization through a recurrent-depth-aware objective, selecting shared low-bit representations by their final prediction loss at the target deployment depth. Channel scaling and orthogonal rotations parameterize the candidate representations. To approximately solve this selection problem efficiently, Loopy progressively allocates calibration windows to promising candidates while preserving complete target-depth execution, using only forward evaluations. Across eight settings, Loopy achieves the state-of-the-art results among different baselines. On Ouro-1.4B under W4A4, Loopy reduces LAMBADA perplexity by 36.5% relative to SpinQuant. Our code is available at https://github.com/Shameless0817/Loopy-review.git.

URLs: https://github.com/Shameless0817/Loopy-review.git.

replace Composing What Each Teacher Learned: Delta Distillation for Multi-Teacher On-Policy Learning

Authors: Yuanda Xu, Hejian Sang, Zhengze Zhou, Shayan Mohajer Hamidi, Xiaomin Li, Rohit Jain, Alborz Geramifard

Abstract: Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) is used in two settings. In common-domain composition, several teachers score each student rollout from one prompt domain and their signals form a single target; in routed-domain distillation, prompts from different domains are assigned to the corresponding specialist. Both settings usually transfer each teacher's endpoint policy, which mixes what post-training changed with preferences inherited from the teacher's base. We introduce $\Delta$-MOPD, which transfers each teacher's teacher-minus-base logit shift re-anchored at the student's frozen initialization, and compare it with endpoint supervision in both settings while holding teacher selection fixed. We first expose the mechanism that impedes endpoint transfer: inherited base pull can exceed the post-training shift. Removing it reduces the teacher-term norm ratio and target--student KL. Across our experiments, the results suggest that shift targets are particularly useful when teacher signals are combined at a state. With three composed teachers, $\Delta$-MOPD exceeds endpoint composition by $4.11$ Math and $1.95$ five-benchmark points; with two, it matches endpoint accuracy. Under phased routing, it achieves higher mean performance in both phase orders and reduces the observed order gap from $10.50$ to $6.42$ points. Under interleaved routing, where each update involves one teacher, the two targets perform comparably. The phased results provide supporting evidence that the benefit may extend to signals accumulated across training phases. Target construction is thus an independent design axis in MOPD, complementary to teacher selection. The experiment repo could be find at https://github.com/HJSang/Delta-MOPD

URLs: https://github.com/HJSang/Delta-MOPD

replace When Dynamic Scaling Fails: A Scale-State Feedback Governs Recurrent-State Quantization in Linear-Attention and SSM LLMs

Authors: Yuhang Jiang

Abstract: Linear-attention and state-space LLMs carry long context in a fixed-size recurrent state, and quantizing that state is the natural way to cut its cost during long decode. Unlike a weight tensor, the state is rewritten at every step, so a quantizer applied at each step sits inside the recurrence and its errors compound. INT4 that leaves synthetic recall perfectly intact at the start destroys it a thousand steps later. The mechanism is a feedback between the quantizer scale and the state it is derived from. Yoking that scale to a full-precision reference removes the resulting norm explosion entirely, yet recovers only 1.8 of the 5.4 nats lost, so numerical instability and functional degradation are separable failures. The remainder is resolution-dependent, and in our per-step setting every outlier-aware scheme we test loses to plain persistent scaling. Allocating bits across layers does help, but under article-disjoint splits a learned allocation collapses onto the trivial rule of protecting the first few layers. Run out to 32K, group-wise INT4 is worth no more than discarding the state. Contain the scale rather than locate outliers, and report the decode horizon at which a bit-width was certified.

replace Compute-Optimal Scaling Laws for Spiking Language Models

Authors: Dan Saunders, Benjamin Ren, Catherine He

Abstract: Spiking neural networks target energy-efficient language modeling through sparse, binary activations, but the efficiency-accuracy tradeoff of spiking language models is uncharacterized at scale. We present the first Chinchilla-style IsoFLOP scaling study of a spiking language model, sweeping SpikeGPT-family models (RWKV backbone) from width 384 to 1536 across compute budgets from $3\times10^{17}$ to $10^{19}$ FLOPs on FineWeb-EDU. We report two findings. First, the compute-optimal frontier is $N_{opt} \propto C^{0.56}$, close to the 0.50 that Hoffmann et al. report for dense Transformers, so spiking does not change how parameters and data should be traded off under a compute budget. Second, against non-spiking RWKV and Transformer baselines trained the same way, the loss gap splits into a backbone cost of about 0.16 nats that does not change with compute and a spiking cost that falls steadily with compute, from 0.34 to 0.23 nats over our range. These results give the first compute-optimal recipe for spiking language models and quantify the accuracy cost of their sparsity.

replace On the Monitorability of Natural Language Autoencoders: Identification vs Discrimination

Authors: Kevin Yandoka Denamgana\"i

Abstract: Natural Language Autoencoders (NLAs) are proposed as oversight tools for language models: an Activation Verbaliser (AV) writes an English explanation of an activation vector, and an Activation Reconstructor (AR) maps the explanation back to a vector. Their value for oversight depends on a human monitor being able to tell a true account of an activation from a false one, given the AV's explanation. We formalise NLA training and NLA oversight deployment as two different referential games from Emergent Communication: training is a generative referential game, while oversight is a discriminative, multi-modal, partially-observable referential game played over a many-to-many relation between the monitored language model's activations and the accounts that are true of them. The gap between the two games predicts that AV's explanations will identify which activation they describe without discriminating its true account from closely matched false ones, since training never asks explanations to rule anything out. Using the NLA's own AR as a maximally-sympathetic surrogate monitor model, we experimentally confirm this theoretical insight on one released checkpoint: true accounts beat accounts of other activations in all 320 comparisons, but beat fluent rivals differing in a single claim only 60.2% of the time (standard error 6.7%, chance at 50%, $n = 35$). A meaning-preserving rewrite by an uninvolved model costs only 2.1% of the distance to another activation's account, so the failure is not due to the NLA pair having developed private conventions in the AV's explanations. Our rival accounts test only transcript-level truths, which the monitored language model's conversation settles, so these results bound the easiest case and leave claims about the monitored model's own processing untested. We state conjectures that would make both results consequences of the framework.

replace Does On-Policy Distillation Need to Complete Every Rollout?

Authors: Zhengxin Zhang, Maosen Tang, Ning Wang, Changhao Li, Jingren Zhou, Bo Dai, Sainyam Galhotra, Alex Townsend, Claire Cardie

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) transfers teacher knowledge to a student through feedback on student-generated rollouts, but generating complete rollouts and computing per-token teacher feedback are computationally expensive. Existing truncation methods save computation by stopping rollouts early. However, they do not compensate for truncated token gradients, which biases the gradient estimates and changes Vanilla OPD's original training objective. Furthermore, their truncation decisions rely on assumptions about supervision quality or criteria that may not generalize across settings. We introduce Stochastic Truncated On-Policy Distillation (STOPD), which accelerates OPD by randomly truncating student rollouts and correcting the resulting sampling bias with blockwise inverse-probability weighting (IPW). At each training step, we sample a stopping position for each rollout before generation and let the student generate until that position or natural termination. We obtain teacher feedback on every generated token and use blockwise IPW to compensate for the lower probability of retaining later tokens while controlling the weights. We prove that this weighting preserves Vanilla OPD's gradient sum in expectation and derive one-step update-error bounds for SGD, Adam, and Muon. We evaluate STOPD across multiple teacher-student model pairs, task domains, and optimizers. STOPD reduces generated tokens by 25-48% while maintaining accuracy comparable to Vanilla OPD, achieving 1.3-2.2x training speedups.

replace Training-Free On-Policy Distillation

Authors: Bangji Yang, Jiajun Fan, Zhaoyu Zhu, Hongbo Ma, Ge Liu

Abstract: On-policy distillation consolidates specialist models into one student at the price of rollouts, teacher passes and gradient steps for every teacher. We show that the consolidation does not need training: with forward passes only, the capabilities of separately tuned specialists can be aggregated into one model that is, on average, more accurate than either specialist alone. When specialists share a base model, distillation has an exact layer-wise solution: each linear layer of the fused model must reproduce every specialist's output on the inputs that specialist receives while reading its own answers, a least-squares problem whose solution needs only the second moments of those inputs. One forward pass over a few hundred rollouts provides them. We call this training-free on-policy distillation. What the rollouts contain matters more than the solver: answers truncated in repetition loops are few among a mathematics specialist's rollouts but carry a large share of their tokens, and removing them lifts the fused model on AIME25 from task arithmetic's level to the specialist's at matched precision. On Olmo-3-7B, a few GPU-hours of forward computation per specialist yield a generalist that keeps most of each specialist's gain on each of seven mathematics and code benchmarks.

replace Batched Contextual Reinforcement II: Does RLVR Need Long Rollout Windows?

Authors: Bangji Yang, Hongbo Ma, Ruihan Guo, Jiajun Fan, Xi Zhu, Ge Liu

Abstract: Long rollout windows make reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) expensive, yet simply shortening them can remove the correctness signal: when every single-problem rollout truncates before an answer, group-relative accuracy advantages vanish. As a technical continuation of Batched Contextual Reinforcement (BCR), we study how concurrent rewards relax this dependence on long windows. Concurrent Constraint Reinforcement (CCR) places three independently verifiable problems in one capped training completion. Correct answers completed before truncation retain credit, while a shared budget encourages shorter useful reasoning without an explicit length penalty. We connect this mechanism to conditional reward- and update-variance analyses and study 4B and 8B mathematical reasoning models. Against single-problem GRPO with a 32k window, CCR with an 8,000-token window reduces tokens per training step from 975,464 to 174,957 on 4B and from 793,832 to 165,788 on 8B, saving 82.1% and 79.1%, respectively. An 8B training trace shows increasing accuracy reward alongside shorter completions. Single-problem evaluations provide supporting evidence of retained reasoning quality. These results identify reward-preserving training structure as a practical route to lower per-step cost in short-window RLVR.

replace Towards Hyperparameter Transfer for Differentially Private Optimization

Authors: Tianze Wang, Zhiqi Bu, Linjun Zhang

Abstract: Scaling laws and hyperparameter tuning remain central challenges in training large-scale models, particularly under differential privacy (DP), where optimization is further complicated by noise injection and per-sample gradient clipping. While Maximal Update Parametrization ($\mu$P) allows for zero-shot hyperparameter transfer in standard training, we show that it fails under DP constraints due to the distinct spectral properties of high-dimensional noise versus low-rank gradients. In this work, we introduce DP-$\mu$P, a theoretically grounded framework that extends $\mu$P to the privacy-preserving setting. By analyzing the interaction between gradient clipping, noise injection, and feature learning, we derive a Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) condition that governs layer-wise learning rate scaling. Combining this with our proposed spectral scaling formulation, we provide specific scaling rules for DP-SGD, DP-Adam, and DP-Muon optimizers. Empirically, we demonstrate that DP-$\mu$P enables effective zero-shot transfer of learning rates across model widths on diverse architectures (MLP, ViT, GPT-2), matching or exceeding the performance of individually tuned baselines while significantly reducing computational costs.

replace A Stronger Teacher Within: Strengthening MoE Models via On-Policy Bidirectional Distillation

Authors: Haoyu Chen, Jun Bai, Yang Liu, Jianfei Zhang, Wenge Rong

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs, but its outcome-level rewards provide only sparse supervision. On-Policy Distillation offers dense token-level feedback, yet requires a stronger compatible teacher, while on-policy self-distillation relies on hand-designed privileged information. We observe that the MoE architecture itself offers a way to resolve this dilemma, as increasing the activation budget per token exposes latent expert capacity and yields a potentially stronger teacher within the model. However, naive expansion degrades performance because newly co-activated experts are not optimized to work together. We therefore propose MoEvolve, a bidirectional distillation framework following a recovery-then-improvement strategy: we first restore the activation-expanded high-$k$ model and further strengthen it by exploiting its additional active capacity, then reverse the direction of distillation to transfer the enhanced capabilities back into the original low-$k$ configuration, retaining its inference-time activation budget. Across diverse MoE models and benchmarks, MoEvolve improves the initial models by up to $22.2$ domain-average points and outperforms same-budget RLVR baselines by up to $6.5$ points. Notably, the final low-$k$ models often surpass their activation-expanded teachers.

replace Response-Informed Value Estimation for Cost-Aware Multi-Turn LLM Routing

Authors: Haotian Yan, Jiaqi Liu, Bingyi Jing, Tianjun Yao

Abstract: Multi-turn LLM routing selects a model at every turn before its response is available, yet task success is revealed only when the interaction ends, leaving each intermediate choice without a label of its own. Logged trajectories, however, retain the response each call produced, which exposes what each turn required and how well the chosen model served it, evidence that outcomes alone hide. We introduce RIVET (Response-Informed Value Estimation for Turn-Level Routing), which exploits this post-call evidence during training while keeping routing strictly pre-call at deployment. RIVET jointly trains two value estimators over a shared state-model representation: a deployable Routing Value Estimator that scores candidate models from pre-call information, and a training-only Response-Informed Value Estimator that additionally observes the realized response. Conditioning on the response turns response-induced return variation from residual noise into an observed covariate, and we show that this reduces the leading asymptotic estimation error of the shared representation. On the MINT benchmark, RIVET solves more held-out tasks than the strongest multi-turn baseline while using 74.7% less total call cost.

replace How Data Scales in Agentic Reinforcement Learning: Laws and Synthesis Strategies

Authors: Bowei He, Yankai Chen, Xiaokun Zhang, Changjiang Han, Ye Yuan, Chong Li, Xue Liu

Abstract: Pretraining scaling laws treat training data as a one-dimensional quantity: a token count. Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) inherits this scalar abstraction, but modern synthesis pipelines now generate \textit{tasks}, \textit{environments}, and \textit{trajectories} independently and at very different costs, turning data into a structured design space. This raises a question pretraining never had to answer: given a synthesis budget, \textit{which axis} should be scaled? We address this question end-to-end with three tightly coupled contributions. First, we decompose agentic data into three cardinality axes (task, environment, trajectory), plus a reward-density control variable, and fit per-axis scaling relations on different large language models (LLMs) across math+code, SWE, web, and UI/tool application domains. Across all model sizes, model families, domains, and RL algorithms (PPO and GRPO) we measured, the scaling exponents order as environment $>$ task $>$ trajectory. When environment diversity is decomposed into world and environment-structure diversity, its advantage is mainly driven by the former, while the latter is just comparable to the task diversity. Second, we use the fitted laws as a common ruler to price synthesis methods, capturing each method's efficiency as a per-axis discount factor and identifying critical synthetic-to-real ratios at which collapse begins; these ratios differ by an order of magnitude across axes. Third, we cast budget allocation as a constrained optimization under our laws and discounts, deriving a Chinchilla-style compute-optimal recipe and validating it at held-out scale. The empirical results demonstrate that following the recipe yields up to $1.7\times$ compute efficiency over the dominant ``scale-the-trajectories'' practice.

replace Learning Administrative Boundary-Aware Location Encoders via Reverse Geocoding

Authors: Lakshay Sharma, Alex Marin

Abstract: Location encoders learn compact representations of latitude--longitude coordinates for downstream geospatial prediction tasks. Existing approaches are pretrained using continuous or naturally occurring supervision, such as imagery, climate variables, or species distributions, and therefore do not explicitly capture administrative boundaries or place names, despite their strong influence on many real-world geospatial phenomena. We introduce, to our knowledge, the first method for learning administrative boundary-aware location encoders, using reverse-geocoded text as a self-supervised training signal. Our approach can be applied to existing location encoder architectures and consistently improves their downstream performance. We further introduce RevGeoRQ, a custom architecture tailored to reverse-geocoding supervision, which yields additional gains and exceeds contemporary location encoders on several downstream tasks. Finally, the use of human-interpretable reverse-geocoded strings and residual-quantized semantic IDs provides a more interpretable representation of geographic location than existing approaches. Code and model weights to be released soon.

replace DeliMF: Gated Cross-Attention Fusion of Structured Time Series and Clinical Text for Early Intensive Care Delirium Prediction

Authors: Yibo Yang, Zerui Huang, Haomiao Yang, Zixia Wang

Abstract: Delirium affects a large fraction of intensive care unit (ICU) patients and foreshadows longer stays, lasting cognitive decline, and higher mortality. Flagging the patients who will become delirious within the first day of admission would give clinicians a window to intervene. Yet state-of-the-art predictors rely only on the structured electronic health record (EHR) of vital signs, laboratory values, and medications, while overlooking the free-text clinical notes written in the same window, which carry information the numbers cannot encode. To this end, we present DeliMF, a multimodal framework that fuses the structured time series with clinical text. It encodes the structured signals with a temporal Transformer and the time-stamped radiology reports with a clinical text encoder, and fuses the two through a gated cross-attention module. The gate learns a per-patient weight on the text; when a patient has no note the gate closes and DeliMF reduces to its structured-only form, giving robustness to missing text by construction. On an incident-only MIMIC-IV cohort, DeliMF improves significantly over the baselines, particularly on patients who carry clinical text, while losing nothing on those without. Ablation studies confirm the contribution of multimodal fusion and gated cross-attention, and a case study further shows that fusing clinical text with the structured stream recovers delirium cases that the structured data alone misses. We hope DeliMF encourages early ICU prediction to make fuller use of the overlooked clinical text and contributes to the early identification and timely intervention of delirium.

replace Process-Aware Event Log Augmentation for Process Prediction under Labeled Data Scarcity

Authors: Sjoerd van Straten, Alessandro Padella, Marwan Hassani

Abstract: Predictive Process Monitoring (PPM) forecasts future events of ongoing business processes based on event logs. However, deep learning PPM approaches are often limited by labeled data scarcity and the low variability of real-world logs. To address this, we introduce SiamSA-PPM, a self-supervised framework combining Siamese learning with statistically grounded data augmentation. It employs novel transformation methods that leverage control-flow semantics and frequent behavioral patterns to generate realistic, semantically valid trace variants, learning generalizable prefix representations without labeled supervision. Extensive experiments on real-world event logs with a wide range of variability, incorporating exhaustive hyperparameter tuning, demonstrate the value of this process-aware augmentation. Compared to state-of-the-art baselines, SiamSA-PPM achieves competitive or superior predictive performance in both next activity and final outcome prediction tasks. An open source implementation of our method is publicly available under https://github.com/SvStraten/SiamSA-PPM-Extension..

URLs: https://github.com/SvStraten/SiamSA-PPM-Extension..

replace Polynomial Learning of Positive ReLU Networks under Gaussian Inputs

Authors: Zhijie Zheng

Abstract: We study positive, bias-free, one-hidden-layer ReLU networks under standard Gaussian inputs. We obtain algorithms with polynomial sample and real-arithmetic complexity, without separation or conditioning assumptions on the hidden directions. When the conditional label mean is a width-$k$ network with total output weight at most one, the learner achieves squared prediction error $\varepsilon$ with output width at most $k+\min\{k,d\}$ and complexity linear in one plus the residual second moment. For arbitrary labels with bounded second moment, we obtain a positive ReLU network of width $O(k\log(2/\varepsilon))$. Its risk is bounded by the irreducible noise variance plus $Ck^3\log^{9/2}(2k/\varepsilon)$ times the squared distance of the conditional mean to the target class, plus $\varepsilon$. The proofs combine implicit Hermite moments, orthogonal tensor-train compression, and a direction decoder whose probability of returning a direction far from every atom is bounded linearly in the compression and estimation errors. Exact Gaussian localization refines the resulting direction covers. In the agnostic setting, a cone-constrained convex program with a scale-dependent penalty produces a positive network; in the conditional-mean setting, exact sparsification yields the smaller width. We also establish projective direction recovery and confidence amplification with sample complexity proportional to the logarithm of the reciprocal failure probability.

replace Truncate or Not? A Scaling Study of Looped Transformers with Truncated Backpropagation

Authors: Xuyuan Xiong, Siyu Chen, Zihan Shao, Yue Su, Weiyang Jin, Ping Luo, Zhuoran Yang, Xihui Liu

Abstract: Looped transformers reuse a shared block of layers to increase computational depth without increasing the number of trainable parameters. They are trained with backpropagation through time (BPTT). To save training compute, BPTT is often truncated to the last few loops, but truncation yields biased gradients that may weaken the benefit of looping. In this work, we study the value of truncation in looped transformer training in a systematic way: we jointly vary model size, training tokens, and forward and backward loop counts across nearly 600 training runs and fit a unified scaling law that captures the effects of looping and truncation through effective parameters. Despite the training-compute savings of truncation, our fitted law favors full BPTT when forward and backward loop counts are jointly optimized. In matched-compute runs, full BPTT through fewer loops consistently outperforms truncated settings of the same compute. The law further shows that at fixed model size, the compute-optimal loop count and training-token budget both grow with compute. When model size is also free, the law identifies two fully trained loops as the best looped setting. Experimentally, this setting achieves lower validation loss than the compute-optimal non-looped model with about 27% fewer non-embedding parameters and only 8% more training compute.

replace A Simple and General Approach to top-$k$ Selection

Authors: Motti Goldberger, Nils Rudi

Abstract: This paper considers the Bayesian top-$k$ identification problem, wherein a decision maker is tasked with selecting $k$ items from a pool of items of unknown quality. To assess the items, the decision maker adaptively allocates a fixed sampling budget, where each sample of an item yields a noisy observation of its quality, with the objective of minimizing the expected opportunity cost of the selected set. We propose an approach that allocates each batch of samples by minimizing a surrogate objective derived from an upper bound on the expected opportunity cost. The approach is general: samples can be allocated in batches, the selection size $k$ is arbitrary, and it applies to many sampling distributions. It is also simple and intuitive. We prove that the method's posterior expected opportunity cost decays at an exponential rate of at least half the best rate achievable by any sampling rule. In simulations across a wide range of settings, the method is competitive with, and often improves upon, the best existing algorithm in each setting.

replace Rethinking LLM Fine-Tuning via Weight Space Reparameterization: Preserving Safety during Downstream Adaptation

Authors: Min-Seong Kim, Jongbok Won, Jeesup Park, Yonghee Choi, Dong-Jun Han

Abstract: Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on downstream tasks often weakens their previously aligned safety behavior, creating an inherent trade-off between task adaptation and safety preservation. Existing approaches attempt to mitigate this issue by identifying safety-relevant neurons, layers, or update directions in the original parameter space. However, safety-related information is often distributed across many directions in this space, causing downstream updates to overwrite parameters that encode safety behavior. We propose WSR-Tune, a Weight Space Reparameterization-based fine-tuning framework that preserves safety during downstream adaptation. Our approach constructs a safety-conditioned basis and reparameterizes the weight matrices such that safety-relevant information becomes concentrated in a small subset of directions. We then identify safety-critical directions and freeze them in the reparameterized space, while updating the remaining complementary directions for downstream adaptation. By explicitly structuring the parameter space to disentangle safety and task information, our method reduces interference between objectives and enables simultaneous preservation of safety and improvement in downstream performance. Experimental results show that our method achieves a more favorable trade-off between downstream performance and safety retention, demonstrating its effectiveness for reliable LLM fine-tuning.

replace HarnessPro: Recursive Harness Self-Improvement with Pareto Optimization for Performance-Cost Trade-offs

Authors: Zhuoshi Pan, ZhiYuan Feng, Yukuo Cen, Honglin Lin, Junru Lu, Zhongxia Chen, Shuo Yang, H. Vicky Zhao, Jie Tang

Abstract: Harness development plays a crucial role in building capable AI agents. Recent approaches improve harnesses through recursive self-improvement (RSI), but their performance gains often come with higher execution costs. We introduce HarnessPro, a label-free method for evolving harnesses to improve task performance while reducing execution cost. In this setting, candidate harnesses are evaluated using LLM-generated tests without access to ground-truth solutions or official tests. Specialized agents diagnose execution trajectories, plan updates with different performance-cost priorities, and implement them as candidate harnesses. Rather than returning a single harness, HarnessPro uses normalized weighted squared-distance selection to provide cost-focused (lite), balanced (medium), and performance-focused (heavy) harnesses for different deployment budgets. Selected harnesses enter the next iteration, where execution feedback guides further updates. Our experiments cover Terminal-Bench 2, SWE-Bench Pro, and ProgramBench, using GPT-5.5 with Codex and GLM-5.2 with Claude Code. Across these benchmarks using GPT-5.5 with Codex, the heavy mode of HarnessPro achieves an average pass-rate gain of 2.0 percentage points and an average per-task cost reduction of 26.1% relative to RHO. This advantage also holds when using GLM-5.2 with Claude Code. Code is available at https://github.com/pzs19/HarnessPro

URLs: https://github.com/pzs19/HarnessPro

replace WeAct: Dynamic Weight Switching as Agent Actions

Authors: Rui Shi, Xunjian Yin, Jiacheng Sang, Zichuan Liu, Shuyan Zhou

Abstract: Long-horizon agents must learn heterogeneous capabilities from interleaved trajectories. Updating shared parameters to learn new capabilities can degrade previously acquired ones, resulting in catastrophic forgetting. As a trajectory unfolds, different stages may require different capabilities, yet conventional joint training and static adapter merging maintain a fixed parameter configuration throughout execution. We introduce WeAct (Weight-level Internal Actions), which trains independent capability-specific LoRA adapters over a frozen backbone and adds adapter selection to the agent's action space. These internal actions use the model's native tool-calling interface for on-demand capability composition in a shared context. Across eight deep-research benchmarks, Qwen3-32B with WeAct improves average accuracy by 6.2% relative to full fine-tuning counterparts despite using less compute, and by 27.5% relative to the strongest static LoRA-merging method. WeAct also supports modular continual adaptation, by adding a new code computation adapter to expand the agent's capabilities and updating the existing writing adapter to improve report quality. Both updates leave the backbone and other adapters unchanged, supporting modular continual adaptation in agents.

replace Text-to-Image Generative Modeling Without Language Pretraining

Authors: Keshigeyan Chandrasegaran, Yue Zhao, Armin W. Thomas, Ehsan Adeli, Jiajun Wu, Juan Carlos Niebles, Li Fei-Fei, Michael Poli

Abstract: Modern text-to-image (T2I) systems use internet-scale language pretraining for text-conditioned image generation. We ask whether language pretraining is necessary for strong T2I generation. We introduce Halcyon, a family of early-fusion multimodal autoregressive models pretrained entirely from scratch on GPIC, a dataset of 100M image-text pairs. Halcyon-0.52B outperforms LlamaGen-XL/8 (48.0 vs. 63.3 gFD) with 18.2$\times$ less pretraining compute. Further, Halcyon-1.6B outperforms JiT-T2I-XXL/8++ (35.3 vs. 41.0 gFD) with 275$\times$ less pretraining compute. Controlled experiments show that text-only pretraining does not improve T2I generation. Halcyon also learns image captioning by replacing caption dropout with I2T supervision, maintaining competitive T2I generation quality under the same pretraining budget. We show that strong T2I systems can be trained from scratch without internet-scale language pretraining. Code and models are available at https://cs.stanford.edu/~keshik/halcyon
