replace-cross TTS-Q: Speaker-Aware Post-Training Quantization of Text-to-Speech Diffusion Transformers

Authors: Xiaoqin Feng, Yuqi Li, Weilun Feng, Yi-Cheng Lin, Kai Li, Xianglong Wang, Kuo Yang, Chuanguang Yang, Yingli Tian, Wei Li, Zhongwei Cheng, Lin Chen

Abstract: Zero-shot text-to-speech (TTS) can clone a voice from seconds of audio. Yet the quality comes from billion-parameter diffusion transformers whose size and multi-step sampling put them out of reach of the on-device, assistive, and real-time applications that need them most. Post-training quantization (PTQ) shrinks such models without retraining, but it has been built for language and vision, not speech. At 4 bits, a strong quantizer leaves intelligibility close to full precision but consistently erodes speaker timbre, so error rates stay low while the voice drifts from the target speaker. We ask why quantization hurts speech, where the damage lives, and whether it can be repaired without retraining. To find out, we run the first 4-bit weight-and-activation (W4A4) PTQ study of a TTS diffusion transformer at 1B and 3.5B. The timbre cost comes from both the weight and the activation quantization, but only the activation part has the timestep structure a precision schedule can exploit. We therefore introduce TTS-Q, which selects hard-to-quantize items from task-matched calibration candidates and applies a budget-balanced mixed-precision schedule that assigns higher precision to late-step feed-forward activations. TTS-Q recovers timbre best at both scales, at no cost to intelligibility. Separately, the deployed calibrated-W4A4 reference reduces peak memory on GPU, providing a practical reference for resource-constrained TTS deployment.

replace-cross Some remarks on the connection between generative diffusion and optimal transport

Authors: Giulio Biroli, Marc M\'ezard

Abstract: We discuss three simple connections between generative diffusion and optimal transport. First, the joint law of the data and of their Ornstein-Uhlenbeck noising is, at every time, the solution of an entropic optimal-transport problem, at a temperature set by the noise level. Backward diffusion can consequently be read as an annealing which gradually decreases the entropic regularization. Second, we study the speciation transition--how the modes of the data distribution emerge during generation--and compare how it happens in diffusion and flow matching with respect to optimal transport. Using Gaussian mixtures as high-dimensional example, we show that the dynamical instability through which diffusion and flow matching separate the modes becomes, for optimal transport, an exponentially thin boundary layer at the beginning of generation, which is rounded by entropic regularization. Third, considering a generic forward dynamics with a general confining potential, we show that the backward process driven by the score also satisfies a drift-based variational principle whose minimal cost is the entropy difference between initial and final distributions.

replace-cross What Survives When Activation Steering Is Used as Memory

Authors: Vedant Jumle

Abstract: Activation steering has recently been used as a memory for skills: past experiences of an agent are stored as steering vectors and retrieved later. Facts and preferences about the user are assumed to need text instead. We test that assumption with a training-free activation memory beside a frozen LLM. The change an experience causes in late-layer states is stored as a shift, filed under a whitened key for the conversation's gist, and added back in a later session only when the gist matches. We argue that such a shift is a fixed average of an effect that attention, in context, recomputes for every question, so only question-independent content can survive. In a pre-registered study on Qwen3.5-2B and 9B, with judges from four model families, the memory is selective (about 98% of unrelated replies are unchanged), but facts are stored as the word to say, in the last layers only, and are almost never used (at most 15% clean use, against 96-98% in context). Leanings transfer only in a narrow strength window. Most strikingly, the reference subtracted when writing a preference decides whether its concept or its direction survives: unless the opposite is subtracted, a stored dislike is recalled as a like.

replace-cross Learning Reachability-Based Safety Representations for Real-Time Robot Navigation in Unknown and Dynamic Environments

Authors: Bojan Deraji\'c

Abstract: Safe robot navigation in unknown, dynamic environments requires more than geometric collision checking: a robot still far from an obstacle may be unable to avoid it because of its speed, orientation, or the obstacle's motion. Hamilton--Jacobi (HJ) reachability analysis captures this in a value function whose sign separates states that admit a safe continuation from those where collision is unavoidable, but computing it online for every newly observed environment is too expensive. This dissertation trains neural implicit representations offline on reachability solutions to give optimization-based controllers this information in real time. Since the value function never exceeds the constraint, it equals a directly computed constraint, such as a signed distance, minus a nonnegative dynamics correction. Learning only this correction keeps represented obstacles outside the predicted safe set despite approximation errors. The first two of five methods use the learned function as a terminal constraint in model predictive control, for static local environments and predicted obstacle motion. The next two use it as a barrier candidate in control barrier function safety filters, for static environments and, through pairwise barriers in relative coordinates, for a varying number of moving obstacles. Simulations and hardware experiments on ground and aerial robots show better collision avoidance than geometric and hand-designed baselines, especially for short horizons and moving obstacles. The fifth method replaces the grid-based offline solver, which limits the first four to low-dimensional models. For control-affine dynamics with polytopic inputs, the HJ Hamiltonian is maximized at a vertex, so a parallelizable tree search over vertex inputs can generate training labels. With a loss from the HJ variational inequality, these labels yield barrier candidates for systems with up to 13 states.

replace-cross Measuring Variable Importance Using Proportional Inclusive Marginal Effects

Authors: Youngseo Cho, V. Roshan Joseph

Abstract: Shapley-based measures are widely used for measuring variable importance in statistics and machine learning. However, these measures have a serious issue--a nonactive variable can get a positive importance measure when it is correlated with an active variable. Herin et al. recently proposed a measure called proportional marginal effects (PME) that mitigates this spurious effect. However, PME is developed for deterministic models and has high computational cost. In this article, we propose a new measure called proportional inclusive marginal effects (PRIME) that overcomes these two limitations of PME. The key idea in PRIME is to use a novel model-free variable selection technique to identify the active variables and include only them in the computation of the importance measure. Analysis of test functions and real datasets show that PRIME can accurately and reliably quantify variable importance from noisy input-output data.

replace-cross Deficit-Driven Stratified Acquisition for Building STRATA-S12: An Open SAR-Optical Corpus for Self-Supervised Learning

Authors: Christian Sanchez-Leon, Dongming Chen, Dongqi Wang

Abstract: Building a co-registered SAR-optical corpus for self-supervised Earth Observation is above all an acquisition problem, yet the main multi-modal datasets are collected opportunistically, with no per-stratum quota control and no mechanism against the geographic dropout that non-uniform cloud cover leaves behind. We present Deficit-Driven Stratified Acquisition (DDSA), an algorithm that treats building a co-registered Sentinel-1/Sentinel-2 corpus as a constrained maximum-coverage problem over a hemisphere-corrected (class x season x region) stratification, enforces strict temporal pairing and a multi-stage quality gate, and closes the loop during acquisition with a human-in-the-loop deficit-repair stage. Measured on its own product, the algorithm yields three results a single-pass acquisition cannot report. Deficit-driven ordering makes a partial run a covering one: it holds every one of the 192 strata populated from the first few percent of the budget, where an abundance-first order leaves most strata empty until the end. Whether a failed attempt can be recovered depends on the temporal persistence of its cause, not the kind of defect: geometric failures such as nodata are among the most repairable (eta = 41.4%) while cloud is among the least (eta = 18.8%), because a cloudy location is cloudy on most dates of its season. The repair loop supplies 39.5% of the released pairs at no measurable quality cost, with 89.8% surviving manual review. The quality guarantee has a price: 2,928,370 acquisition attempts produced 740,746 released pairs. We release the corpus, STRATA-S12, with STAC/PDS4-aligned FAIR sidecars, and a pre-registered linear-probe check confirms it is fit for self-supervised pre-training. A metadata-level coverage comparison against SSL4EO-S12 and SEN12MS situates the corpus over a shared frame; systematic cross-corpus and cross-method comparison is left to follow-up work.

replace-cross Complete or Sparse: A Tale of Two Identifiabilities

Authors: Junze Zhou, David Klindt

Abstract: Understanding when neural networks learn interpretable representations is central to mechanistic interpretability. We study this through identifiability: when can supervised learning recover the true latent variables underlying the data? We introduce two data generating processes distinguished by their causal direction where either labels cause latents (generative) or latents cause labels (discriminative). When the representation has at least as many dimensions as the latent space, we prove that the optimal encoders are linear in the latents. Conversely, when dimensions are insufficient, identifiability generically fails and encoders become nonlinear. We then show that additional constraints yield recovery of individual latent variables, not just linear mixtures. In the generative setting, non-negative activations and energy efficiency force each neuron to encode exactly one latent, providing a precise account of selective grandmother cell-like responses. In the discriminative setting, when latents are sparse and the true measurement matrix satisfies the spark condition, the learned encoder inherits compressed sensing structure, enabling sparse dictionary learning to perfectly recover individual latents. This constitutes, to our knowledge, the first identifiability result for undercomplete nonlinear independent component analysis. Our framework unifies nonlinear ICA, compressed sensing, and neural network interpretability, delineating exactly when representations transition from nonlinear distortions to linear representations and, under additional constraints or steps, to clean, axis-aligned codes. Theoretical predictions are validated on synthetic benchmarks and on activations drawn from vision models, large language models, and macaque inferotemporal cortex.

replace-cross Beyond Invariance: Factor-wise Selective Treatment of Shifts for Multimodal Learning

Authors: Guimin Hu, Qiwei Peng, Boyan Xu, Ruichu Cai

Abstract: Distribution shifts pose a fundamental challenge to robust multimodal learning. Existing methods typically promote invariance by suppressing environment-dependent variations, implicitly applying a uniform treatment to shifted information. However, shifted information can have different predictive consequences: some contributes little to prediction, some remains reliably informative, and some exhibits environment-dependent predictive effects. This raises a fundamental question: which shifted information should be suppressed, preserved, or attenuated? To address this question, we propose FaShift, a factor-wise shift framework that diagnoses latent representation factors according to their distributional changes, intervention effects, and cross-environment stability. By factorizing multimodal representations and evaluating these complementary properties, FaShift distinguishes non-predictive factors, stably predictive factors, and predictively useful but unstable factors. It then selectively suppresses, preserves, or attenuates their contributions, rather than enforcing uniform invariance. Experiments on MOSI, MOSEI, and CH-SIMS show that FaShift achieves the best ID accuracy and F1 across all three datasets. Compared with the strongest competitors reporting OOD results, it improves OOD accuracy/F1 by 1.5/3.6 points on MOSI and 1.7/2.7 points on MOSEI, while achieving the best reported OOD accuracy and F1 on CH-SIMS.

replace-cross HyCo: Hybrid Drafting for Cost-Efficient Mobile-Cloud Speculative Decoding

Authors: Hanbin Bae, Heejin Choi, Joohyung Lee, Byoung Jin Choi, Sangjun Park, Hoon-Young Cho

Abstract: Device-cloud speculative decoding reduces server computation, but growing a deep draft tree with a small language model (SLM) requires repeated passes on a mobile neural processing unit (NPU). We present HyCo, which runs the server model's native multi-token prediction (MTP) head on the device to build a deep chain and uses one SLM pass to add alternative branches. The server returns the hidden state, input embedding, and key-value entries needed by the head instead of executing its sequential steps. Quantization, a restricted vocabulary, and a 1K-token window fit both drafters to the NPU. An {\epsilon}-threshold rule additionally relaxes exact preservation of the verifier's sampling distribution. In single-client runs with a Galaxy S26 Ultra over LTE/5G, HyCo reduces server GPU time per output token relative to server-side MTP self-speculation by 38.6% on Spec-Bench and 29.2% on 32K-token summarization. With the same acceptance rule, three runs of each system on Spec-Bench show 9.0 percentage points more saving and 1.9x delivery speed than an SLM-only drafter. HyCo differs from Cloud+MTP by +0.2 judge-score points (paired 95% CI: -0.9 to +1.3); exploratory translation favors SpecEdge-{\epsilon}. Without relaxation, HyCo exact saves 27.7% and 11.9% on the two datasets.

replace-cross Knowledge That Is Linear Before Fine-Tuning Generalizes After It

Authors: Faridoun Mehri, Hinrich Sch\"utze

Abstract: Not all facts a language model can recall support further learning equally well. We distinguish active facts, which support generalization beyond their own recall, from inert facts, which can be recalled but do not support such generalization in a given task. We study this distinction by constructing new relations from existing ones. Take the relation that maps a place to its continent (Paris -> Europe) and assign each of its answers an arbitrary new label (Europe -> crab, Asia -> zebra). The new relation then maps Paris -> crab. We fine-tune the model on the new relation for a subset of subjects, without telling it the original relation or the relabeling rule, and test whether it assigns the corresponding labels to held-out subjects that share an answer in the original relation. Having learned Paris -> crab, does it answer Munich -> crab? Across eleven language models and 70 relations, how linearly readable the original relation is in pretrained representations strongly predicts this generalization. Within each model, the two correlate across relations at Pearson r = 0.87 to 0.92. This association persists when controlling, separately and jointly, for the number of fine-tuning subjects and answers, the model's accuracy on the original relation, and how often the subjects occur in pretraining text. It is also robust to re-randomizing the label assignment and fine-tuning seed, and to replacing the animal labels with labels from thirteen other semantic categories. When we map a fine-tuned model's wrong label back to the original answer it was assigned to, Munich -> zebra becomes Munich -> Asia. Answers recovered this way are more often among the linear probe's top answers for the subject than among the pretrained model's own. Whether a new relation generalizes depends less on whether its facts can be recalled than on how readily their shared structure can be read out linearly.

replace-cross LLM-Attack Suite: A Unified Red-Team Framework for Privacy Evaluation of Split and Distributed LLM Systems

Authors: Georgios Politis, Evangelos Pappas

Abstract: Split language models keep input text on a client but send intermediate representations to a server. Attacks can reconstruct text from these messages. Comparing privacy evaluations is difficult when attackers see different messages, know different model weights or use different datasets and recovery measures. We present LLM-Attack Suite, a framework that records these choices and lets researchers check, compare and extend an evaluation. Compatible attacks reuse recorded messages, and their predictions are saved for independent scoring. The scoring rule requires a linked undefended reference before a defence can pass. Reports also identify attacks that remain untested. In a controlled GPT-2 experiment, adding gradients returned during training increased token recovery from 94.20% to 97.38% and exact-document recovery from 13.71% to 37.77%. The 95% interval for the token gain lay above the two-percentage-point threshold fixed before testing. This experiment is shared with a companion study; here it tests how the evaluation responds to a change in attacker access. Further studies measure how corpus choice and compression defences affect recovery, compare an adapted published attack with its reference implementation, and test the measurement code. The framework reports implementation checks separately from measured attack effectiveness. Each privacy claim should state the attacker, dataset and scoring rule, report its controls and identify untested attacks.

replace-cross DIFFLET: A Fast Inference Engine for Diffusion Transformer Models on AWS Trainium

Authors: Bin Ma, Min Yu, Vijay Kodamalla, Jonny Liu, Da Li, Dong Li

Abstract: On AWS Trainium2, every component of a diffusion model runs as a program compiled ahead of time for fixed shapes, while pipelines differ across models, prompt lengths across requests, and caching decisions across denoising steps. We present Difflet, a serving engine in which five model pipelines share one compilation backend, joint attention is split across cores by attention head, and a host controller decides at each denoising step whether to run the denoiser or reuse its earlier output. A small probe program computes the caching signal on the device and returns one number per step, and a resident model process keeps every program loaded across requests. Across five image and video model families, fused attention accelerates denoiser steps by 1.2-4.3x over the same framework using PyTorch/XLA attention, and at 32.8k visual tokens it is the only attention that compiles. On four of the five models, resident serving is 1.5-3.4x faster than standalone runs that reload weights from a warm file cache for each generation.

replace-cross NestedVLA: Learning to Consolidate and Generate Skills for Vision-Language-Action Models

Authors: Guodong Du, Wanyu Lin

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models can learn a wide range of robotic skills, but combining separately trained skills remains challenging. The original training data may be unavailable for joint retraining, while directly merging model weights can cause interference. We propose NestedVLA, which consolidates independently fine-tuned VLA policies without access to their original training data. Instead of merging all updates into one checkpoint, a hypernetwork embedded in the VLA generates action-expert weight updates for a selected task. Each task's compressed vision-language model update is stored separately, and both updates are added to a frozen base VLA to instantiate the corresponding policy. We evaluate NestedVLA by consolidating three policies fine-tuned with reinforcement learning on MetaWorld, ManiSkill, and CALVIN. It achieves 66.7% average success, improving on the strongest model-merging baseline by 41.1 percentage points. It performs within 1 percentage point of per-task fine-tuning on ManiSkill and substantially narrows the gap on CALVIN. In this three-policy setting, total storage is 1.5x that of a single full checkpoint. Code is available at https://github.com/WanyuGroup/NeurIPS2026-NestedVLA.

URLs: https://github.com/WanyuGroup/NeurIPS2026-NestedVLA.

replace-cross Blackbox Performance Analysis of LLM-based Network Control

Authors: Mahdi Alizadeh, Pooria Namyar, Arvin Ghavidel, Ramesh Govindan

Abstract: LLMs are becoming an indispensable part of network control systems due to their impressive reasoning. Before such LLM-enabled systems can be deployed, it is imperative to develop tools that can audit their performance on previously unseen inputs. However, auditing these systems is challenging due to the inherent opacity of LLMs. LENS is the first step towards black-box performance analysis of LLM-enabled network control systems. It uses novel methods to extend deep reinforcement learning to guide the search for performance gaps and inputs that make LLMs struggle. LENS finds large gaps for LLM-based traffic engineering and GPU allocation systems, and even matches existing white-box analyzers designed for hand-crafted TE heuristics.

replace-cross Selecting the Best Few with Human Evaluators: Adaptively Combining Ratings and Pairwise Comparisons

Authors: Motti Goldberger, Nils Rudi

Abstract: This paper considers a decision-maker tasked with identifying the $k$ best items from a large pool of candidates, such as job applicants, LLMs, award nominees, or new product ideas. The decision-maker learns about the items' quality by soliciting human evaluations. Each evaluation can be costly, so the decision-maker aims to use the limited evaluation budget efficiently by allocating evaluations adaptively. A key consideration is the form of evaluation to solicit, which should be something evaluators are comfortable providing. Two popular forms are ratings and pairwise comparisons, and we allow for both, as they may be especially useful in combination given the limited budget. Ratings can be used early on to get a coarse picture of which items are good and which are not. As that picture forms, comparisons can refine the ordering of the good items, narrowing in on the ultimate top-$k$ selection. To solve the allocation problem, we develop an adaptive algorithm built from two allocation policies. The first, our primary contribution, is based on a surrogate objective that is naturally forward-looking, valuing evaluations that help inform what to evaluate in future rounds, not just those that directly improve the selection decision. The decision rule is interpretable, and we prove that it is asymptotically consistent. Late in the evaluation process, when being forward-looking is less productive, we construct a myopic policy and a criterion for switching to it. In simulation, the method gives insight into how to combine these two types of evaluations, and beats the (limited) existing benchmarks.

replace-cross MemT-1.0: Towards Memory-Native Evolution

Authors: Zhiyu Li, Zeyu Zhang, Linyun Liu, Yiwei Bu, Chenyang Xi, Xichong Zhang, Yi Yu, Yu Yu, Zhichen Liu, Yuxiao Yi, Chunyu Li, Haoran Liu, Zhenkai Ding, Le Zhou, Juncheng Zhang, Boyu Chen, Zehao Lin, Guorui Li, Renyu Fu, Ji Li, Youyang Xia, Jiajun Shen, Harvey Li, Jingsong Zhou, Helan Hu, Tong Shen, Tianxiao Xu, Chenye Han, Weijie Li, Zhongquan Zhou, Hongming Xu, Yuekeng Li, Erenkai Zhu, Peng Yu, Wenhao Li, Jingyi Kang, Xixuan Hao, Xuzhou Zhu, Can Chen, Jiawei Liu, Chen Jiang, Tao Jiang, Fangwei Xiang, Zepeng Yao, Kai Chen, Guang Yang, Mingfei Lu, Chunhui Li, Zhengxuan Jiang, Bo Tang, Shiyu Zhan, Yihang Sun, Jie Chu, Wenjun Xiong, Zhenxiang Hua, Chen Lin, Detong Su, Dongyu Deng, Qiuping Ding, Guofeng Ding, Si Chen, Guohua Wen, Xu Chen, Zhi-Qin John Xu, Ying-Cong Chen, Ningyu Zhang, Haofen Wang, Muning Wen, Haotian Chen, Wentao Zhang, Xuanhe Zhou, Rita Zhang, Junchi Yan, Feiyu Xiong

Abstract: Memory-native evolution requires making memory an intrinsic capability of foundation models, enabling them to continuously accumulate and utilize information. To this end, we introduce MemT-1.0, a new architecture of memory foundation models that internalize memory as part of model computation. Building on Metis, MemT-1.0 employs Gated DeltaNet-2 with a stable token-level memory update mechanism within each turn. We further introduce Mixture-of-Storage to scale native memory capacity. Following a deliberate backbone preparation, we develop a two-stage training pipeline consisting of continued pre-training and post-training, supported by 34B tokens of training data and efficient training and inference infrastructure. We release a model family comprising MemT-1.0-Nano and MemT-1.0-Flash, and conduct a comprehensive evaluation of memory and general capabilities. The results show that they outperform the parametric memory baselines on average and remain competitive with retrieval-augmented methods, while largely preserving general capabilities. At a history length of 128K tokens, MemT-1.0-Nano achieves approximately $1.86\times$ end-to-end inference speedup and a $66\times$ reduction in persistent storage relative to full context KV cache. By internalizing retrieved procedural experience, MemT-1.0 improves task success on agentic tasks, providing a practical step toward memory-native evolution.
